# Psalms 1-36

# A PREFACE

## CONCERNING THE ARGUMENT, USE, AND DIVISION, OF THE BOOK OF PSALMS

This book, (so cited by Christ himself, Luke 20.42. and by Peter, Acts 1.20. which as it confirms the antiquity of this inscription so to prevent scrupulousness in titles and quotations, Luke 24.44. we find it, *in the Psalms*, barely; not, *the book of Psalms*, as before:) has of ancient times, both by Jews and Christians, been subdivided into five books; the first, ending at the 41. Psalm: the second at the 72. The third, at the 89. The fourth, at the 106. The fifth comprehending the rest unto the end, the reason of this subdivision is by most given, the concluding words in those last Psalms: *Amen, amen*, in the three first books: a single *amen* in the fourth: and an *hallelujah* at the last. Other reasons are given too, but not any very probable or satisfactory. See more upon Psalm 41.72. last words. However, this division being ancient, should not be unknown to them that read ancient books. Other divisions according to the order of reading or singing, called (καθίσματα) *cathismata*, &c. which according to different Churches and rites, have been various, we here omit. Of the division of the book into 150. Psalms, see upon Psalm first.

The book of Psalms: according to the original, properly, *of praises*: so styled from the greater part, which contain the praises of God, in several respects, and upon several occasions. Penitential Psalms also, which contain confessions of sins, may truly, according to the Scripture phrase, be called praises, because when we freely and ingenuously confess our sins, we are said to glorify God, Josh. 7.19. Apoc. 16.9. &c. *The book of prayers*, Psal. 72. last verse. See there: and a little after (here) of the use of these Psalms. Psalms that contain praises, are properly called hymns. And again, Psalms are such hymns properly, as are fitted for some musical instrument. However, in common use, what Psalms are found in other books of Scripture besides this, we commonly call hymns, and so distinguish them from those herein comprehended.

The author of this book (the immediate and secondary we mean, besides the original and general of all true Scripture, the Holy Ghost: see of the Psalms particularly, Mar. 12.36. Act. 1.16. & 4.25.) though named in some other places of Scripture, David; as Luke 20.42. and elsewhere; is not there in the title of the book expressed. The truth is, they are not all David’s Psalms, some having been made before, and some long after him; as shall be showed in due place. Neither is that to be trusted to, as a general rule, that those only are David’s, that bear his name in the title or beginning, since (besides other reasons) we find some in other places of Scripture directly ascribed to David, which have not his name prefixed; as Psal. 2. Act. 4.25. However, to make good this title and inscription, it is enough that the greater part were made by David, and others by him (as is generally believed) collected into one, and appointed or fitted for public use.

Of the excellency of this book, by a divine hand, nay by the immediate hand of God himself, as we may say, so wonderfully contrived and fitted for all men’s occasions, whether in adversity, or prosperity of what degree or condition soever they be (a thing so known, and acknowledged by all, that have made any trial) we shall not need to speak: But never was greater injury done to any book of Scripture, than has been done to this, by one Bonaventure, who substituting the word *Mary*, or *Lady*, instead of *Lord*, or *God*, in most places, with some other alterations, (in some more, in some less) has made them all applicable, or rather has actually applied and transferred them to the Virgin Mary. As for example, he makes the first Psalm to begin, *Blessed is the man that loves your name, O Virgin Mary; your grace shall comfort him.* The seventh, *O glorious Lady, I have put my trust in you: I beseech you deliver my soul from the hands of mine enemies.* Cassander, a moderate Papist, speaks of it with much indignation; *Quid, quod totum Psalterium sublato ubique Domini nomine, in nomen Dominae commutatum legitur?* Nevertheless the book is in credit among the vulgar; and that it might be of more vulgar use, is translated into vulgar tongues. What has been here cited, is taken out of the French translation, printed at Paris, 1607. The original Latin is extant in Kemnitius his *Examen*.

The use of the Psalms (common and practical) is twofold: Either for a daily exercise of devotion, and divine worship whether private or public; (therefore also called prayers, as before noted:) or for particular occasions and occurrences, whether private or public, according to the subject and argument of every Psalm.

For the first; that the Psalms (not to speak of holy songs and hymns, long before David used upon extraordinary occasions, as we find recorded in the Scriptures;) as they were partly composed, and partly collected and put together by David and others; so were ever since David’s time used as part of God’s public service among the Jews, is not questioned by any. And that this use of them did continue among Christians since the first plantation of Christianity in the world, may appear by the most ancient records and monuments of those times, that remain unto this day. As for private devotion, though we will not condemn the use of other late books and helps, knowing what variety there is of spiritual, as well as sensual palates and appetites: yet we must be allowed, both for profit and delight, to give the preeminency to this divine book, far above and beyond whatsoever, that has been since, or shall ever be invented and devised by human wit. And as we give the preeminence to the Psalms; so after the Psalms to those books of private devotions, above all others, that are grounded upon the Psalms especially, and go along with them, or take them along: as I have seen some. Now for this use, whether public or private, the reading of the Psalms in order, as commonly digested, by certain portions, as it is most ordinary; so, except there be extraordinary occasion, it is most profitable.

The second use, we said, was upon special occasions and occurrences, such as this life does afford many, both public and private, according to the particular subject and argument of every Psalm. Now because such occasions, as we said but now, are very frequent, and that all men are not so well versed in the Psalms; or, though well versed, have not that judgment, as to discern of themselves what Psalm is fit and proper for every particular occasion, wherein nevertheless the chief use of the Psalms does mainly consist: many divisions have been by divers, both ancient and late, and divers tables set out to this purpose, representing the Psalms digested according to their several subjects and arguments. The most ancient table now extant in that kind is that of Athanasius, an ancient Greek Father; and though divers have been set out by others since him, yet I know not any that have been more copious and accurate in that kind than he, as may appear to them that shall peruse him. But of all those tables in general, it must be considered; First, that difference of translations, as it must needs breed diversity of expositions, so some diversity of applications too, to particular occasions. Neither is this diversity observable in ancient translations only, compared with modern: but even in, and among later translations also. So, for example, in some tables of that kind, that have been set out by some learned men, we are sent to the 41. Psalm, to learn that we should not judge rashly and uncharitably of the just and righteous, when he falls into troubles and adversities. There indeed, according to the translation and exposition which we embrace in these notes, we shall find a notable lesson to that purpose, as may be seen there: but according to some other both translations and expositions (*Blessed is he that considereth the poor*, &c. which seems to import relief and succor only:) little or nothing to that purpose. Secondly, it must be considered, that the argument of many Psalms is mixed and various, so that the same Psalm may be reduced to several titles, according to its several parts; as petitory, deprecatory, imprecatory, eucharistical, & the like: So that it is no wonder if all tables do not agree in their indications. For these reasons, and because that according to variety of tempers, opinions, fancies, and capacities, some men are more taken with some than others, it were better for every man that is able, with diligent reading and digesting of the said Psalms, there being store to fit all, to make such a table for his own particular use, to which he may have recourse upon occasion, and not be deceived of his expectation, as haply he may sometimes, if he trust to common tables. However, for the help and direction of them that are not so able of themselves, we have here set down some few generals of most common use, to which divers Psalms may be referred. They that desire a larger direction may have recourse to Athanasius, in his Epistle *ad Marcellinum*, περὶ τῆς ἑρμηνείας, &c. where they shall find a double table, very copious & accurate both, and both tending to one effect. The first begins, Οἱ μὲν ἐν διηγήμασι λεγόμενοι, &c. The second, Ἂν τοίνυν μακαρίσαι τινὰ θέλῃς, &c. The same have been translated not into Latin only, & often so printed, but in English also, if I be not mistaken.

As for imprecatory Psalms, which are many, how to be read, and applied, see upon Psal. 35.4. *Let them be confounded*, &c. and upon Psal. 143.3. *For the enemy hath persecuted my soul*, &c.

The seven, so commonly called penitential Psalms: The 6. *O Lord rebuke me not*, &c. 32. *Blessed is he*, &c. 38. *O Lord rebuke me not*, &c. 51. *Have mercy upon me O God*, &c. 102. *Hear my prayer O Lord*, &c. 130. *Out of the depths*, &c. 143. *Hear my prayer, O Lord*, &c.

To these, may be added, for remission of sins, increase of grace, and deliverance from sickness, and other pressing calamities; the 25. *Unto thee, O Lord*, &c. 39. *I said I will take heed*, &c. 88. *O Lord God of my salvation*, &c. 90. *Lord thou hast been our dwelling place*, &c. and divers others; as particularly, the 22. *My God, my God*, &c. though principally intended of Christ.

For the Church, in extremity of misery and desolation: the 44. *We have heard with our ears*, &c. 74. *O God, why hast thou cast us off*, &c. 79. *O God the Heathen*, &c. 80. *Give ear, O shepherd of Israel*, &c. 83. *Keep not thou silence*, &c. 89. *I will sing of the mercies of the Lord*, &c. 102. *Hear my prayer, O Lord*, &c. 137. *By the rivers of Babylon*, &c.

Of zeal for God’s house, and public assemblies, &c. the 26. *Judge me O Lord*, &c. 27. *The Lord is my light*, &c. 42. *As the Hart panteth*, &c. 43. *Judge me, O God*, &c. 65. *Praise waiteth*, &c. 84. *How amiable*, &c. to which some of the former, Of the Church, &c. as 102. *Hear my prayer, O Lord*, &c. may be added.

For the supreme, the 20. *The Lord hear thee*, &c. 21. *The King shall joy*, &c. 61. *Hear my cry, O God*, &c. 63. *O God, thou art my God*, &c. and in time of great distress and adversity, 89. *I will sing of the mercies of the Lord*, &c.

To praise God for his goodness, and his wonderful works in general, with reference to man in most particulars: the 8. *O Lord our God*, &c. 19. *The Heavens declare*, &c. 29. *Give unto the Lord*, &c. 65. *Praise waiteth for thee*, &c. 104. *Bless the Lord, O my soul*, &c. 107. *O give thanks unto the Lord*, &c. 111. *Praise ye the Lord*, &c. 136. *O give thanks unto the Lord*, &c. 139. *O Lord thou hast searched me*, &c. 145. *I will extol thee my God*, &c. 146. *Praise ye the Lord*, &c. 147. *Praise ye the Lord, for it is good*, &c.

Concerning Christ, these especially: the 2. *Why do the Heathen rage*, &c. 22. *My God, my God*, &c. 40. *I waited patiently*, &c. 45. *My heart is inditing*, &c. 72. *Give the King thy judgments*, &c. 110. *The Lord said unto my Lord*, &c.

To praise God for deliverances; as from sickness, or any outward evil and calamity; as also for remission of sins, and spiritual afflictions: the 30. *I will extol thee, O Lord*, &c. 31. *In thee O Lord, do I put my trust*, &c. 32. *Blessed is he whose transgression*, &c. 34. *I will bless the Lord at all times*, &c. 103. *Bless the Lord, O my soul*, &c. 116. *I love the Lord*, &c. 118. *O give thanks unto the Lord*, &c.

Against offence, because of the prosperity of the wicked, the 37. *Fret not thyself*, &c. 49. *Hear this all ye people*, (where see the annotations) &c. 73. *Truly God is good to Israel*, &c. 92. *It is a good thing*, &c.

For more particular applications, we must refer the reader to the annotations themselves, where the argument of several Psalms, where any doubt or obscurity did appear, is showed.

# PSALM I

*Psalm first.*] That this distribution or division of Psalms into first, second, &c. is ancient, nay authentic, may appear by Act. 13.33. *as it is also written in the second Psalm*: though some question be made by the most learned (whereof see in Beza upon the place) of the right reading of that place. However, that the whole book is, and has been of old divided into 150. Psalms, the learned, both Jews and others, do agree; but of the division of every particular Psalm, to make up this number, there is not like agreement. The first and second Psalms are by most ancients reckoned but for one, to wit, the first: who also in that place of the Act. 13.33. had it in their copies, *in the first*; not as it is now, *in the second psalm*. Some of the learned Rabbins also would have it so, and give some reasons why these two (as we reckon them) should be but one psalm. Again, the ninth psalm with us, in the Greek and vulgar Latin make two, the ninth and the tenth: so that from that place, to Psalm 147. (where we agree again) their account exceeds ours constantly by one. Other differences there be in some other Psalms of less moment. As therefore we hold it safest and surest to follow our ordinary division; so we need not press it upon others too rigidly, or to build upon it as canonical, since so much can be said on the other side. See more upon Psalm 96. at the beginning.

This first Psalm, according to our division, is by divers of the ancients rather taken as a preface or introduction to the whole book, than as one barely; to wit, the first of the number: by which (whether Psalm, or preface) the happiness of man, what it is, and wherein it consists, is declared. Indeed it has always been accounted the part of a prudent wise man, before he betakes himself to any serious work, to consider first of the general end, and what reference it has unto it, that accordingly he may bestow his pains more or less, as he sees it more nearly and immediately, or otherwise, to tend unto the end. Now the main and general end of all men, is happiness, or blessedness. And therefore the wisest and most renowned of all ancient philosophers, his two best pieces, (both for use and accurateness) his *Ethics* and his *Politics*, he begins with this very consideration of the end, laying this for a foundation, That all men desire to be happy. Now that is it that we are here taught by this first Psalm, what is true (though not according to ordinary judgments perchance) and real happiness, and what is not: a way to this happiness being also showed and proposed to them that shall make a right use of this book. See more upon Psal. 10. v. 4. *will not seek after God*.

**Verse 1. ***{Blessed}* The original is plural; which nevertheless, according to the propriety of the word, may be joined (& so rendered) with either plural or singular. But whether a substantive, or adjective, is controverted. Most take it for a substantive, as if it were *blessednesses*: whereupon some infer the multiplicity; others, the perfection of that happiness here intended. But this being the common use of the word, there can be no great solidity in this conceit.

*{the man}* האיש Some observe the emphasis of the article, as set of purpose, in opposition to common human judgment: as if he said, Blessed is, not whom the world does ordinarily bless, and count happy; but truly blessed he, &c. The word may also (as often) be rendered, *quilibet*, whoever; Blessed is he, whatever he be, (whatever others think and judge) who, &c.

*{walketh not in the counsel, &c.}* Most observe a gradation in these several words; 1. To walk, to stand, to sit: 2. Counsel, way, seat: 3. Ungodly, sinners, scornful. The beginning of godliness,1 say they, is to company with the ungodly, to keep correspondency, and to communicate counsels with them. For it cannot be, but their example will infect in some measure, and cast us into some inconveniences, though against our wills and purposes. But to stand, argues some purpose and resolution: as, to sit, the extremity of wickedness, when we make a practice of it without any remorse, or reluctancy. So, counsel, we may take of intention; way, of action; but seat, of profession; wherein is the height of wickedness, when that we have done wickedly, we dare boast of; and are ready, yea desirous (as masters of the chair) to teach others. So far the most, and most sober; as Calvin, and some others: but at the third and last gradation some stick, because they find no gradation in רשעים (here translated, *ungodly:*) and חטאים (*sinners;*) though they acknowledge themselves well satisfied about the last word, לצים (*scorners)* that by it, professed atheists, that make a mock of godliness, and deride the Scriptures, (the very worst of sinners) are understood. But since all the rest agree so well, a probable gradation between these two words also that are stuck at, may without much difficulty be found and showed, as it is here by some others. Somewhat to this purpose, see upon Prov. 12.12. *The wicked desireth the net of evil men*, &c. And these gradations admit of several interpretations, as may be seen in variety of interpreters: the most literal and probable, have been set down. Nevertheless, if any like better of their opinion, who think all these words promiscuously to be heaped and put together, the more emphatically and fully to express the care and duty of a godly man, in avoiding the company, as much as he can; but especially, the example of wicked and profane men; all wickedness, for the most part, proceeding from bad examples, and imitation: this, also without any alteration of the main scope, may be allowed. See more upon v. 4. *The ungodly are not so*; and Psal. 95.6. *Let us worship*. And of the danger of evil company in general, upon Psal. 26.4. *I have not sat*, &c.

**V. 2. ***{in the law of the Lord}* To the wicked and ungodly, he does oppose, not the godly and religious simply; but those, whose godliness is grounded upon the law, or Word of God. Many are very religious (or rather superstitious) in their own way, or led by others whom they have addicted themselves unto: but those only truly religious, whose religion is well grounded. See 2 Tim. 3.15,16,17.

*{meditate day and night}* Many do receive the Word of God in general, but take no thought or care to understand it. (*they have a zeal of God, but not according to knowledge*, Rom. 10.2.) Others, receive it with joy, but their joy continues not: as in the parable of the seed, Mat. 13. To meditate here, is to take care that we may understand: day and night, imports constancy and perseverance. By meditating also may be understood an endeavor to digest; that is, to reduce into practice; without which bare knowledge profits not. Now, this description of blessedness in these two verses, how it does agree with that, Psal. 32.1,2. and St. Paul’s inference or observation upon it, Rom. 4.6,7,8. see more upon that Psalm.

**V. 3. ***{And he shall be like a tree}* The happiness and prosperity of the godly is here set forth by a similitude: which although according to the measure of knowledge in those days, and the promises of the law, (subject nevertheless to many limitations, as we shall have occasion to show afterwards,) may admit of a literal interpretation also; that is, may be understood of temporal prosperity, according to that of the Apostle, *That godliness is profitable unto all things, having the promises both of this life and of that which is to come*, 1 Tim. 4.8. Yet is not this the happiness or prosperity principally here intended; but that which is permanent and eternal. To which also, not reason only, but the very words, if well pondered and examined, do lead us. For what is the greatest prosperity that this world does afford, or the longest life of man, or what account to be made of it, but, according to the Scripture, as of *grass, and the flower of the field*, Esa. 40.6. which *in the morning flourisheth and groweth up; in the evening it is cut down and withereth*, Psal. 90.6. see also Psal. 103.15,16. And what account David himself made of it, appears Psal. 39.5,6. & Psal. 62.9. which even they, who because they had no certain knowledge of any better after this, had most reason to make the most of this present world, and the goods of it, have freely acknowledged and expressed in many pithy sentences and speeches, comparing all human felicity to a shadow, to a dream, to a bubble, &c. So noted and incurrent into the eyes of all wise men has this truth ever been. This sense therefore, as principally intended, and most proper to the place, we shall henceforth insist upon, in the following words.

*{a tree}* See Jer. 17.8. & Ezek. 47.12. where it is apparent, that the fruit and waters spoken of, cannot be understood of others, but spiritual and celestial. Some very learned interpreters would have the tree here spoken of, particularly to be the palm tree, named Psal. 92.12. *The righteous shall flourish like a palm tree*; because divers things are written of the palm tree, which seem to agree well to this description; as that it is always green, bears always, &c. So they gather from Pliny. But I doubt they will scarce make that good; that it grows and thrives by rivers. The author of the *Geoponics* (as I remember) says it grows in none but dry salt grounds: I know not how truly Pliny says in light and sandy, which must often be watered. But whereas they quote Pliny for the perpetuity of the fruit he speaks it not of the tree, but of the *frutex*, which he distinguishes from the tree; *non in arborem sed in fruticem*; the manner of growing whereof is also different from the growing of the tree. It is not necessary therefore that we should understand it of any certain tree really extant in the world, no more than in the passage of Ezekiel, before quoted. Such a tree may be conceived, and from things merely conceivable, similitudes are often taken.

*{planted}* Not growing of itself. *Every plant which my Heavenly father hath not planted, shall be rooted up*, Mat. 15.13.

*{rivers of water}* Those waters that issue out of the Sanctuary, Ezek. 47.12. of which Christ speaks, Joh. 4.14. *the water that I shall give him, shall be in him a well of water, springing into everlasting life*.

*{his fruit in due season}* *Every branch in me that beareth not fruit he taketh away*, Joh. 15.2.

*{shall prosper}* *All things work together for good to them that love God*, Rom. 28. and *in all these*, (tribulations, distress, persecution, &c.) *we are more than conquerors*, ver. 37.

**V. 4. ***{The ungodly are not so}* Here by the word ungodly, must be understood the sinners and scorners also, mentioned in the first verse: whence some would infer, that there also by those three different words, the same must be understood, because all are comprehended here under one. But that is not necessary: for it is ordinary enough, as to other authors, so to the Scriptures, to use one and the same word, (sometimes in one and the same verse,) in different senses. So the Hebrew word לחם Psal. 104.14. is taken, and so translated, for *food*; a general word: and in the very next verse, translated *bread*; as *bread* is taken in its proper sense. So the word νόμος, law, Rom. 8.1,2. &c. is taken in different senses: sometimes more generally, and sometimes more particularly. However, I am not against it, (as was said there,) that the same may by those three several words in the first verse, (if any like it better so) should be understood. See also upon Psal. 26.4. *I have not sat with vain persons*.

*{like the chaff}* They have no hope after this life, which for the transitoriness and instability of it, may well be resembled unto chaff, (as unto a shadow, Eccl. 8.13. a hand breadth, and nothing, Psal. 39.15.) which is driven by the wind. *The world passeth away, and the lust thereof: but he that doth the will of God abideth for ever*, 1 Joh. 2.17. *The wicked is driven away in his wickedness; but the righteous hath hope in his death*, Prov. 14.32.

**V. 5. ***{not stand}* They shall not be acquitted: sentence shall be pronounced against them. *Watch ye therefore, and pray always, that ye may be accounted worthy to escape all those things that shall come to pass, and to stand before the Son of man*, Luke 21.36. See also, Ephes. 6.13.

*{in judgment}* *For God shall bring every work into judgment, with every secret thing*, Eccl. 12. last words.

*{sinners in the congregation of the righteous}* *There shall be weeping and gnashing of teeth, when ye shall see Abraham and Isaac, and Jacob, and all the Prophets in the Kingdom of God, and you yourselves thrust out*, Luke 13.28.

**V. 6. ***{knoweth the way of the righteous}* *Preserveth the way of his Saints*, Prov. 2.8. *He knoweth the way that I take; when he hath tried me, I shall come forth as gold*, Job 23.10.

*{the way of the ungodly shall perish}* *There is a way which seemeth right unto a man; but the end thereof are the ways of death*, Prov. 14.12. and, *when thou awakest*, (see there) *thou shalt despise their image*, Psal. 73.20.

# PSALM II

**Verse 1. ***{Why do the Heathen}* Throughout the Psalm, David is set out as a type of Christ. Whatsoever is here spoken of David, though it was fulfilled in him more immediately, yet by the principal aim and institution of the Holy Ghost, it belongs to Christ (as shall appear) more essentially and properly. The Apostles therefore apply these words to Christ immediately, Act. 4.25,26. As also verse 7. of this Psalm: see there.

*{rage}* Or, *tumultuously assemble*.

*{imagine}* Heb. *meditate*.

*{a vain thing}* That is not grounded on reason, or can produce any good effect.

**V. 2. ***{The Kings of the earth}* It was prophesied of Christ’s kingdom by Esay 49.33. *And Kings shall be thy nursing fathers, and their Queens thy nursing mothers: they shall bow down to thee with their face towards the earth, and lick up the dust of thy feet, and thou shalt know that I am the Lord.* This was fulfilled in the miraculous conversion of so many Gentile Kings and emperors to the Christian faith, whereof we read in Ecclesiastical history. However, this happened not presently but after many bloody persecutions; many plots and projects contrived with all possible human subtlety, for the destruction of Christians. As for David the type, what oppositions and difficulties he met with, both before and since he was come to his Crown, the history of Samuel does show. But nothing there that can make the application of these words so proper to him, as to Christ.

**V. 3. ***{Let us break their bands asunder}* By bands and cords, total subjection and strict obedience, such as theirs must be, that are truly converted unto Christ, must be understood. Much like unto this is that expression, Ezek. 38.4. *I will put hooks into thy jaws.* Many Kings and kingdoms, since their first conversion and submission to the Gospel of Christ, have since apostatized, and shaken off the yoke; and more (according to the predictions of Christ and his Apostles) may yet: but shall (all kingdoms and nations) in due time return to their obedience, notwithstanding all opposition, and small appearance of it to human judgments.

**V. 4. ***{that sitteth in the heavens}* God, emphatically, in opposition to Kings of the earth, before, verse 2.

*{shall laugh}* Prov. 2.16. Psal. 37.13. & 59.8.

**V. 5. ***{Then shall he speak unto them in his wrath}* God’s plagues will declare, that in resisting David and Christ, men fought against God.

*{in his wrath}* See upon Psal. 90.11. *Even according to thy fear, so is thy wrath*.

*{vex}* Or, *trouble*.

**V. 6. ***{Yet have I set my King}* Or, *Even I*. It is in vain for you to seek to pull him down, whom I have set up.

*{set}* Heb. *anointed*.

*{upon my holy hill of Zion}* Heb. *upon Zion, the hill of my holiness*, Psal. 15.1.

*{my holy hill of Zion}* Spoken of with so much respect and love, by God himself, Psal. 78. & 132. a type of the Church; to which belongs that promise of Christ, Matth. 16.18. *that the gates of hell shall not prevail against it*. See more upon Psal. 68.16. *The Lord will dwell in it for ever*.

**V. 7. ***{I will declare the decree}* In the Hebrew אל־חק, which word has troubled interpreters, and is differently translated, whereof variety of interpretations of the whole passage have issued. But I hope we shall so clear it, as no further doubt shall need to be made. This word then חק in the Scripture, besides some other uses is many times used to express that law and order, which God has set to natural things; those particularly, that are generally accounted most immutable: as to the Sea, that it shall not overflow the earth, Jer. 5.25. to sun and moon, that they shall give their light, Jer. 31.35,36. to the vicissitude of night and day, Jer. 33.25. In all which places (as elsewhere also, Psal. 148.6. &c.) the word חק is used; translated sometimes decree; and sometimes ordinance. And this to be the force and scope of the word in this place also, may further appear by Jer. 33. v. 20,21. and v. 25,26. where the Prophet speaking of this very thing, the promises of God unto David, he does not only use the word חק (or another equivalent ברית, translated covenant) but also sets out the immutability of these promises, that decree, by likening it to those immutable ordinances of night and day, &c. *Thus saith the Lord, if you can break my covenant of the day, and my covenant of the night, and that there should not be day and night in their season: then may also my covenant be broken with David my servant*, &c. and in the 25. verse, *—and if I have not appointed the ordinances of heaven and earth*, &c. By all which may clearly appear, that חק here does imply an immutable decree, in effect as irrevocable (so much to be collected out of the propriety of the word) as those things are, that are most irrevocable in the course of nature.

*{thou art my Son}* Of David, the type, as Psal. 89.26,27. *He shall cry unto me, Thou art my father*, &c. But of Christ, to whom it more properly belongs, and to whom it is applied, Act. 13.33. Heb. 1.5. & 5.5. Whether it is spoken of his divine nature, as he is the Son of God from all eternity; or as God and man, either conceived at such a time, as some; or publicly declared by a voice from heaven to be the Son of God, at his baptism; as others: Or lastly, constituted King after his resurrection; is not agreed among interpreters. Those that understand it of his divine and eternal generation, observe the propriety of the word, *this day*, which they say, notes the perpetuity of the act without any variation, or vicissitude of time. But not to spend time needlessly in refuting of others, it is certain from what has been observed before of the propriety of the word חק, that this generation (whatever it be otherwise) must be a thing promised, and (when this was written) of future and expected accomplishment; not therefore that eternal generation of the Son of God: Nor *This day*, the immovable (as some express it here) point of eternity. We follow them therefore, who interpret this generation of the manifestation, and public demonstration of Christ his Deity, by his resurrection; upon which (as here also follows) presently followed his inauguration, or solemn admission, into the rule and government of the world, as God and man: whereof himself speaks, Matth. 28.18. *All power is given unto me in heaven and in earth*. And thus was the Psalmist interpreted by Saint Paul himself, Act. 13.32,33. *And we declare unto you glad tidings, how the promise which was made unto the fathers, God hath fulfilled the same unto us their children, in that he hath raised up Jesus again; as it is also written in the second Psalm: Thou art my Son: this day have I begotten thee.* This passage indeed is eluded by some, who by reason of the ambiguity of the original word ἀναστήσας, not so proper to the resurrection, but that it may be otherwise interpreted also (as for example, Act. 3.22.) would have this *raised up*, to be taken in a more general sense, as if it had been, *has constituted and appointed*: or, *has stirred up*. But as there was no need of this device, since that the word ἀναστῆναι is more usually taken in the New Testament of the resurrection: so if they had thought of another passage of the same Paul, which does not admit of this evasion, they had certainly forborne. And that is, Rom. 1.3,4. where the words are, *Concerning his Son Jesus Christ, our Lord, which was made of the seed of David according to the flesh; And declared to be the Son of God with power, according to the Spirit of holiness, by the resurrection from the dead.* If any stick at this expression, *I have begotten*, for, *I have declared, or manifested you to be begotten*: let them consider of those, *Immundabit & mundabit sacerdos*, Lev. 13. he shall (if we may so speak) unclean and clean. As it is in the Hebrew, often repeated in that Chapter; for *he shall pronounce unclean or clean*, as it is there rendered: and Jer. 1.10. *to root, and to pull down*, &c. for to prophesy of rooting, and pulling down, &c.

*{this day}* At a certain and appointed time; the better and more emphatically to set out the certainty of the promise. Or, *this day*, particularly to be understood of the day of Christ’s resurrection; as those words also, Psal. 118.24. *This is the day which the Lord hath made, we will rejoice and be glad in it*; according to some there, both old and latter.

**V. 8. ***{Ask of me}* The better to set out the love of God to his Son, according to that voice from heaven, Matth. 3.17. *This is my beloved Son, in whom I am well pleased*. It was a fashion in former days among great princes, when they would show their great affection, or extraordinary good liking, to bid the parties ask what they would, promising they should have their desire. Besides what examples common histories do afford, see Esther 5. & 7. & Matth. 14.6,7. To this custom is here alluded. Such allusions are frequent in the Scripture. Some take the words literally and simply, and refer to this, that prayer of Christ, recorded Joh. 17.5. *And now, O Father, glorify thou me with thine own self, with the glory which I had with thee before the world was*. It does not seem to me so probable.

*{and the uttermost parts of the earth for thy possession}* Not only the Jews, but the Gentiles also.

**V. 9. ***{Thou shalt break them with a rod of iron}* Revel. 2.27. & 19.15. The word in the original Hebrew, here translated *to break*, may also come from a root, which signifies *to feed*; as feeding does include all duties of a pastoral charge; to lead, to rule, &c. Whence it is, that the Septuagint here translate it ποιμανεῖς: whom the author of the Apocalypse does follow, 2.27. and 19.15.

*{like a potter’s vessel}* So as they can never be restored, Isa. 30.14. Dan. 2.44.

**V. 10. ***{Be wise now therefore, O ye Kings}* He exhorts all Rulers to repent in time, and leave conspiring against him, or Christ.

**V. 12. ***{Kiss the Son}* In token of homage, according to the custom of those days and places. So Gen. 41.40. *Thou shalt be over my house, and according unto thy word shall all my people be ruled*. It is in the original, ישק: *shall all my people kiss*: (*osculabitur*,) as some interpreters there. The Septuagint with the Chaldee Paraphrase, translate the words, *Take hold of instruction*: wherein they may be thought, as in divers other places, to have had more respect to the sense and matter of the words, as they did understand them; than to the words themselves. But I rather believe that they had a respect unto the words, as well as to the matter. For נשק Ezek. 3.13. signifies, *to touch*: as it is well rendered there; which is as far from the proper signification of the word, (*to kiss*:) as δράξασθαι, or *to take hold*, is from *touching*. Then בר without any alteration of the consonants, may signify *purity*, (a principal effect of good instruction, or discipline) as well as *filius*, a son. Besides, παιδεία, (instruction, or discipline:) in Greek, comes from παῖς, a son, because unto such properly discipline does belong. In this word therefore παιδεία (sonship, as it were:) they had a respect doubtless to the original word, which signifies a son. St. Jerome by kissing here, understands adoration, because it was the custom in old time, as appears by many ancients, and by Job 31.27. to kiss the hand, when they adored: as the very Latin word *adorare*, (according to the common opinion, and etymology) does import. His translation therefore of the words is: *Adorate purè*, or, *adorate filium*: of which he treats in divers places. Some of the Rabbins also, (as Aben Ezra for one) are for this translation; *adorate filium*: and acknowledge this whole Psalm to belong to the promised Messias.

*{and ye perish from the way}* *When the wicked shall say, Peace and rest*, seeming yet to be but in the midway of their purposes, *then shall destruction suddenly come*, 1 Thess. 5.3.

*{blessed are all they that put their trust in him}* Prov. 16.20. Isa. 30.18. Jer. 17.7. Rom. 9.33. and 10.11. 1 Pet. 2.6.

# PSALM III

## TITLE

*{When he fled from Absalom his son}* 2 Sam. 15.14.

**Verse 1. ***{How are they increased that trouble me?}* This was a token of his stable faith, that for all his troubles he had his recourse to God.

**V. 2. ***{Selah}* There be variety of opinions, whereof many have been collected of old by old Jerome, concerning the use and true signification of this word. Theirs to me (and they are the learnedest of these latter times) seems best grounded, who make it a bare musical note, whether for raising of the voice, repetition, or the like. The vulgar opinion is, that it points at some extraordinary thing, which requires elevation of mind, or more than ordinary attention. Indeed it is hard to say of any place of Scripture, where least words sometimes afford great mysteries, that it is not extraordinary, or needs not elevation of mind. But if a man should peruse all those places (some 70. in the Book of Psalms; and some two or three in the Prophet Habakkuk:) where this word is found, he would be put to it perchance, to give a reason, why such places as these, and set after *leasing*, Psal. 4.2. *and remain in the wilderness*, Psal. 55.7. *that would swallow me up*: and, *they are fallen themselves*, Psal. 57.3,6. *I proved thee at the waters of Meribah*, Psal. 81.7. *they have holpen the children of Lot*, 83.8. and the like, should require more peculiar elevation of mind, than a thousand other places of the same Psalms, apparently more mysterious, or sententious. That we be not therefore forced to devise mysteries, where there is no need, and to impose our own conceits upon the Scripture, I think safest to let it pass where we meet it, as either a musical note, as was said before; or unknown word, as many believe.

**V. 3. ***{for}* Or, *about*.

**V. 5. ***{I laid me down and slept: I awaked}* Though the words, of themselves, simply taken, afford a good sense, as Prov. 3.24. Job 11.18. yet that sense will be more full, if it be observed, that it was a proverbial speech among the Hebrews, to set out great confidence and security. As for example, Mark 4.27. (which not understood there, has troubled some interpreters;) *So is the Kingdom of God, as if a man should cast seed into the ground: and should sleep, and rise night and day, and the seed should spring and grow up, he knoweth not how.* *Sleep and rise night and day*; that is, trouble himself no more about it; take no further thought, but rest himself with all confidence and security.

**V. 6. ***{I will not be afraid of ten thousands of people}* Psal. 27.3. When he considered the truth of God’s promise, and tried the same, his faith increased marvelously.

**V. 7. ***{upon the cheek bone}* You have laid reproach upon them, Job 16.10. and weakened their power, Psal. 58.6.

*{thou hast broken the teeth of the ungodly}* You have taken away all means of hurting me from them.

**V. 8. ***{Salvation belongeth unto the Lord}* Isa. 43.11. Hos. 13.4. Be the dangers never so great, or many, yet God has ever means to deliver his; and he is the only author of safety, Psal. 60.11. Prov. 21.31.

# PSALM IV

## TITLE

*{To the chief musician}* Or, *overseer*. Among them that were appointed to sing the Psalms, and to play on the instruments, one was appointed chief, to set the tune, and to begin, who had the charge, because he was most excellent, 1 Chron. 15.21. & 23.4.

*{Neginoth}* That is, on hand-instruments, which were played on with the hand only, Isa. 38.20. Hab. 3.19. as harps and cymbals.

**Verse 1. ***{Hear}* Heb. *Answer*.

*{O my God of righteousness}* Or, *O my righteous God*, as Psal. 59.10,17. You that are both most righteous, and the defender of my righteous cause, Psal. 26.1. and 35.1.

*{when I was in distress}* Both in mind and body.

*{have mercy upon me}* Or, *be gracious unto me*.

*{my prayer}* Complaint made to you, as to my Judge; for so the word signifies, Psal. 17.1,2.

**V. 2. ***{my glory into shame}* Whether this Psalm were made by David when he was persecuted by Saul, or by his own son Absalom, is uncertain; but when under persecution, all agree: and that by my glory here, he understands his kingdom: as if he had said, How long will you thus continually plot and project, how you may either keep me out, or defeat and disappoint me of that honor and dignity which I have boasted of, and rejoiced in, as cast upon me by God’s immediate providence; and secured unto me by divine promises and revelations; to make me appear to the world as an usurper, or impostor?

*{love vanity, and seek after leasing}* All your endeavors against God and me, are but in vain: though for a while you prevail; yet in the end, the fruit of all your plots, and daily machinations, will be but wind, and a lie: a mere abortive of your own fancies, that shall never come to pass really.

**V. 3. ***{the Lord hath set apart}* Marvelously separated, by some sign of distinction and excellency, Exod. 8.22. & 9.4. & 11.7. & 33.16. Psal. 17.7.

*{that is godly}* A King that walks piously in his vocation; not like Saul.

**V. 4. ***{Stand in awe, and sin not}* Before, David spoke to his enemies; Now, (as some of the most learned here,) to his friends, that were extremely scandalized, and confounded in themselves, at his sufferings; and that God should suffer the wicked to prevail so much against his own cause. As if he said; Let not your indignation and just resentment of these confusions proceed to a wicked murmuring against God, or rash forsaking of his truth; but consider of it wisely, in your most retired sober thoughts. The word רגז translated here (*contremiscite*) stand in awe, does also signify, *commoveri irâ*, to be angry: and by the Septuagint, (who did often affect to keep to the propriety of words according to the etymology; the words ὀργὴ, et ὀργίζω, in all probability proceeding from the Hebrew רגז:) is here rendered ὀργίζεσθε, *be angry*. Whose translation is followed by St. Paul, Ephes. 4.26. *Be angry and sin not; let not the Sun go down upon your wrath.* Either of those significations, *stand in awe*, or *be angry*, may do well enough in this place; but this latter (preferred also by Mollerus,) will fit better.

**V. 5. ***{Offer the sacrifices of righteousness}* If you cannot (in this time of confusion, and being driven from Jerusalem and the Temple) offer legal sacrifices, and want the comfort of that solemn service; you may (in any place, and at all times) offer the sacrifice of a godly righteous life, and put your trust in the Lord, which is above all legal sacrifices. *The sacrifices of God is a broken spirit: a broken and a contrite heart, O God, thou wilt not despise*, Psal. 51.17. & 50.8, &c. *I will not reprove thee for thy sacrifices or thy burnt-offerings*, &c. *Offer unto God thanksgiving, and pay thy vows unto the most High. And call upon me in the day of trouble; I will deliver thee, and thou shalt glorify me.* See also upon Psal. 51.19. *Then shalt thou be pleased*.

**V. 6. ***{Who will shew us any good?}* The multitude seek worldly wealth, but David sets his felicity in God’s favor.

*{Who will shew us any good}* *We looked for peace, and there is no good: and for the time of healing, and behold trouble*, Jer. 14.19.

*{lift thou up the light of thy countenance upon us}* Num. 6.26. Make us feel the full effect of your favor, as the sun scatters his beams at noon.

*{the light of thy countenance}* *For they gat not the land in possession by their own sword, neither did their own arm save them; but thy right hand and thine arm, and the light of thy countenance, because thou hadst a favour unto them*, Psal. 44.3. See also Psal. 80.3,7,19. *Turn us again, O God*, &c.

**V. 7. ***{Thou hast put gladness, &c.}* He praises God, that in the midst of these temporal troubles and afflictions which God had laid upon him, so great, and so lasting, that many of his side were ready to sink; yet God had afforded unto his soul such spiritual joys and comforts, as he valued more than the greatest joys of this world; such as used to be among the generality of men in time of harvest and vintage. Whence it is, that the joy of harvest, was among the Hebrews proverbially used for a great extraordinary joy, Esa. 9.3. & 16.10.

**V. 8. ***{I will both lay me down in peace, and sleep}* Psal. 3.5.

*{for}* Or, *though*, as Gen. 8.21. Josh. 17.18.

*{thou Lord only makest me dwell in safety}* God alone gives him safety, Deut. 32.12. Or, God keeps him safe when he is alone, and helpless, that his foes may not hurt him, Num. 23.9. Psal. 3.8. & 40.17. 1 Sam. 30.6.

# PSALM V

## TITLE

*{Nehiloth}* Upon wind-instruments, that have hollowness in them to blow into.

**Verse 1. ***{Consider my meditation}* That is, my vehement prayer, and secret complaint and sighings, proceeding from meditation.

**V. 3. ***{My voice shalt thou hear in the morning}* See upon Psal. 30.5. *Weeping may endure*, &c. See also Psal. 130.6.

*{in the morning}* I will pray betimes, and will not cease till I be heard, Psal. 88.13. Hab. 2.1.

*{will I direct my prayer}* Heb. *set in order*; to wit, my petitions; or words, Job 32.14.

*{will look up}* Psal. 123.1,2. Heb. *look out*; to wit, like a watchman.

**V. 4. ***{that hath pleasure in wickedness}* Seeing that God by nature hates wickedness, he must needs punish the wicked, and save the godly.

*{neither shall evil dwell with thee}* Heb. *sojourn, or be harbored as a guest*, much less dwell constantly, Psal. 84.10.

*{evil}* Or, *the evil man*.

**V. 5. ***{The foolish shall not, &c.}* The Hebrew word הוללים does more properly signify *madmen*, as it is here translated by most interpreters; roarers, roysters, riotous persons. The same root (in another conjugation) signifies to laud and praise: and is often used of them, who were appointed solemnly to praise God in his Church. Now, in outward appearance, there is sometimes some affinity of carriage between those that are so mad, through excess of worldly mirth, and joviality, as they call it; and those that praise God, aloud, with intention of mind and voice. David therefore by this word here, excluding such from the presence and house of God, (mentioned in the 7. verse,) seems to allude to those solemn, or any other devout praisers of God, who had a right to his house and presence.

*{in thy sight}* Heb. *before thine eyes*.

**V. 6. ***{the bloody and deceitful man}* Psal. 55.23. Heb. *the man of bloods and deceit*.

*{and deceitful man}* Secret sinners; for bloody men are open, deceitful ones work privily.

**V. 7. ***{I will come into thy house}* In the deepest of his temptation, he puts his confidence in God.

*{in thy fear}* Or, *in the fear of thee*, as Esay 53.11.

*{toward thy holy Temple}* When they had not access to the Temple, as in time of persecution, exile, &c. yet were they, wherever they were, to turn themselves towards it, when they made their prayers, 1 King. 8.48. Dan. 6.10. But if this Psalm, according to the title, and common opinion, were David’s; these words can have no relation to that custom, at least, as grounded upon those places before quoted; because the Temple (properly so called) was not yet built: and by the Temple, must here be understood the Tabernacle. But if by Temple, we understand here the inmost and holiest part of the Tabernacle, into which none had access but the High Priest; then even they that were within the Tabernacle, might be said to pray *towards*, as it is here. See also, Psal. 28.2. *toward thy holy Oracle*. See also upon Psal. 138. v. 2. *towards thy holy Temple*.

**V. 8. ***{Lead me}* Leading, is for the directing of the journey, Psa. 25.5. & 143.8,10. Prov. 3.6. Making plain the way, is for the easier walking, Esay 40.3,4.

*{because of mine enemies}* Because you are just, therefore lead me out of danger, for many watch to hurt me.

*{mine enemies}* Heb. *those which observe me*.

*{straight}* Or, *even*, as Esay 26.7,10.

**V. 9. ***{no faithfulness in their mouth}* Nothing that one may trust to; no steadfastness.

*{faithfulness}* Or, *steadfastness*.

*{in their mouth}* Heb. *in his mouth*; that is, *in the mouth of any of them*.

*{very wickedness}* Heb. *wickednesses*.

*{their throat is an open sepulchre}* Rom. 3.13. Desires to swallow men like the grave, Prov. 27.20. & 30.16.

*{open sepulchre}* Jer. 5.16.

**V. 10. ***{Destroy thou them}* Or, *make them guilty*.

*{by their own counsels}* Or, *from their counsels*. Let their own devices overthrow them.

*{in}* Or, *for*.

*{transgressions}* Or, *defections*.

*{they have rebelled against thee}* Destroy them not for my sake, but for your sake.

**V. 11. ***{But let all those that put their trust in thee, rejoice}* Your favor towards me shall confirm the faith of others.

*{thou defendest them}* Heb. *thou coverest over, or, protectest them*.

**V. 12. ***{compass him}* Heb. *crown him*.

*{as with a shield}* So that he shall be safe from all dangers.

# PSALM VI

## TITLE

*{Neginoth}* Psal. 4. title.

*{upon Sheminith}* Or, *upon the eighth*. Upon an instrument with eight strings; or, *in a bass-tune*, 1 Chron. 25.21.

**Verse 1. ***{O Lord, rebuke me not in thine anger}* Psal. 38.1. Jer. 10.24. Though I deserve destruction, yet let your mercy pity my frailty.

**V. 2. ***{my bones are vexed}* For my whole strength is abated.

**V. 3. ***{but thou O Lord, how long?}* I have been long sick, but you defer to heal me.

**V. 4. ***{deliver my soul}* My life from death, which is nigh, verse 5.

**V. 5. ***{For in Death, &c.}* See Isa. 38.18,19. & Psal. 30.9. & 115.17,18. and elsewhere. All which places must not be understood as though it were intended by such expressions positively to determine anything concerning the estate of the dead, (a thing in those infant days of the Church not so fully revealed, nor ordinarily talked of) but only, the better to set out the duty of the living. Now God himself having revealed unto men, that the most acceptable service (far above the legal and ceremonial) they could do unto him, was to call upon him in time of trouble; and after deliverance, to glorify him: as is more fully expressed, Psal. 50. from verse 8. to 16. those holy men, being in danger of death through sickness or otherwise, could not pitch upon any other consideration, upon which they might press their recovery, or deliverance, with God, with more boldness or confidence, than this of God’s glory, so much interested (as by his own gracious declaration they were assured) in the thanks and praises of the living. This therefore they insist upon, and press it home; professing also at the same time, that it is the main end, and scope of their lives, to glorify God. See more upon Psalm 88.10. *Wilt thou show*, &c.

**V. 6. ***{all the night}* Or, *every night*.

**V. 7. ***{Mine eye is consumed because of grief}* It is eaten out with grief, as with a moth, because my foes insult over me.

**V. 8. ***{Depart from me all ye workers of iniquity}* Matth. 7.23. & 25.41. Luke 13.27. God sends comfort and boldness in affliction, that we may triumph over our enemies. See upon Psal. 22.22. *I will declare*.

**V. 10. ***{Let all mine enemies be ashamed}* When the wicked think the godly shall perish, God delivers them suddenly, and destroys their enemies: so they lose their hope, and are confounded, Job 6.20. Jer. 48.1,20.

# PSALM VII

## TITLE

*{Shiggaion}* The meaning of this word is very uncertain. Some would have it an instrument of music: Others, a certain tune; or beginning of song, or certain kind of verse. They that have written most of it, profess themselves most irresolute, and think it not very material, or necessary to be known whatever it be. Which may be said, and indeed is said by the most learned, of all these titles of Psalms (that have any obscurity) in general, as belonging all to that either music, or poetry, then in use; now, altogether unknown.

*{Cush the Benjamite}* Most interpreters by Cush, think Saul, who was the son of Kish, a Benjamite, 1 Sam. 9.1, &c. to be intended. This reason of changing the name, some think was in reverence to his sacred person and function, Job 34.18. Others, the better to set out his obstinate impenitency, and hardness of heart. For Cush properly signifies an Ethiopian, or Blackamoor: according to that of Jer. 13.23. *Can the Ethiopian change his skin? or the Leopard his spots? then may ye also do good, that are accustomed to do evil*. Certain it is, that such secret allusions, or disguises of proper names have been observed by the learned in divers places of Scripture. See Junius upon 2 King. 23.13. and others there. Nevertheless, their opinion that would have this Cush rather to have been some of Saul’s officers or courtiers, who had calumniated David, and incensed Saul against him, as though he had plotted secretly to take his life away; if any like better of it, the tenor of the Psalm will agree well enough with it. But then it will be better to translate this דברי here in the title, *the words*, as it is already in the text; than, *the business*, as in the margin.

**Verse 2. ***{Lest he tear my soul like a lion}* He desires God to deliver him from the rage of cruel Saul, his principal adversary.

*{none to deliver}* Heb. *not a deliverer*.

**V. 3. ***{if I have done this}* Wherewith Cush charges me; to wit, if I have sought to slay Saul, and seduce the people.

*{if there be iniquity in my hands}* That is, in me. But he names hands, because they are the instruments of murder.

**V. 4. ***{If I have rewarded evil unto him that was at peace with me}* If I reverenced not Saul as God’s anointed, and preserved his life, 1 Sam. 26.8,9.

**V. 5. ***{and lay mine honour in the dust}* Let me not only die, but be dishonored forever.

*{dust}* Job 16.15.

**V. 6. ***{lift up thyself}* Set yourself on your judgment-seat. Tribunals were high, 1 King. 10.19.

*{rage}* Or, *outrages*.

*{to the judgment that thou hast commanded}* David seems here to challenge that *jus regni* (as Vatablus here, and some others) which God had promised unto him. Now we read of a *jus regni*, 1 Sam. 8.9,10, &c. and Chap. 10.25. where indeed we find the very same word משפט, as is here; though there translated *manner*; here *judgment*. But of that *jus* there, how to be interpreted, there is much controversy; and it seems to have more particular reference unto Saul. The *jus regni* challenged here by David was his right and title to the Crown, by God’s promise, and Samuel’s anointing of him by God’s command, in greater confirmation. The words also may be taken more generally; That God would do him right, and execute justice, in his behalf; which God himself has commanded to be executed among men.

*{so shall the congregation of the people compass thee about}* Then shall all men flock unto you, and glorify you; then shall religion and piety be in credit among men, when they see your just judgments. For *the Lord is known* (among the generality of men especially) *by the judgment which he executeth*, Psal. 9.16. See also, Psal. 58.10,11. & 64.9,10.

*{for their sakes}* Not only for mine, but for your Church’s sake also, declare your power.

*{for their sakes therefore return thou on high}* As before, verse 6. *Lift up thyself*, &c. that is, Betake yourself to your throne, and high tribunal of justice, from whence by this prevailing of wickedness among men, you seem to have been absent of late. That this word *high*, has relation to administration of justice among the Hebrews, appears also by Eccl. 5.8. *If thou seest the oppression of the poor, and violent perverting of Judgment and Justice in a Province, marvel not at the matter; for he that is higher than the highest regardeth; and there be higher than they*: that is, Though men do not, whom God has trusted, yet there is a superior Judge, that in due time will do justice, and render unto every one according to his works. Let it not therefore seem strange unto you, if, among those inferior Judges, injustice and extortion do prevail for a while.

**V. 8. ***{me}* Or, *for me*; as Psal. 43.1.

*{according to my righteousness}* Psal. 18.20. As touching my behavior towards Saul, and mine enemies.

**V. 9. ***{Oh let the wickedness of the wicked come to an end}* Whereby they seek to undo me, and other good men.

*{for the righteous God trieth the hearts and reins}* 1 Sam. 16.7. 1 Chr. 28.9. Psal. 139.1. Jer. 11.20. & 17.10. & 20.12.

*{the righteous God trieth}* As gold is tried in the fire, Isa. 48.10. Psal. 66.12. Job 23.10.

*{the hearts and reins}* The thoughts and affections. God knows my innocence; and my accusers’ hypocrisy.

**V. 10. ***{my defence is of God}* Heb. *my Buckler is upon God, or with God*.

**V. 11. ***{God judgeth the righteous}* Or, *God is a righteous Judge, or, God judgeth for the righteous*.

*{God is angry}* See upon Psal. 90.11. *even according to thy fear*.

*{every day}* He does continually call the wicked to repentance by some judgment or other.

**V. 12. ***{If he turn not}* The wicked man, ver. 9. or, mine enemy, ver. 4,13.

*{he will whet his sword}* God will plague him.

*{whet his sword}* Deut. 32.41,42.

**V. 14. ***{Behold, he travaileth with iniquity, &c.}* Job 15.35. Isa. 59.4. Jam. 1.15.

**V. 15. ***{He made a pit and digged it}* Heb. *he hath digged a pit*.

*{and is fallen into the ditch which he made}* Psal. 9.15. & 10.2. Prov. 5.22. Esth. 7.10.

**V. 16. ***{his violent dealing shall come down upon his own pate}* From heaven shall he be punished by God for his cruelty, Rom. 1.18.

**V. 17. ***{according to his righteousness}* In keeping faithfully his promise with me, and rewarding mine enemy according to his violence against me.

# PSALM VIII

## TITLE

*{Gittith}* Some instrument that came from Gath, or, that was used by the sons of Obed-edom the Gittite, 2 Sam. 6.10. The word Gittern, or Guittern might come from thence. However because Gath signifies also a wine-press, it is not unlikely that this and other like Psalms that set out the goodness and providence of God towards man in this kind, were, in time of harvest and vintage, publicly sung.

**Verse 1. ***{Who hast set thy glory above the heavens}* Your throne, the sign of your glorious Majesty.

*{above the heavens}* Psal. 113.4. & 148.13.

**V. 2. ***{Out of the mouth of babes and sucklings}* The common opinion is, that David does here set out the wonderful providence of God toward infants, in their birth and sustentation; and particularly, in providing such convenient food for them, as milk, and conveying it to them so fitly, and fitting them by secret instincts and dispositions of nature for it, and the like: Which things, indeed, if well and rationally considered, are very wonderful, and afford matter of high contemplation to a naturalist; as may be seen by them that have written of those particulars, philosophers, and others. See upon Psal. 22.9. *thou took me out of the womb*. See also, Psal. 139.13,14, &c. & Psal. 22.9,10. Psal. 71.5,6. Nevertheless, this being no common subject for every ordinary man’s speculation or observation; nor of itself so far above and beyond the ordinary course of nature’s wonders in natural things; those especially, that belong unto the nature and structure of man in general, whether his body or soul be considered: That David would pitch upon this one particular of infants, as the most eminent and conspicuous of God’s wonderful works, to stop the mouths of blasphemous atheists, does not seem to be very probable. The heathens I am sure (whose testimony in this case is very considerable) in their writings upon this subject, unanimously acknowledge the sight and contemplation of the heavens, sun and moon, &c. so open and obvious unto every ordinary man, that has anything of common sense and reason in him, to be the thing, if not the only, yet the chief and principal, that has compelled the most obstinate and obdurate to an acknowledgment of a Deity. And so David himself, Psal. 19. *The Heavens declare the glory of God*, &c. and Psal. 89.5. *The heavens shall praise*; and 97.6. *The heavens declare*. That therefore which I shall propose to the further consideration of the learned, is, whether these words, babes and sucklings here, might not be taken proverbially, for things that have no voice, or cannot speak; it being so proper to, and observable in infants, that they do not speak, that from that very thing they have their names in the Latin tongue; *infans*, properly signifying one that cannot speak; and *infantia*, disability to speak. And is not this the very thing that David does in that other Psalm of the same subject as this, more plainly, in words; but with the same acuteness of sense, insist upon, That the heavens, though they have no speech, nor language, nor voice, (for all those words are there, ver. 3. as shall be showed there) yet they have power enough to speak and preach their Maker all the world over? Neither can it be any material objection against this interpretation, that Christ does apply these words to children, that were very children, not figuratively so, Mat. 21.16. For besides that there be examples, divers, of more remote applications in the New Testament: it is apparent, that the propriety of words may not be pressed in this particular, since that Christ there applies them to children (though such otherwise as might be called infants well enough; as the word even by profane authors is used sometimes) that actually did speak: whereas here, such as cannot speak, (wherein is the chiefest emphasis, or *dinosis*, as they call it) are properly intended. Yet was Christ his application of the words very proper and natural, according to the proper nature of such applications; which sometimes are but bare allusions, as it were; and yet such allusions, as out of all question, were even from the beginning so forecast and appointed, as it were by the Holy Ghost.

*{ordained}* Heb. *founded*.

*{the avenger}* Of all enemies, those that pursue after vengeance, use to be the fiercest and most diligent. Here it is taken for a desperate atheist; one that is very busy and active to oppugn God’s providence: that greedily catches at all opportunities to blaspheme. So also, Psal. 44.16. *For the voice of him that reproacheth*, &c.

**V. 3. ***{the heavens, &c.}* There be two faces or aspects of heaven, that are usual and ordinary; the one by day, the other by night. By day, that which is most conspicuous, is the sun: (for what else we see, is rather clouds, and air, than heaven) here therefore included in the word heaven. By night, the moon and the stars are seen, mentioned by themselves. This I take to be sufficient and satisfactory, so that we shall not need to mention multiplicity of reasons that have been devised by interpreters, why the sun, the most glorious of all, is here left out among the works of God, which I conceive to be but their mistake. If they had said, why not named, they had said something.

*{the work of thy fingers}* A curious divine work. A metaphor from them that make tapestry.

*{the moon and the stars}* These were David’s night-meditations, for he mentions not the sun. So some: but see before.

**V. 4. ***{What is man, &c.}* That is, you that are the Maker of such glorious creatures, so pure, so bright, so beautiful; how can you stoop so low, as to take care of man, so vile, so frail, so wretched, in comparison? Or, which is more; What was man unto you; or what in himself, that you should create and provide for his use, such glorious creatures? Not for his use only, to enjoy hereafter, when transferred thither after death; (which some would have chiefly intended) but for his use now living; for the contentment of his contemplation, and for the benefits which he receives from them: not light only, but the seasons of the year, and the fruits of the earth, which without the heavenly influences could produce nothing. And so much some of the heathen have acknowledged, and from thence well inferred both the goodness of God, and the worthiness of man, above other creatures. These, and the following words are interpreted of Christ, by the Apostle, Heb. 2.6, &c. and 1 Cor. 15.27. Eph. 1.22. From hence as it may well be inferred that Christ, indeed is the principal aim in the intention of the Holy Ghost, who dictated it unto David; so nothing hinders, but that David might have a more immediate and obvious aim, to set out, (as was said before) the goodness and providence of God to mankind. And though it is true, that man since his rebellion against Him, of whom he had received it, has lost much of that sovereignty upon the creatures insisted upon in the following verses; yet that it holds to this day in a great measure, is evidently true, and acknowledged by ancient heathens, Tully, Plutarch, and others; who from thence fetch excellent arguments for a providence.

*{What is man that thou art mindful of him? and the son of man, &c.}* Job 7.17. Psal. 144.3. Heb. 2.6.

*{that thou visitest him?}* That you mind him more than other creatures.

**V. 5. ***{Than the Angels}* In the Hebrew, אלהים; than the Gods properly; or, *than God*, βραχύ τι παρὰ θεὸν; as some ancient Greek versions had it. However, אלהים is often taken for Angels; as Psal. 97.7. *worship him all ye gods*; that is, Angels; and elsewhere. And so is the place expounded in the Epistle to the Hebrews.

**V. 6. ***{thou hast put all things under his feet}* 1 Cor. 15. verse 27.

**V. 7. ***{all sheep and oxen}* Heb. *flocks and oxen all of them*.

# PSALM IX

## TITLE

*{Upon Muthlabben}* Heb. על־מות לבן. It may be translated (not as it is now; but as it may be pointed) *Upon the death of Laben*: So the Chaldee Paraphrase indeed: על־מות *super morte*. But then, who this Laben should be, is not agreed. Some would have Goliath understood: others, some principal man under Saul. Many again, (of all most improbable) his own son Absalom. There be some very learned men, who by a metathesis, or transposition of letters; in proper names, more properly called *anagrammatismus*; (of which kind there be divers examples in Scripture; as of transpositions in general, פדר for פרד:2 אלמגים for אלגומים, &c. and in proper names, יעבץ for יעצב; 1 Chron. 4.9. *Jehojachin*, for *Jechonias*, or *Jechonias* for *Jehojachin*, I know not which: 2 King. 24. & 25.) read this Laben, the radicals being the same in both, Nabal. Some tell us of Laben, that was a singer; mentioned, they say, 1 Chron. 15. and so *alamuth*, or *galamuth*, might be taken for an instrument. But I find no such name there: except they mean Ben, mentioned there verse 18. and would have לבן here to be translated *for Ben*. When all has been said that can be said, the conclusion must be the same as before; That these titles are very uncertain things, if not altogether unknown in these days.

**Verse 1. ***{With my whole heart}* God is not rightly praised, except the whole heart go along in our praises.

*{all thy marvellous works}* Hidden works, that pass man’s reason, and so make him marvel, Psal. 136.4.

**V. 2. ***{and rejoice in thee}* Your favor shall be the subject of my joy, verse 14.

*{thou most High}* Psal. 83.18.

**V. 3. ***{When}* Or, *Because*.

*{are}* Or, *were*.

*{turned back}* Because you have made my foes flee before me.

*{they shall fall and perish}* Or, *they fell and perished*.

**V. 4. ***{Thou hast maintained my right}* Heb. *thou hast made my judgment*. However the enemy seem for a time to prevail, yet God preserves the just, Psal. 4.1.

*{right}* Heb. *in righteousness*.

**V. 5. ***{Thou hast rebuked the heathen}* Job 20.28. And, by rebuking them, made them to flee.

*{Thou hast destroyed the wicked}* The captain of them; for he speaks but of one.

*{thou hast put out their name for ever and ever}* Wiped out. It signifies an utter destruction, Deut. 9.14. and 29.20. Psal. 109.13.

**V. 6. ***{O thou enemy, &c.}* Most take these words as a sarcasm, (or close derision) as if David intended to upbraid them of their bloody thoughts and threats: O enemies, your intended and so much threatened desolations have taken effect; cities are destroyed, and their memory perished: So you boasted and threatened indeed; and had done it perchance, but that there is a just God in heaven, whom your rage could not reach, &c. Others make this to be a continued prosecution of the former verse, expressing the vengeance of God upon his enemies: and translate the first words; (making האויב not a vocative, but a genitive) *The desolations, or devastations of the enemy are come to an end*, &c. This indeed does cohere better with the former; but not so well with the latter verse. It may be that David did not aim here at any particular enemy, but only intended to set out the terrors of war, and the strange issues and effects of it; when both the guilty and the innocent are involved in one ruin, as though all were committed and permitted to the power of the sword, and no superior power to overrule: which breeds great confusions among men, and great murmurings. To this David, in the next verse, with much confidence, grounded upon his own experience, &c. does oppose, That notwithstanding these confusions, there is a God in heaven, still; a righteous God, who though for a while, for secret causes, &c. he give way, and suffers the triumphing of the wicked; yet will in due time, sooner or later, in this world or that which is to come, &c. Upon this follows this inference, That men therefore at such times, should not so extremely be dismayed, but lift up their hearts to God, with assured confidence, and wait patiently. So Isa. 51.12,13. *I, even I, am he that comforteth you; Who art thou, that thou shouldst be afraid of a man*, &c.? *and hast feared continually every day, because of the fury of the oppressor, as if he were ready to destroy: And where is the fury of the oppressor?* Which words do very much illustrate these, and confirm this interpretation of them. See also upon Psal. 62.11. *God hath spoken*, &c. at the end there.

**V. 8. ***{he shall judge the world in righteousness}* Psal. 96.13. and 98.9.

*{in uprightness}* Heb. *in uprightnesses*. That is, most upright.

**V. 9. ***{The Lord also will be a refuge for the oppressed}* Psal. 37.39. & 46.1. & 91.2.

*{a refuge}* Heb. *an high place*.

*{a refuge for the oppressed}* Our miseries are means to cause us to feel God’s present care over us.

**V. 10. ***{that know thy Name}* As God is said to know them by name, Exod. 33.12, &c. whom by special favors, and evidences of his love and care he takes notice of: So they are said to know the Name of God, who besides the general knowledge, and acknowledgment of a Deity, have a more particular knowledge (grounded upon his word, and their own careful observation. See Psal. 107. last verse; and Psal. 28.5. Eph. 5.12, &c.) of his nature, and attributes: as mercy, justice, &c. See Exod. 34.5,6. Psal. 91.14, &c. Sometimes by Name, barely, (and so in the writing of the Rabbins usually) God is understood; as Levit. 24.16. twice השם *the Name*; there rendered, *the Name of the Lord*: by some others, *the Name*, only. So Psal. 75.1. *for that thy Name is near, thy wondrous works declare*. More of God’s Name, see also upon Psal. 68.4. *by his Name JAH*, and Psal. 54.1. *by thy Name*.

*{hast not forsaken them that seek thee}* 2 Cor. 1.10. 2 Tim. 4.17,18. See upon Psal. 27.8.

**V. 12. ***{When he maketh inquisition for blood}* Gen. 9.5.

*{When he maketh inquisition for blood, he remembereth them}* Though God revenge not suddenly the wrong done to his, yet he suffers not the wicked to go always unpunished.

*{blood}* Heb. *bloods*: That is, bloodshed or murder, Psal. 10.12.

*{them}* Or, *it*.

*{humble}* Or, *afflicted*.

**V. 13. ***{thou that liftest me up}* Or, *lifting me up*. To wit now.

*{from the gates of death}* From present and most certain death, Gen. 4.7. Judg. 5.8.

**V. 14. ***{in the gates of the daughter of Zion}* In the open assembly of the Church. Gates of Zion are opposed to gates of death, v. 13.

**V. 15. ***{The heathen are sunk down in the pit that they made}* Psal. 7.16. & 57.6. For God overthrows the wicked in their enterprises.

**V. 16. ***{by the judgments which he executeth}* *The Lord of Hosts shall be exalted in judgment*: Isa. 4.16. But, *when sentence against an evil work is not executed speedily, the heart of the sons of men is fully set in them to do evil*. Nothing more works upon the generality of men, to make them acknowledge a providence, than a sight of his just judgments: and hardly will they believe a God or a providence, longer than they see them. See Prov. 1.32. Job 19. last verse.

*{Higgaion Selah}* *Higgaion*, taken ordinarily, from הגה, is as much as *meditatio*; so used (in construction, as they call it) Psal. 19.15. But here what it signifies, or to what purpose, I know no man that can tell me certainly: The Greek and vulgar Latin leave it quite out, as no part of the text; which I think is the safest way, except we know more of it. Of *Selah*, see before, Psal. 3.

**V. 17. ***{into hell}* Some by hell understand here, sepulcher; and so translate it. Others, the place of torments. For otherwise, say they, what man is he, good or bad, that can escape the grave? As for the Hebrew word שאול (whereof so much has been written) it is certain it signifies both. But besides these two significations, שאול (as ᾅδης in Greek) does also sometimes import a bare nonentity, or annihilation; as Isa. 5.14. *Therefore hell hath enlarged herself, and opened her mouth without measure: and their glory, and their multitude, and their pomp, and he that rejoiceth shall descend into it*. So here also some expound it, *redigentur in nihilum*: they shall be reduced into nothing: not that they shall be annihilated in very deed by death; (which is the error of many Jewish interpreters, who teach, that the wicked and ungodly shall altogether be extinct, nor ever rise again: contrary to Christ’s direct doctrine, Joh. 5.28,29. and the Apostles, 2 Cor. 5.10.) but because this word serves well, more fully to set out the vanity of worldly men; both of their lives, and of their actions.

*{that forget God}* Who think not on God, nor his judgments on oppressors, and so go on to wrong the poor, verse 18. Psalm 50.22. and these shall be destroyed.

**V. 18. ***{for ever}* Though God seem to neglect his for a time, yet he will not do it always, verse 12.

**V. 20. ***{to be but men}* Which they may learn by your judgments inflicted on them.

# PSALM X

**Verse 1. ***{Why standest thou so far off O Lord}* God, essentially is in all places equally, *intra omnia, sed non inclusus*, &c. that is, within all things, but not included: without all, but not excluded: whole and entire in the whole; and in every part of the whole: as the Schoolmen speak, and the Scriptures (David himself) teach, Psal. 139. and elsewhere. However, He is frequently said, to be, or to dwell, where his glory is most visible, as in the heavens; or in his Church, or wheresoever he does in an extraordinary manner manifest himself by any effects of his power or grace. Now because nothing is more proper and natural (if we may so speak) unto God, than to be just and righteous: where (for just, always: but hidden causes often:) where I say, he does suspend the execution of his just judgments, and suffers iniquity to prevail and to prosper; He is there, by a kind of *anthropopathia*, said to absent himself, or to hide his eyes: as being *of purer eyes* (Hab. 1.13.) *than to behold iniquity*, and suffer it. And sure enough it is, that he never does, absolutely and totally: but for a while only; (for what is long to him, to whom a thousand years are but as one day, or a watch in the night? Psal. 90.4. though to us it is otherwise, who by reason of our short continuance, think ten or twenty years a great matter:) for the further manifestation both of his justice and of his mercy in after-times; either in this world, or that which is to come; to which most promises in the Scripture, if rightly understood, have reference.

This whole Psalm, with divers others of the same argument, may serve for an ample confutation of their error, who make the worldly success of great undertakers, without any further inquiry, an argument of the goodness of their cause; as also for their consolation, and confirmation, who suffer, though it be much and long, (Psal. 74.9.) for a good cause. See also upon Psal. 37.1. *Fret not thyself*, &c. and Prov. 1.32. *For the turning away of the simple*, &c.

*{Why hidest thou thyself in time of trouble}* Heb. *Why hidest thou in time of trouble*: which I conceive would better have been supplied by a noun substantive, *thy face*, or *thine eyes*, than by this reciprocal pronoun, *thyself*. So is God in the Scripture very commonly said in full expression, *to hide his face*; as Deut. 31.18. Ezek. 39.23. Micah 3.4. and elsewhere frequently. And so Es. 57.17. *I hid me and was wroth*: there too there being no *me* in the text, I conceive it would according to the usual phrase of Scripture, more warrantably be supplied, *my face*: I do not remember this *me*, of God’s hiding, expressed in all the Scripture: included in the reciprocal verb, I find it in one place: Esa. 45.15. *Verily thou art a God that hidest thyself, O God of Israel the Saviour.* But the hiding there intended, is not the same as is here spoken of, of God his not affording himself to the relief of his people in time of trouble and persecution; but of his ways and counsels, both in his general administrations, and particular judgments, for the most part, impenetrable, and incomprehensible to mortal man: as we are taught, Rom. 11.33,34.
