Psal. 77. last verse, and elsewhere. And this to be the meaning of the Prophet in this place, besides other reasons may appear by ver. 9,10. of the Ch. *Woe unto him that striveth*, &c. Saturn, one of the ancientest heathen gods, had his name from thence: from *Sater*, I mean, *to hide*; as all learned men agree. And why not from this very kind of hiding, here spoken of by the Prophet; as well as divers other particulars of their mythology from other Scriptures, of which no question is made? Heathen mythologists, I know, tell us of another kind of hiding, belonging to this supposed Deity; as their manner is, when long time and prevailing superstitions, had obliterated original truths. There is good use to be made of such observations: neither do I remember to have met with it elsewhere. But to the Psalmist here: Though I know no great difference, as to the sense, whether we translate, *Why hidest thou thyself*, or, *thy face*: yet because this is the usual phrase of the Scripture; and that smallest differences in Scripture, sometimes, may afford observations of no small weight; I therefore take notice of it here. Besides in this particular place; for God to *hide himself in time of trouble*, might sound at first hearing, (to vulgar ears) as an imputation of some fear in God for himself: but to *hide his face*, or, *eyes*, (as some Rabbins here supply) though that too might be mistaken; yet not so easily, as I conceive.

**V. 2. ***{The wicked in his pride doth persecute}* Heb. *in the pride of the wicked he doth persecute*.

*{let them be taken in the devices that they imagined}* Psal. 7.16. & 9.16. Prov. 5.22. Let their devices not hurt us, but work their own ruin.

**V. 3. ***{For the wicked boasteth}* Insults, triumphs. *Why boastest thou thyself in mischief, thou mighty man?* Psal. 52.1. *Whose glory is in their shame*, Phil. 3.19. Or, boasts of God’s favor, because of his success, Joh. 16.2. *whosoever killeth you, will think that he doeth God service*. See upon Psal. 21.2. *his heart’s desire*.

*{and blesseth the covetous}* Rather, as in the margin there, *the covetous blesseth himself; he abhorreth the Lord*. See Deut. 29.19. *that he blesseth himself in his heart*, &c. and Deut. 8.11,12, &c.

Some translate the whole verse, which the words will bear too, *He praiseth the wicked for the desire of his heart, and blesseth the covetous, who abhorreth* (or despises) *the Lord*.

**V. 4. ***{will not seek after God}* In the Hebrew, there is no more question, *will not seek*, or, *enquire*. Now because to seek God, or after God, is an usual Scripture phrase, by which all duties of religion are often comprehended, as Psal. 9.10. *Thou hast not forsaken them that seek thee*, and elsewhere very frequently: why may not after God, here also, though not expressed, be supplied in the translation? I see no great reason but it may. Yet some interpreters of best account, are much against it, and call it (somewhat too rigidly by their favor) a depravation of the sense. For, say they, by this general word, will not seek, or consider; David does here point at the original cause of all wickedness and impiety; which is, want of due consideration. And that is very true; For if men (as would well become rational creatures to do) would take time, and make it a business, (which is indeed the business of their lives) well and seriously to consider with themselves; first in general, what is the end of man, as endowed with a rational and immortal soul: and then of particular actions, how they conduce more or less, or how contrary to that end: what is true, real, solid happiness; what is deceitful, and imaginary: this, and the like, often and seriously thought upon, must of necessity bring a man to a desire of seeking after God, and to a pursuit of the means, a conscientious life, and the like. See more upon Psal. 1. upon the inscription of it, *Psalm first*. However, though this general sense may do better here; the limited, by addition of those words, after God; warranted by so many other places, ought not in my judgment, to be called a depravation. But may not the foolish Seekers of our time, of whom the Scripture speaks: *ever learning and never able to come to the knowledge of the truth*, 2 Tim. 3.7. *tossed to and fro and carried about with every wind of doctrine*: (Ephes. 4.14.) whose labor is, by opposing approved and long settled truths, to overthrow all truth: may they not take some advantage of this general sense? They may certainly, as well as they can pervert all other Scriptures; their chiefest study: and as easily may they be answered, if any answer will serve men of such perverse judgments and affections.

*{God is not in all his thoughts}* Or, *all his thoughts are, There is no God*.

*{thoughts}* Psal. 14.1. & 53.1.

**V. 5. ***{he puffeth at them}* He scorns them, as if he could blow them all away with his breath.

**V. 6. ***{I shall not be moved}* *The evil shall not touch me*, Isa. 28.15. or it may be read, *who was never in adversity*: That is, Why should I fear danger, that never was in any? Psal. 73.5.

*{never}* Heb. *unto generation and generation*.

**V. 7. ***{His mouth is full of cursing}* Rom. 3.14.

*{deceit}* Heb. *deceits*.

*{under his tongue is mischief and vanity}* His words tend to the hurt of others.

Some, by the tongue here, would have us to understand the heart; because, say they, the heart is under the tongue. That the tongue is so taken sometimes, I grant; but I do not approve of the reason. However, under his tongue, for, in his tongue (that is, whatever he speaks) is the more probable interpretation in this place.

*{vanity}* Or, *iniquity*.

**V. 8. ***{He sitteth in the lurking places of the villages}* Some of the Rabbins by חצרים here turned villages, understand *atria*, that is, open courts, public Halls, and the like. The word of itself does include both senses we know: and some interpreters, besides Rabbins, like well of this latter, as if David aimed here, by this word, not at petty thieves, who rob in obscure places; but at the grand ones, who in open courts, and public places of commerce; by perverting justice, by horrible, though tolerated, and allowed extortions and exactions, and the like, do the same as the petty thieves, with as bad a conscience; but with more credit and allowance. And here some inveigh very much against merchants, as men usually very guilty of this kind of theft. For my part, I do not think so ill of that profession, but that even among them, I believe may be found men of great integrity, and generosity; nor so well of any other, that is a *quaestuosa professio*, (that is, whose chief end is gain and profit:) but that it will afford examples of such thieves good store. But how shall lurking places agree with open courts and common Halls? It may be answered, I know that the original word may be translated (as it is by divers) in ambushes, as well as lurking places: and ambushes may be laid in public places, there is no question: but then it follows immediately, במסתרים (*in latebris*) in secret places, which is directly opposite to public. Somewhat might be said to that too: but when all is done, I must profess, that I conceive villages, as it is here, the more warrantable translation of the two.

*{are privily}* Heb. *hide themselves*.

**V. 9. ***{He lieth in wait secretly, as a lion in his den}* He shows that the wicked have many means to hide their cruelty, and therefore ought the more to be feared.

*{secretly}* Heb. *in the secret places*.

**V. 10. ***{He croucheth}* Heb. *he breaketh himself*.

*{that the poor may fall by his strong ones}* Great men, if good and just, and truly generous, are a great support to the poor; and so it is commonly in a well governed state: But if otherwise, if of themselves wicked and ungodly, and the times licentious and tyrannical, no wonder if they prove great oppressors. The Hebrew word חלכאים, here translated the poor, is by most interpreters supposed to be a compound of two, and to signify the company, or congregation of the poor (or, *lowly*.) It is found in the Scripture nowhere, but in this place: except it be the plural of חלכה which is here in the 10. and 14. verses of this Psalm; and nowhere else to be found.

*{by his strong ones}* Rather, *into his strong*; to wit, teeth, or paws. For he continues the same metaphor of a lion couching and lying in wait. See Job 38.39,40.

**V. 11. ***{God hath forgotten}* He has forgotten the poor, therefore I may oppress them, verse 1.

*{he hideth his face}* Psal. 89.7.

**V. 12. ***{Arise, O Lord}* Psal. 82.8. Arise to help the afflicted; let the oppressors, that say, You regard not the poor, verse 11. see that they are deceived.

*{forget not the humble}* Psal. 9.12.

*{humble}* Or, *afflicted*.

**V. 13. ***{Wherefore doth the wicked contemn God?}* Psal. 74.10. Therefore you must needs punish this their blasphemy.

*{Thou wilt not require it}* To wit, mischief and oppression, verse 14.

**V. 14. ***{the poor committeth himself unto thee}* Leaves himself or his cause to your fidelity, Gen. 39.6. Isa. 10.3.

*{committeth}* Heb. *leaveth*.

**V. 15. ***{Break thou the arm of the wicked}* Job 38.15. Psal. 37.17. That is, all the instruments of his cruelty.

*{seek out his wickedness till thou find none}* Or, *then shalt thou seek out his wickedness, and find none*. They are incorrigible, therefore rid the world of them and their sins at once, Ezek. 23.48.

*{till thou find none}* Make diligent search; let not any of his sins pass unpunished, Or, so break him, that he have no more power to do wickedly.

**V. 16. ***{The Lord is King for ever and ever}* Psal. 29.10. & 145.13. & 146.10. Jer. 10.10. Lam. 5.19.

*{the heathen are perished out of his land}* Such as live like heathen in Canaan, Ezek. 16.3. or, God having driven out the Canaanites, Psal. 44.2. has given a sure token that he will not always bear with ill men.

**V. 17. ***{thou wilt prepare their heart}* To prayer, that they may know both how, and what to pray for. See Rom. 8.26. Jam. 4.3. Of ordinary speech, Prov. 16.1. *The preparation of the heart is in man*, &c. that is, man may propose to himself what (upon occasion) he means to say; but God will guide his tongue. He must treat God for that. See Psal. 141.3.

**V. 18. ***{To judge the fatherless}* God helps, when man’s help fails, Esay 59.16.

*{prepare}* Or, *establish*.

*{the man of the earth}* Man, that is, mere clay; dust and ashes. So Esay 51.12. *I, even I am he, that comforteth you: who art thou, that thou shouldest be afraid of a man that shall die, and the son of man which shall be made as grass?* By *man of the earth*, also, may be understood proud insolent men; whom the Grecians also call γηγενεῖς, and πηλογενεῖς. See also Jam. 3.15. *This wisdom*, &c.

*{that the man of the earth may no more oppress}* Or, *that man may no more daunt him out of the earth*.

*{oppress}* Or, *terrify*.

# PSALM XI

**Verse 1. ***{In the Lord put I my trust, &c.}* As if he had said; God has passed his promise to me; and my trust is fixed: Why do you set your hearts so violently to chase and pursue me, (*as a Partridge in the Mountains*, as himself speaks to Saul, 1 Sam. 26.19.) as though you would frustrate the purpose of God?

*{how say ye to my soul}* 1 Sam. 26.19. *for they have driven me out this day from abiding in the inheritance of the Lord, saying, Go serve other Gods.* Not that they said so in very deed; but the more emphatically to set out their wickedness and cruelty, representing the consequent of their act, as the original aim and purpose of their hearts.

*{flee}* A plural in the Hebrew, *flee ye*: as to David and his company.

**V. 2. ***{privily shoot}* Heb. *in darkness*; or, *in the darkness*: meaning (as most interpreters) that there was no place so private, or so dark, left to him, that was free from the persecution of his enemies.

**V. 3. ***{If the foundations be destroyed}* Heb. השתות, here rendered *foundations*, is by some interpreters both here and Isa. 19.10. *And they shall be broken*, &c. translated *retia*, or, *nets*. According to which translation some make the sense of the verse to be: *Certe retia destructa sunt: justus quid operatus est?* (so Vatablus) that is, *Their nets indeed are broken and destroyed; but what hath the just done to bring this to pass?* As if David intended by these words to give the glory of his deliverance, not unto any human power, or policy; but to God only, and his immediate working. But that this word שתות does also signify *retia*, is not a thing agreed upon, and generally granted: and being chiefly grounded upon that place of Esay, it will appear upon further examination, but a weak ground. That it signifies *foundations*, is not denied, or doubted by any. Now, *foundations*, taken metaphorically, may be applied to many things: which has bred great diversity of interpretations. Most think by *foundations*, the laws, execution of justice, &c. upon which commonwealths are chiefly founded, to be meant. Some take *foundations* here, and Psal. 82.5. to be meant of the same; and refer to that place, for the meaning of this. But the words, though the same in our translation, are different in the original; and even of the meaning of the word there, there is some controversy also, as we shall see there. The right meaning here, I take to be this; That there is a time of such general confusion, when the very foundations which kept up other structures, are overthrown: and then no wonder, if the righteous also have his share of sufferings. So God himself once answered Baruch, by his Prophet Jeremy, Jer. 45.4,5. *Behold, that which I have built, I will break down; and that which I have planted, I will pluck up*, &c. See also Psal. 32.6. Now at such times when weak men begin to stagger, or to murmur, it is the part of a well-grounded godly man, to comfort himself, in this confidence, that God is in heaven, nevertheless, and does see and consider, &c. as David here in the next verses. See before upon Psal. 9.6. *O thou enemy*, &c.

**V. 4. ***{The Lord is in his holy Temple}* Hab. 2.20. The Lord is still where he was, and therefore I will look up to him for justice, who can and will right me, when men will not.

*{his holy Temple}* By *Temple*, here, most understand heaven: which indeed is God’s most proper Temple, whereas Temples or Churches made with the hands of men are but figures and types. Neither does the original word היכל, though frequently used of the Temple; properly signify a Temple, but a palace: as it is here rendered by some. Much like unto this, is that expression Psal. 102.19. *For he hath looked down from the height of his Sanctuary: from heaven did the Lord behold the earth*: here also by *Sanctuary*, heaven is understood.

*{his eyelids}* That is, *his eyes*, according to the Hebrew phrase: and the repetition of the same thing in other words; (*The Lord is in his holy temple, the Lord’s Throne is in Heaven: his eyes behold, his eyelids try*, &c.) very well sets out David’s confidence, and earnestness, being then in great affliction, and in the bitterness of his sufferings; (when men of a temporary faith, and weakly grounded in their principles, are wont to give off:) opposing their reiterated maxims, to such inferences as wicked men, and weak judgments (when they see the afflictions and oppressions of the righteous, and the success of the proud and ungodly: whether openly so (proud and ungodly) as sometimes: or covertly, under the pretence of zeal and piety; which is very ordinary; and of the two, the more dangerous:) would make. See also upon Psal. 33.13. *The Lord looketh*, and 38.9. *Lord all my desire*.

**V. 5. ***{The Lord trieth the righteous; but the wicked}* See Jam. 1.12. *Blessed is the man*, &c. and Hebr. 12.6,7,8. *For whom the Lord loveth*, &c.

*{his soul hateth}* He hates a wicked man with all his heart. An emphatical expression, very seasonable then, when a good cause does suffer, and most men are ready to judge, (as heathens were wont of them that oppressed the Jews:) that they are most in God’s favor, and by consequence, most godly, who have most occasion to boast of their successes and *heart’s desire* (Psal. 10.3.) in this world.

**V. 6. ***{rain snares}* Ezek. 38.22.

*{fire and brimstone}* As in the destruction of Sodom, Gen. 19.24.

*{an horrible tempest}* Or, *a burning tempest*. All expressions of terrible judgments and calamities which by God’s special order and appointment shall befall those wicked ungodly men (though great pretenders of godliness perchance) who did, or do so much boast of their flourishing condition, and great successes in this world. Now because it does often fall out that men that have been great oppressors of the Church, and of God’s people, enjoy their peace and prosperity to the last, and die quietly, as to the world, upon their beds: it is very probable that David by these expressions intended especially the judgments of God upon them that are such, in another world: of which though the Old Testament speaks but sparingly, and obscurely; (whereof see upon Psal. 37.1. *Fret not thyself*, &c.) according to the condition of those times: yet in the New, we read plentifully; so that all execution of justice and judgment seems almost to be referred to that time: to that at least, if not as the only, yet the proper time. See 2 Thess. 1.5,6,7,8,9. *Which is a manifest token of the righteous judgment of God*, &c. Nay from this very thing, (besides the revelation of Scripture) many learned men have inferred the necessity of a future judgment, because we see things fall out oftentimes, so contrary to what we apprehend of God’s justice, in this world. We may the sooner be induced to believe that David by these words intended eternal pains and torments, because hell in the New Testament is not only set out by *fire*; absolutely: but by *fire and brimstone*, in St. John’s Apocalypse, more than once: the original of which expression in St. John, though it be truly derived from that particular *fire and brimstone*, which was the destruction of Sodom and Gomorrah, as in Genesis we read at large, Gen. Ch. 19. and is further confirmed by our Savior, Luk. 17.29. yet it is possible that it was not without some allusion to this very place of David, and other like, if other like be. See also upon Psal. 68.3. *But let the righteous*.

*{this shall be the portion of their cup}* Which they shall drink, even to the dregs, Ezek. 23.34. Psal. 75.8.

**V. 7. ***{For the righteous Lord loveth righteousness}* Heb. *righteousnesses*: That is, all manner of righteousness, to punish the evil, and preserve the good. Or, *righteousnesses*, that is, righteous men: the abstract, for the concrete; as *scelera* for *scelesti*, and the like.

*{his countenance doth behold the upright}* The Rabbins do mention three several interpretations of these words: all good and orthodox, and all agreeable enough to the original words. The first is that we have here. But here it must be noted, that in the original it is in the plural, *their countenances do behold*: not *his countenance*, &c. Whence some would infer the mystery of the Trinity to be here intended: and whereas it is objected that the affix לו3 is sometimes singular; Genebrard says no, but only where that mystery is intended. But that is manifestly false. Mercerus upon Job does observe many places, where it is used for a singular. As for the plurality of the verb in the original, (ויחזו *videbunt*) that makes nothing to this purpose, because it depends of the nominative plural, פנים *facies*, for *faces*; or *countenance*, according to the Hebrew idiom. However, if we follow this first interpretation, it is free for us, if we will make a mystery of it, to make a plural of this affix; since it may be either plural, or singular.

Now for the sense of the words according to this first translation: we need not go far: the *countenance of God* is proposed unto us, as the sum and perfection of happiness: *There be many that say: who will shew us any good? Lord lift thou up the light of thy countenance upon us*, Psal. 4.6. But secondly, if we translate, (as we may very well, without doing any violence to the words) *The upright*, (*rectitudo*, for *rectitudine praediti*: examples of such defective expressions in the Scripture there be many:) *shall see thy face*: then have we here a clear expression of the happiness of God’s Saints in heaven, which does consist in the *beatifical vision*, as it is called by the Schoolmen: according to that of David, Psal. 17. last verse. *As for me* (having spoken before of worldly men’s happiness:) *I will behold thy face in righteousness: I shall be satisfied when I awake with thy likeness*; and that in the Gospel spoken to Christ by one of his Disciples: *Lord shew us the Father, and it sufficeth us.* I like this here the better, because it will confirm the interpretation of the former verses, *fire and brimstone*, &c. of which we have spoken there: it being so much the more probable that by those expressions the eternal pains of hell are intended; if it be granted that by this here, eternal blessedness is set out, and proposed: of which no question can be made, if this translation of the words, against which I know nothing very material, that can be objected, be allowed of. But because I think it the part of a faithful interpreter to unfold and pursue the riches of God’s word, where it may be done without affected and groundless subtlety: I must not conceal from the reader a third interpretation of the words, not less warrantable, for ought I can see, than either of the former, and affording a doctrine full of comfort, and of great use, in times of great confusions. It is this: *Their faces* (of the just and righteous:) *shall see him righteous*, or, *that he is righteous*. The providence of God, which in this world is an object of our faith; it shall be hereafter, of our eyes: we shall then know, and discern clearly, (and in that, no small portion of our happiness:) which we do here but imperfectly; (witness, David, Jeremy, Habakkuk, and so many other of the godly that have stumbled at this block, even ready to fall, as themselves have acknowledged:) that God is just and righteous in all his ways: and that all things, even that seeming prosperity of the wicked, which now we can so hardly brook, was but a mystery of God’s wisdom, to bring about with more admiration the true blessedness of those that put their trust in God, and have patience unto the end, &c. Very pregnant are the words of Malachi to this purpose: *Ye have said, it is vain to serve God: and what profit is it*, &c. *And now we call the proud happy: yea they that work wickedness, are set up: yea they that tempt God, are even delivered.* Mal. 3.14,15. then follows verse 17, &c. *In the day when I make up my jewels*, &c. *Then shall ye* (both good and bad: all men shall) *return and discern between the righteous and the wicked: between him that serveth God, and him that serveth him not.* So Ezekiel: *and you shall know that I have not done without cause all that I have done in it, saith the Lord*, Ezek. 14. last verse.

# PSALM XII

## TITLE

*{upon Sheminith}* Or, *upon the eighth*. See notes on the title of Psal. 6.

**Verse 1. ***{Help}* Or, *Save*. Nothing does so much afflict pious souls; no public nor private calamity goes to their heart so much, as when they see impiety and profaneness to prevail, and to bear sway: men that formerly made a show of godliness and piety, to be carried with the stream, and to draw others by their examples, that are not thoroughly well grounded: in a word, the kingdom of Satan, (Epicurism, Libertinism, sacrilege, and the like:) to prevail and flourish; God’s power, and sovereignty, to be eclipsed: whereby a way is made to public and professed atheism; as often, through just judgment, it does happen, where God had been long dishonored by formal and hypocritical religion.

*{for the godly man ceaseth}* Mic. 7.2. Which dares defend the truth, and show mercy to the oppressed. חסיד in the original: a very remarkable word: of which see upon Psalm 86. verse 2. *for I am holy*.

**V. 2. ***{with flattering lips}* He means the flatterers of the court, which hurt him more with their tongues than with their weapons, Psal. 5.9. Or, as some of the best of commentators here, *Potius David in genere deplorat mores suae aetatis*, &c. It is more likely that David does here deplore in general the corrupted lives and manners of the most of his days, their base and fraudulent dealing towards one another; oppression of the poor, and the like.

*{with a double heart do they speak}* Heb. *an heart and an heart*, 1 Chron. 12.33. that is, a deceitful heart; as *a stone and a stone*, signifies deceitful weights, Deut. 25.13,14.

**V. 3. ***{proud things}* Heb. *great things*, Dan. 7.8.

**V. 4. ***{With our tongue will we prevail}* We are able to persuade to whatsoever we take in hand.

*{our lips are our own}* We have skill, power and liberty to speak.

*{are our own}* Heb. *are with us*.

*{who is lord over us?}* Who shall appoint us what we shall say?

**V. 5. ***{For the oppression of the poor}* God is moved with the complaints of the poor, and sets them at liberty for his promise’s sake.

*{saith the Lord}* He makes God himself, by an ordinary figure of rhetoric, to speak: which of itself is very elegant and emphatical: and serves here very well to set out the certainty of the matter, here averred, and which David would have all men, though there were little appearance of it to the eye of many, very confident of; that God would in due time do justice, and bring down the power and pride of these lofty ones.

*{from him that puffeth at him}* Or, *that would insnare him*; as is in the margin. The Hebrew words יפיח לו may also, with no less advantage to the sense, be translated (as indeed they are by more than one) *He will [freely] speak unto him*; or, *He will puff at him*; that is, the godly, before oppressed by the mighty, will now dare to speak freely, &c.

**V. 6. ***{The words of the Lord are pure words}* Having spoken before of the treachery of men, and breach of faith; and of his confidence in God, grounded upon the Word of God: he now falls (by way of brief interposition) upon the commendation of God’s Word, the sincerity whereof, certainly believed, as it much adds to our confidence; so it condemns the more, the infidelity of men, before spoken of.

*{pure words}* Without dross of falsehood sticking to them, or mingled with it.

*{as silver tried}* Some think that the furnace here intended, is the furnace of afflictions and tribulations, by which as the faithful are tried; so by those comforts they then receive from the Word of God, they try and prove the truth and excellency of it, more sensibly, than at any other time, or by any other way. The Grecians also use the same similitude, to express the trial of a friend in time of adversity, saying; Κρίνει φίλους ὁ καιρός, ὡς χρυσὸν τὸ πῦρ. and Χρυσὸς μὲν οἶδε ἐξελέγχεσθαι πυρί· Ἡ δ’ ἐν φίλοις εὔνοια καιρῷ κρίνεται.4 In the similitude of pure silver, besides the worth, &c. (in which respect the similitude of gold is elsewhere used, Psal. 19.10. & 119.127.) the pure whiteness does also well fit to set out the purity of God’s word. Campensis, in his so much commended paraphrase, expresses it; *Verba & promissiones Domini candidissimae sunt*, &c.

*{as silver tried in a furnace of earth}* 2 Sam. 23.31. Psal. 18.30. & 119.140. Prov. 30.5.

*{purified seven times}* That is, many times, 1 Sam. 2.5. Prov. 24.16. & 26.25.

**V. 7. ***{Thou shalt keep them}* Meaning the poor and needy, spoken of, ver. 5.

*{them}* Heb. *him*; that is, *every one of them*.

**V. 8. ***{when the vilest men are exalted}* Vile men in authority, set up wicked men, and pull down good men, Prov. 28.12,28. & 29.2.

*{vilest men}* Heb. זלות, *vilitates*. The abstract for the concrete, as often.

# PSALM XIII

## TITLE

*{Chief Musician}* Or, *overseer*.

**Verse 1. ***{How long wilt thou forget me, O Lord, for ever?}* Or; *How long, O Lord? wilt thou forget me for ever?* Psal. 6.3. *O, How long, O Lord, wilt thou forget me? for ever?*

*{for ever}* The expression is very pathetical. There can be no greater aggravation of misery, than that it should be both grievous, and lasting. *We see not our signs, there is no more any Prophet, neither is there among us any that knoweth how long*, Psal. 74.9. A sad business, when it is come to that. But this, *how long*: this, *ever*; faith will tell us, though it should be as long as we live, is but a very little time; yea a very moment of time; no ways considerable to eternity. So does Ecclesiastes oppose faith, to sense. *Though a sinner do prolong his days*, (as to the eyes and thoughts of men, that look no further than this world:) *Yet he shall not prolong*, &c. Eccles. 8.12,13. and the Prophet Habakkuk, 2.3,4. upon another occasion, yet to the same purpose, grounding upon the inconsiderableness, (if well considered:) of that which we commonly call *long*: *For the vision,—though it tarry—it will not tarry,—but the just shall live by faith.* And so David himself, in other places, which we shall meet with, and take notice of, as we go along.

*{wilt thou hide thy face}* This is contrary to the light of God’s countenance shining on his, Psal. 4.6.

**V. 2. ***{How long shall I take counsel in my soul}* Heb. *counsels*: that is, change my purpose often, by reason of new troubles: as a sick man does his place. Or, think daily of new ways of escaping.

**V. 3. ***{lighten mine eyes}* As when the body is much exhausted through sickness, or otherwise; it appears most in the eyes, which are as it were a glass through which we may look into the inward temper and disposition of the whole: So if the body be revived, and receive comfort; it will soon appear by the quickness and cheerfulness of the sight. See 1 Sam. 14.29. Jonathan, Saul’s son, after he had refreshed his *faint and wearied* body, with the taste of a little honey; *See I pray you*, says he, *how mine eyes have been enlightened, because I tasted a little of this honey.* When therefore David does here pray, that God would lighten his eyes, he prays for his whole body, that God would raise it from that pining condition into which perpetual grief and danger had brought it; and by consequence he prays, that God would deliver him from those crosses and persecutions, which had been the occasion of his grief. See Psalm 38.10. *My heart panteth, my strength faileth me: as for the light of mine eyes, it also is gone from me.* But in a sense much different from this, the word of God is said, *to enlighten the eyes*, Psal. 19.8. which is to be understood of the eyes of the mind, or understanding, as it is expressed, Ephes. 1.18. verse.

**V. 4. ***{I have prevailed against him}* Which might turn to God’s dishonor, if he did not defend his.

*{moved}* Or, *troubled*, Psal. 10.6.

**V. 5. ***{But I have trusted}* Mine enemies could not draw me from trusting in you, though they endeavored it with all their might.

*{have trusted}* Or, *do trust*.

*{in thy mercy}* The mercy of God is the cause of our deliverances, Psal. 6.4.

*{shall}* Or, *doth*.

**V. 6. ***{because he hath dealt bountifully with me}* Because of benefits past, I will depend on him for others to come, Psal. 9.10.

*{dealt bountifully with me}* Psal. 116.7. & 142.7.

# PSALM XIV

This Psalm, with very little variation, we have again Psal. 53. whereof more there.

**Verse 1. ***{The fool}* That is, the wicked as appears by what follows; and so *folly*, for wickedness, in the Scripture, often. Aristotle teaches in his Ethics, that a man may be imprudent, or simple, (that is in effect, a fool;) as to the main end of his life, (which is the general end of all men, true happiness:) who is very wise, or rather, as the same does well distinguish there, very prudent to some particular (worldly) ends: and on the other side, some very wise, as to the main; who to particular ends and businesses, are very simple and mere idiots. *Ethic. Nicom.* lib. 6. cap. 5. et 7. To which purpose is that of the Prophet Jeremy also; *For my people is foolish, they have not known me; they are sottish children, and they have none understanding: they are wise to do evil, but to do good, they have no knowledge*, Jer. 4.22.

*{fool hath said in his heart}* Psal. 10.4. & 53.1. Philosophers distinguish between the λόγον ἐνδιάθετον, and the λόγον προφορικόν of man: his inward, and his outward speech.

*{There is no God}* He shows, that the cause of all wickedness is infidelity; that is, either an absolute denying of a God: which scarce any man, or heathen ever did: and if any did, they were, even by heathens themselves (as we call them: but more religious in many things, than many professed Christians:) severely punished in former times: or infidelity, (from whence we term infidels) may be taken, and is commonly, for a denying of God’s providence, and his care of particular men, and that he is a rewarder of the just, and unjust. Such an infidel, or atheist was Epicurus: and it is much to be feared that many such are to be found among ordinary men, who may be known by their loose and riotous lives, and even contempt of godliness: but among many also, who make great show of holiness, and have the name of God very frequent in their mouths, but for worldly ends, that they may have the praise of men; or the better to bring their politic ambitious designs to pass; there being no better and surer instrument, for the most part, for the achievement of worldly ends, than hypocrisy, and palliated sanctity. Such were the Pharisees of old among the Jews, Matth. 23.14. and those whom the Apostle speaks of, 2 Tim. 3.5,6. Now of such atheists or infidels in general that deny, that is, believe not in their hearts, a providence, &c. the Psalmist has spoken already, Psalm 10. where he describes them more particularly, verse 4,11,13. and it is very probable that he intended no other here. For to deny the providence and justice of God, is in effect to deny the being of a God: and if there be any difference, this is the worst and most dangerous (the denying of a providence either general, or particular:) atheism. But how could David see their hearts, or judge of their thoughts? no otherwise, (not to speak of him here as a Prophet, which would be impertinent) than by their deeds, and actions. However, he speaks not here of any particular man; but in general delivers this doctrine, that whoever they be that do such and such things; whatever they say, or profess of themselves; yet in very deed, they are no better than arrant atheists.

*{they are corrupt}* There is nothing but disorder and wickedness among them.

**V. 2. ***{The Lord looked down from heaven upon the children of men}* Psal. 102.19. and 33.13. *The Lord looked.* See there.

*{if there were any}* By these and the following words, are chiefly intended by David natural men, not enlightened by his Word, nor sanctified by his Spirit: which because the greater part, by far; in those days, especially: he sets out in general terms, as speaking of all. Except we take the *children of men* properly here, (as some do) opposed to *the children of God*, Gen. 4.26. & 6.2. However, these words of the Psalmist seem to be applied by St. Paul to mankind in general: and in some sense, it may be true even of the best, that their best actions are not without some mixture; nor so exactly good, as to abide a trial of God’s strict judgment. See Job 4.18,19. & 15.14,15. Some would have David to mean this of his persecutors only; which is contrary to the whole tenor of the words.

**V. 3. ***{They are all gone aside}* God found all out of the way, Rom. 3.12.

*{filthy}* Heb. *stinking*.

*{there is none that doth good, no not one}* Rom. 3.10. See upon verse 2. *If there were any.* And upon Psal. 53.1. *that doth good*.

**V. 4. ***{who eat up my people as they eat bread}* Λαοβόροι, an usual epithet of unjust, tyrannical judges and governors. See Mic. 3.3. However, as is here observed by some, there being no note of similitude in the Hebrew, these words, *they eat bread*, may be joined also with those that follow, *and call not upon the Lord*: as an expression of their security; as Jer. 22.15. *did not thy father eat, and drink*, &c. that is, did not he live securely: or, enjoy peace and prosperity? as some there. See also before, upon Psal. 3.5. So, *to eat bread*, of a feast, or banquet, Exod. 18.12. Then the next words would well follow upon these, the better to set out their great unthankfulness and infidelity; since that this plenty of food and provision, which God does daily afford us, (with all other necessaries to life;) should be a clear evidence (even to natural men; according to St. Paul’s doctrine, Act. 14.17. *Nevertheless he left not himself without witness*, &c.) of the goodness of God; and a daily provocation to us, to praise and glorify him (that is, *to call upon him*) for those things we receive from him.

*{call not upon the Lord}* Isa. 64.7.

**V. 5. ***{There they were in great fear}* Their destruction shall come upon them suddenly, in their greatest security, (*where no fear was*, as Psal. 53.) and to their great amazement: Esay 13.8. *And they shall be afraid*, &c. The words also might be taken of the godly; as an expostulation or check for their too great amazement in time of trouble and confusion, as though no God were in heaven, &c. See before upon Psal. 3.6. and the passage of Esay there.

*{were they in great fear}* Heb. *they feared a fear*. See more upon Psal. 53.5. *where no fear was*.

*{for God is in the generation of the righteous}* Because their conscience tells them, that God loves the godly, whom they oppress. Or, rather, the Psalmist himself shows the ground of his confidence, why it must needs be, that wicked men shall in time be destroyed, and the godly delivered; because God is, where godliness is: he is with, and among a righteous *generation*, or nation, and people. *O my Lord*, says Gideon to the Angel that appeared unto him, *if the Lord be with us, why then is all this befallen us?* Judg. 6.13. The Israelites went upon the same ground, that *none evil could befall them*, because *God was among them*: Micah 3.11. but they mistook in the main, for which they and their confidence is rebuked by the Prophet. For though God was *among them*, in some respect, as in their Temple, and because he did own them still, as his peculiar people, and the like: yet they had driven him from that possession of their hearts, (by which he might most truly and effectually be said to be among them,) by their corrupt lives, and abominable sins; as is there more particularly declared unto them by the said Prophet. Now for Christians, (since the perfect light and revelation of eternal life, and rewards, as is showed more at large upon Psalm 37. *Fret not thyself*, &c. the case is not altogether the same. We cannot absolutely ground, either privately or publicly, upon temporal deliverances, because we know, and have just ground to confide, that *God is with us, and among us*. It is enough for us to know, and firmly to believe, that nothing shall, or can *separate us from the love of God*: no *sword, famine, nakedness*, &c. that is, no kind of death, or calamity, though never so terrible to flesh and blood: and that *all things work together for good, to them that love God*: as the Apostle teaches at large, Rom. 8.28,35,36. &c. The same may be said of the godly of those times under the Law, sometimes; as does appear by many examples: especially in the case of particular men, who might suffer sometimes, though just and righteous themselves, with the ungodly. And sometimes alone, for particular ends, and trials, as Job did.

**V. 6. ***{You have shamed the counsel of the poor}* By mocking them for trusting in God, Psal. 22.8.

**V. 7. ***{O that the salvation of Israel were come}* Heb. *Who will give, &c*.

*{out of Zion}* Some think though the occasion of this Psalm was in the time of David’s persecutions under Saul, before he was possessed of the kingdom, and had settled the Ark of God (which he long before purposed and intended) in Zion: yet he might write this afterwards, when King, &c. Others make him pray to God for the speedy accomplishment of what, by Divine inspiration, he intended; to wit, when he should be established in his kingdom, to remove the Ark, and to settle it in that place. *Out of Zion*, then, because of the Ark of God, and propitiatory there placed; to which (that is, to God, there resident and president:) supplications were made, and deliverances commonly said to come from thence: as Psalm 20.3. *Send thee help from the Sanctuary, and strengthen thee out of Zion.* See also upon Psal. 63.2. *To see thy power*, &c. Or, Or, out of Zion, with reference to Christ, the expected Redeemer and Deliverer, the last refuge of the faithful Jews in their greatest distress and calamities, (as to us Christians, the coming of Christ unto judgment:) *the consolation of Israel*: Luke 2.25. who was promised, and expected out of Zion, Ps. 10.2. *The Lord shall send*, &c. because at Zion, and Jerusalem began the preaching of the Gospel; according to that of Esay 2.3. *for out of Zion shall go forth the Law: and the word of the Lord from Jerusalem*.

*{when the Lord bringeth back the captivity of his people}* He compares their misery to a captivity; for the people were not captives in David’s time: as Job 42.10.

# PSALM XV

**Verse 1. ***{Who shall abide in thy Tabernacle?}* Psal. 24.3. &c.

*{abide}* Heb. *sojourn*.

*{who shall dwell in thy holy hill?}* The Tabernacle before, and the *holy hill*, here, (that is, mount Zion; or mount Moriah:) types and figures of heaven. For otherwise we know, that these places were not places of habitation: especially for so many, as either among the Jews, or in any other nation (Act. 10.34,35. *Of a truth I perceive*, &c. *But in every nation*, &c.) were so qualified, as to pretend justly to these privileges. It further appears by the last words: *He that doth these things, shall never be moved*: which cannot be made good, but of an everlasting habitation: (Lu. 16.9.) such as we know this present world does not afford unto any. However, to love God’s houses, built with the hands of man, with relation unto God, as they are God’s houses; places of God’s worship; appropriated to that use; and to be conversant in them, (so long as God is, and may be served in them, as he should be:) with zeal and affection; is a very good sign; and he that is a hearty Christian will think it no small happiness in this world, to have the privilege of such a house, unto which he may freely repair, and wherein he may perform his duties and devotions.

I believe David did know what it was to worship God *in spirit and truth*; as well, as most in our days: and that he was not deficient in that kind of worship, we may guess by that interest he had in God (*a man according to his own heart*: 1 Sam. 13.14. and elsewhere:) and by these very Psalms of his, so high and sublime in their kind, that they are able to carry a man’s soul to heaven, whilst he reads them, and to bring him to the sight of Him, that cannot be seen with mortal eye; if he reads with an innocent heart, and with competent zeal. Yet David as spiritual as he was, how heartily he did affect God’s material house, and solemn standing service, may sufficiently appear by his own expressions, so frequent in these his Psalms: whereof somewhat has been said upon Psal. 27. v.8. *seek ye my face*, &c.

They that have a more particular relation to God’s house, or houses, all true lawfully ordained ministers, they have reason to applaud their happiness, for that nearer access they have unto God, by their place, than other men; and that interest they have, by their place, to his house, above other men. If they be of David’s spirit, they will: *For a day in thy Courts, is better than a thousand*, says he. *I had rather be a door-keeper in the house*, &c. Psal. 84.10. But they must be such too, in their lives and conversations (besides other qualifications more proper to that calling:) as he describes here: else, their judgment will be so much the heavier, that having been so much honored by God, they have so much dishonored Him. For the literal interpretation of these words, (which we willingly allow of, though not as chief and principal in this place:) we have Ps. 84.1. *How amiable are thy tabernacles*, &c. and v.4. *Blessed are they that dwell in thy house*, &c. where the literal meaning is most proper, and so principal. And so Ps. 27.4. *That I may dwell in the house of the Lord.* See also upon Psal. 43.30. *Send out thy light*.

*{holy hill?}* Heb. *hill of holiness*.

**V. 2. ***{He that walketh uprightly}* The Heb. word (תם or תמים) properly imports simplicity and sincerity of heart, which commends all our actions unto God, and without which our best actions before God, (such as are outwardly and materially good, and of themselves very commendable; but not done with reference unto God, and his glory; nor out of any true love of goodness itself, but for some other end,) become greatest sins; hypocrisy being so great a sin, as that all sins are sometimes comprehended under that name.

*{in}* Or, *with*.

**V. 3. ***{taketh}* Or, *receiveth, or endureth*.

**V. 4. ***{In whose eyes a vile person is contemned}* It is out of all question, that the original words do afford that sense also that was in the former English translation: *He that setteth not by himself, but is lowly in his own eyes*: which sense is not rejected by any, as not agreeable to the Hebrew; but because they conceive the other, (which we have here) to be more agreeable to the context. For, say they, most things here set down, have reference to mutual conversation; and therefore more likely that David intended here also somewhat to that purpose. Besides, some add the opposition between the two members of this verse, *a vile person contemned, but honoreth them*, &c. I am not against this sense, which most do follow; though for the other, we have the authority of the Chaldee Paraphrast, of great antiquity, which is somewhat: nay and some Rabbins too, of very good account. But for the reasons that are opposed, they are but plausible, at the most, not convincing; of the first, which by most is insisted upon, we shall have occasion to speak in the next note, and shall examine of what force it is.

But grant it plausible; may not this be as plausible, that David intending here the description of a perfect honest man that is in the right way to heaven; as he describes him by his moral performances, without which, it is certain that there is no way to heaven: and that all pretence to godliness without that, is but mere delusion and hypocrisy: so also that he should add somewhat of a just and godly man’s apprehension of his own insufficiency, to perform or fulfill the law: upon apprehension whereof he must, laying aside his own righteousness, cast himself of necessity upon God’s mercy, and seek for some other way of salvation, than what he can find from his own performances? This would be very well expressed by these words so rendered; *that setteth not by himself, but is lowly*, &c. and would very well agree with Christ’s judgment concerning the proud and high-conceited Pharisee, (set out unto us as a very innocent moral man, and very charitable, &c. but for his pride, blameless:) and the humble Publican, whose very profession, a publican, was accounted a practice and profession of sinning, Luk. 18.9. &c.

And as for the opposition of the members, objected against this sense: I account them a very weak objection. For why is not the opposition as good, and as apparent, if we say, A man, who though he think of himself, and his own parts and performances very meanly; that is very apt to judge the best of others, and does highly honor all them that make profession of godliness, and live accordingly? However my meaning, is not so to commend this sense, that I would pass by the other, which I think very good and warrantable too: as was said before.

*{In whose eyes a vile person is contemned}* This must be understood with certain limitations. First it must be a *vile person* indeed; not in our own private judgment; nor upon a judgment grounded upon particular quarrels and interests; (for most are very prone to fly in the face of them, whom they suppose to have done them wrong, though it may be a wrong judgment in them to think so; and that the case, by men indifferent, is otherwise apprehended:) but generally so accounted, by all, or most honest men, that make profession of godliness. Secondly, we must distinguish between private men, and magistrates. *Fateor quidem si dominentur improbi, non ita spernendos esse, quin illis pareamus*, &c. I must confess, if wicked men be in power, we may not so far contemn them, but that we must yield obedience unto them so far, &c. they are the words of one upon this place, whose zeal was not always tempered with so much discretion and moderation, as himself, in some of his prefaces and epistles, does acknowledge and bemoan; as might be wished: however, for his zeal, and much other worth, by men of zeal and moderation worthily honored: but very unhappy in many of his disciples and abettors, whose ignorant rigidness, in the most, and contempt of all antiquity, has been the occasion of many troubles both in Church, and commonwealth.

But this, *obiter* only. If we must obey bad men in authority, in all lawful commands; much less are we allowed to revile them. Job 34.18. *Is it fit to say to a King*, &c. and Exod. 22.27. confirmed by St. Paul, Act. 23.4,5. However so far a *vile man* (wicked, impious, sacrilegious, &c.) though in authority may and must be *contemned* by us, that what respect we give him, must be in submission and obedience to God; not out of any admiration, or high esteem we have of his greatness, wealth, honor, power: which things we must look upon with a generous scorn, and contempt: nor think them happy for this their exaltation, and glory, be it more or less: but rather (within ourselves, at least) pity them, as men accursed (in their condition) in the eyes of God: (Psal. 92.7. *When the wicked spring as the grass*, &c.) and pray to God to keep us and our friends from such places, such preferment, such prosperity; and to bless the condition of an honest beggar, before theirs. As for others that are no magistrates, and are known to have prospered by wickedness, it were not amiss if such were publicly scorned by all men, *ne se magnum aliquid adeptos putent, qui huc felicitate perveniunt, ut rideantur*: that they may have no occasion to applaud themselves, but to repent, when they see what is the fruit (even in this world) of their unrighteous courses; rather to be an object of scorn, than of envy.

*{he that sweareth to his own hurt, &c.}* *He that sweareth to his neighbour, and disappointeth him not*: was our old translation, embraced not only by all, or most ancients, and by all modern interpreters and commentators of the Roman party: (as many, at least, as I have seen:) but so well liked of by most of our side, that were it not for the Hebrew points, which are conceived to cross it, they show a good inclination to prefer it, before that we have here. Indeed there is reason for it: the same as was alleged before, for that sense, *In whose eyes a vile person is contemned*: because most passages in this description, or character of a moral honest man, are conceived to relate to mutual conversation. I confess, that is some reason; but not of such force, that except we can answer what is objected against it, I would think myself bound to give it the first place. The objection is, that הרע so pointed as we find it in our Hebrew Bibles, signifies *malum*, not *proximum*. The one to be read *harang*, the other, *hareangh*. Calvin’s words are observable: *Optime quadraret Graeca versio; nisi puncta obstarent: quibus tamen si nunquam caruerunt Hebraei, credibile tamen est, non semper scribendis fuisse ab illis expressa.* It is not likely, says he, though points have always been, yet that they were always written. Any man may see, what he aims at, when he does say so. Yet he dares not but embrace the other version.

Another tells us plainly, (a man of great exactness, for the text, and particular words, as any has written upon the Bible:) that though it be not so usually, yet he knows no reason to the contrary, but that *rang*, both here, and in some other places of Scripture, may be taken for *reangh*, the difference being so small. It cannot be denied, but that there be examples, good store, of far greater anomalies and permutations of words, in the Scriptures: which have been collected by learned men, and are obvious enough to men acquainted with these studies. However, since this sense is good too, and very warrantable, that is made of the words, without this shift; there is no reason that we should impose it upon any man against his will; this that we have spoken of, I mean, generally so well approved of, but not so well agreeing with the common use and notion of the word, רע: at least, so pointed. We shall therefore take that sense into consideration too, and another too, besides that, though not ordinarily taken notice of; after we have said somewhat more of this we are now upon. Of which all that we have more to say, is concerning the word *reangh*, in this and the former verses, commonly translated *proximus*, or *neighbor*: that we take heed of the mistake of the Pharisees, who by *neighbor*, in the Scripture, (as where we are commanded to love our neighbor, and the like;) understood them that lived near, and whom we daily converse with: whereas by the idiom, and propriety of the Hebrew tongue, רע or הרע (in both places, at least:) is no more than ἕτερος, or *alter*: any man (friend, or foe; known, or unknown:) that comes in our way, and whom, for the present, we have to do with. And so the Apostle Paul, what he found in the Hebrew, *Thou shalt love thy neighbour*: he very well expresses in the Greek, ἀγαπήσεις τὸν ἕτερον. Ὁ γὰρ ἀγαπῶν τὸν ἕτερον, says he: *For he that loveth another, hath fulfilled the Law*, Rom. 13.8. Neither is this proper to the Hebrew word only: for both the Grecians use their ὁ πέλας; and the Latins, their *suus*, much after the same manner; as might appear by divers examples, if need were. However, it does not follow from this, but that we may owe some duties to a neighbor, which we do not to a stranger: as again, such a case there may be, where we ought to prefer a poor stranger, before a neighbor. But these notes are not for the examination of such cases.

*{he that sweareth to his own hurt, and changeth not}* Most Jews expound this, *to his own hurt*, of penances, by vows imposed upon ourselves, whether upon our own bodies, purses, goods, or anything that belongs unto us. In this sense, the word *evil* perchance, will be more proper than *hurt*. The Grecians also call such penances, such especially, that are inflicted upon the body, κακώσεις, and κακουχίας. As this sense is not to be excluded, since we know that this very thing was taken into consideration by the Law of God, so I grant that I see no reason to make it the proper sense of the words. It is ordinary for men to make bargains, and to pass their words, and to confirm them by oath, and afterwards to repent. Or to promise of their own accord, and to bind themselves by oath, and afterwards to be sorry. It were well, if Christians, ordinary Christians were as strict, and conscientious in keeping their oaths, as ordinary heathens, Romans especially, have been. But a hundred evasions have been found out by Christians, that were never thought of by heathens: or if used by some, yet condemned by most: yea and severely punished in some places. As for forced oaths, or unlawful oaths, it affords many speculations, and limitations, which we are not here to determine of. I find a third translation and interpretation of the words: *that sweareth to do evil* (to another, to be supplied) *but doth not recompense it*: or, *fulfill it*. This sense is not very probable, and therefore less will serve of it. It is true, it is the part of an honest man, if he have made any such vow, or oath, not to keep it; but to repent of his rashness, and perjury. For though there may be more hurt in the execution, than in the perjury; yet the unlawfulness of the execution, does not acquit him of the guilt of perjury before God. A godly man that makes conscience of idle words, will not easily ensnare himself with such rash oaths, and therefore though such a thing may happen perchance, yet it is very unlikely that David in this his description, among other particulars of daily occurrence, would insist in a case so extraordinary as this.

*{and changeth not}* He that will not hinder his neighbor, though he hinder himself, v.4.

**V. 5. ***{He that putteth not out his money to usury}* There is no obscurity in the words, but about the thing, *usury*; many questions are raised, which to discuss, or determine, would require more room, than such annotations can afford. The common distinction of biting, and more moderate usury, has no ground at all in Scripture; neither is the bare etymology of the word, a sufficient ground to build such a distinction upon. For certain it is, that the Jews were forbidden all manner of usury, without any such difference among themselves. But to one that was a stranger, they might put money to use; that was permitted. What from hence may be gathered, or whether anything at all, to make it lawful among Christians, those that have written of this controversy at large, may be consulted. See, among others, learned Grotius upon Luke 6.35. μηδὲν ἀπελπίζοντες.

*{taketh reward against the innocent}* Exod. 23.7,8. Deut. 16.19.

*{he that doth these things}* For, *not the hearers, but the doers of the Law shall be justified*, Rom. 2.13. and, *Happy are ye not, if ye know these things: but if ye do them*, Joh. 13.17. A doctrine much to be pressed, because many *deceived*, 1 Cor. 7.9,10. and Gal. 6.7. through ignorance of it.

*{shall never be moved}* Out of an happy estate, into which he is entered already, Psal. 55.22. Prov. 10.30. & 12.3. This may go for the literal meaning of the words, not altogether to be rejected, because even to us Christians (though to be understood with many limitations and restrictions,) the Apostle says: *That godliness is profitable unto all things, having promise of the life that now is, and of that which is to come*: 1 Tim. 4.8. and that this literal sense and meaning seems also to be further confirmed by the Prophet Esay, *He that walketh righteously and speaketh uprightly, he that despiseth the gain of oppressions*, &c. *He shall dwell on high: his place of defence shall be the munition of rocks*, &c. Esay 33.15,16. However, we must still remember, that it is the proper style of the Law, under the types and figures of things material and temporal, to propose unto us, that are now come to the full light of the Gospel, and to the knowledge of those mysteries concerning the kingdom of heaven, which were not known in former times; to us, I say, Christians, things spiritual and eternal. And more particularly, that the *mount* and *tabernacle* here spoken of, are to be understood mystically, as to the chief intention of the Psalmist; as has been said upon the first verse.

# PSALM XVI

## TITLE

*{Michtam}* Some render, *A golden Psalm*, which the words would bear, and the matter of this Psalm being very sublime, as treating of the resurrection of Christ, &c. would be not improbable, were there not other Psalms so inscribed, of which (eminently above the rest) no such thing can be said. Others therefore take it for an instrument of music: others for a certain tune. Eugubinus upon these Psalms has divers other conjectures and expositions: but not any, that he can fix upon. Nothing more certain, than that it is uncertain what to make of it. See upon former Psalms 7, &c.

**Verse 1. ***{In thee do I put my trust}* The Psalmist does often profess this, and repeat it in his greatest extremities, sometimes with much variety of words, and sometimes repetition of the same, that he had great confidence in God. *I will say of the Lord, He is my refuge and my fortress; my God, in him will I trust*, Ps. 91.2. And this, not without cause, but upon a sure confidence, grounded upon good experience, that God did delight in nothing more, than that a man should put his trust in him, Psal. 50.14,15. *Offer*, &c. *And call upon me*, &c. and Psal. 147.10,11. *He delighteth not*, &c. *The Lord taketh pleasure*, &c. St. Chrysostom, upon the tenth Psalm 8. verse, maintains, that even sinners, yea great sinners, if they put confidence in God, are able to do much: and become ἀχείρωτοι, that is, to others inconquerable. It may seem strange, and somewhat contrary to some places of Scripture: himself acknowledges it θαυμαστόν: a strange thing: yet I believe he spoke it upon good experience, Acts 14.9. it is recorded of a certain cripple: *The same heard Paul speak: who steadfastly beholding him, and perceiving that he had faith to be healed*, &c. For want of which faith, or confidence, we read that Christ himself *did not* (nay *could not*, Mar. 6.6.) *do many mighty works in his own country*, Matth. 13. last verse. We might enlarge ourselves upon this subject, if David’s words required it. All we shall add is, that if Christians everywhere did well understand the nature of God in this one point, and particularly, there would not be such suing to Saints, and to the blessed Virgin (ever to be remembered with all honor and respect:) as is in many places. God himself, in Christ, would oftener be called upon, and oftener thought upon, than he is.

**V. 2. ***{My goodness extendeth not to thee}* As Job 22.2. & 35.7. Nor my goods, Psal. 50.9. &c. and this seems the most genuine sense of the words: though some (men of eminent worth otherwise:) upon this ground, that because some parts of this Psalm do certainly belong unto Christ, as applied to him by the Apostles, therefore all must: upon this ground, they have devised this sense: *my goodness*; that is, my sufferings and afflictions, my cross and passion, עליך, *is for thee, and from thee*: that is, say they, not occasioned by any sin, or proper transgression of mine; but to satisfy your justice, &c. But as I conceive their ground to be but weak, (as may appear by divers other Psalms, and particular chapters of Prophets, where this rule does not hold) so this interpretation as violent.

**V. 3. ***{But to the Saints}* My goodness, as they that concur with me in the same affection of zeal for your glory: that think it greatest happiness, to see piety and religion promoted, &c. and my goods, as they that have a peculiar interest in them.

*{and excellent}* Excellency is often ascribed to God, and to those things that belong to him more nearly. *His Name alone is excellent*, Psal. 148.14. and, *the excellency of Jacob*, in the Prophets often: and Psal. 47.4. *His excellency is over Israel*, Psal. 68.34. As if he said here; My goodness is nothing to the Holy One, and Excellent of heaven; but to the Saints and excellent upon earth; whose nearer relation unto God, and imitation of his holiness, &c. gives them right (among men) to these titles.

**V. 4. ***{their sorrows}* עצבות in the original: and עצבים from the same *radix*, being taken for idols; as Jer. 22.28. &c. some think the former also should have that meaning in this place. However, if we like better of that more proper signification, *sorrows*; yet since idols are here spoken of, certain it is, that the Psalmist does thereby allude to the etymology of the word עצבים taken for idols, coming from a *radix* that signifies *dolore afficere*, to occasion grief, or pain. Now superstition and idolatry may be said to occasion sorrow and grief, and trouble of mind, in many respects, which is showed at large by Plutarch in his treatise περὶ δεισιδαιμονίας, and some others of the ancients, that have written against superstition.

*{hasten after another}* Or, *give gifts to another*.

*{their drink offerings of blood will I not offer}* He would neither do as they did, nor honor them so much as to name them.

*{names into my lips}* Exod. 23.13. Josh. 23.7.

**V. 5. ***{The Lord is the portion of mine inheritance}* Deut. 32.9. Lam. 3.24. Inheritances were divided, and drink likewise in banquets, of old: each had his part. Let others choose worldly comforts, God is my portion.

*{of mine inheritance}* Heb. *of my part*.

*{thou maintainest my lot}* You not only give it me at first, but also preserve me in it against my foes.

**V. 6. ***{The lines are fallen unto me in pleasant places}* That is, my inheritance. The land was divided of old by lines, Isa. 34.17. Psal. 78.55. The word might have been translated *portion* here, as well as Josh. 17.5,9. & 19.9. and elsewhere. *A portion is fallen to me*, &c. It is the same word in the original.

**V. 7. ***{my reins also instruct me in the night seasons}* Job 33.15. Night after night; for the word is, *nights*. In which time you do, by secret inspirations, instruct me, Psal. 51.6. Or, my inward thoughts or affections direct me to praise God. It is certain, that according to the Scripture phrase, or rather, Hebrew dialect, and idiom, the *reins* are often put for the heart: or at least, the same ascribed to the one, as is to the other. So God as he is said to search the heart, so the *reins*, often: and so joy, as to the heart, Prov. 23.15. *my heart shall rejoice*: so to the reins in the next verse, *yea my reins shall rejoice*; is ascribed. To seek a natural reason, (which some do here) I think is not so necessary, as I think it impertinent, in anybody, to require it. For common words and expressions, in all languages, generally, (and every language has its proper customs) go more by use and custom, than by any reason grounded upon nature. *Purgatis auribus*, and *emuncta nare* does very well in the Latin, to express quickness of sense and apprehension: in some other language it would be mere nonsense. So, *pectus sapiens*: and *ex pectore sapientiam*, and the like. However, it is common to all languages, to set out wisdom, and counsel, by profundity, and secrecy; to which purpose the *reins* fit very well; besides what may be said of their resemblance to the heart itself, observed by Hippocrates, and further explained by anatomists. See also upon Psal. 38.7. *For my loins*, &c.

Now because the motions of God’s Spirit, are secret and invisible; and the fittest time for them, when we are freest from other objects, and most retired to ourselves; which is chiefly in the night, upon our beds: therefore most interpreters, by this *instruction of the reins* here mentioned, understand, divine inspiration, sanctified thoughts, secret instincts, and the like; which may receive some confirmation from Psal. 51. verse 6. *Behold thou desirest truth in the inward parts: and in the hidden part thou hast made me to know wisdom.* Anatomists teach, that the kidneys have two membranes; an inward, very thin, without any fat, or veins: and an outward, which is very thick of fat: insomuch, that in gross bodies, the kidneys are as it were hidden, and appear not easily. And therefore say some of them, *to search the reins*, in Scripture, is as much as, to seek out, and to bring to light, things that are hidden, and abstruse. Their confidence makes me take notice of it: but if we were to discourse it, somewhat else might be found, no less probable and satisfactory, I believe; but whether more true, I know not.

**V. 8. ***{I have set the Lord, &c.}* Act. 2.25. Heb. *equally set*. I have looked on him without irregular affections and passions.

*{before me}* In my heart, by faith; in obedience, as my mark; in sincerity, as my judge; in hope, as my rewarder and reward.

*{he is at my right hand}* To uphold and defend me.

*{right hand}* Psal. 73.23. & 110.5. & 121.5.

**V. 9. ***{Therefore my heart is glad}* *Even in laughter the heart is sorrowful*, sometimes: witness the Wise man, Prov. 14.13. That is true joy, that comes from the heart; and communicates itself to other parts; as to the tongue particularly: by which it works, (except it be kept in of purpose) and shows itself abroad. *For out of the abundance of the heart, the mouth speaketh*, Matth. 12.34.

*{my glory rejoiceth}* Meaning his tongue, Psal. 30.12. & 57.8. Act. 2.26. Gen. 49.6. according to some translations. For man’s tongue is the instrument of glorifying God, and man’s glory above the beasts, that have tongues and speak not.

*{my flesh also shall rest in hope}* In hope of resurrection, when it is in the grave, ver. 10.

*{rest in hope}* Heb. *dwell confidently*.

**V. 10. ***{For thou wilt not leave my soul in hell, neither, &c.}* Act. 2.31. & 13.35.

*{For thou wilt not leave}* This is meant of Christ, who raised himself, having not seen corruption, and will raise all his to glory.

*{my soul in hell}* Or, *me in the grave*, Psal. 3.2. & 11.1. as Lev. 22.4. or rather, *my body in the grave*. So it suits best with that which follows, and with Peter’s proof of Christ’s resurrection, Act. 2.27,31. *soul*, for *dead body*, Lev. 21.1. Num. 6.6. the other word is put for *the grave*, Gen. 37.35.

**V. 11. ***{In thy presence is fulness of joy}* Rather, You will show me fullness of joy *with thy countenance*; for so it is interpreted, Act. 2.28. Except we should follow their translation, who there also translate the Greek, μετὰ τοῦ προσώπου σου: *in tui conspectu*; that is, *in thy presence*; as Beza does: and this translation fits better to the purpose of the Apostle there, speaking of the resurrection.

*{joy}* Heb. *joys*.

*{at thy right hand, &c.}* Or, *by thy right hand you will show me pleasures for evermore*, Act. 2.33. If so be that the Apostle there did intend these words of the Psalmist, which is not likely: neither was that their proper place, if he had intended them, but ver. 28. It is more likely that Christ did allude to these words of the Psalmist, Mat. 25.33,34. where by the *right hand of God* is plainly meant, *eternal life*. So expressed in the 46. verse.

# PSALM XVII

**Verse 1. ***{Hear the right}* My plea for my righteous cause.

*{the right}* Heb. *justice*.

*{attend unto my cry}* Men pray earnestly, and cry out, in great troubles.

*{not out of feigned lips}* Heb. *without lips of deceit*. The deceitfulness of the heart, is often ascribed unto the *lips*, because the lips and tongue are the usual instruments of deceit. So the Israelites, in their adversity, they sought unto God, with diligence and earnestness, Psal. 78.34. *When he slew them*, &c. but it follows there; *nevertheless, they did flatter him with their mouth; and they lied unto him with their tongues. For their heart was not right with him: neither were they steadfast in his Covenant*, 36,37. It should be our first care, before we put up our prayers unto God, to take some time, to examine our hearts, and consciences, whether all be right there; and so may we pray with confidence.

**V. 2. ***{Let my sentence come forth from thy presence}* Make it known that I have a just cause.

**V. 3. ***{in the night}* When none but you could see me, and my soul free from business, was in its own posture.

*{thou hast tried me}* Strictly examined me, as gold is tried by fire.

*{shalt find nothing: I am purposed that my mouth shall not transgress}* Or, *hast found nothing: I did purpose that my mouth should not transgress*.

*{I am purposed that my mouth shall not transgress. 4. Concerning, &c.}* The words in the original Hebrew, if taken by themselves, are all plain enough, but the construction and connection of them is very intricate and obscure. Some of the most accurate, translate the former words: *Quod cogito, non transgredietur os meum.* Or, *quod cogitavi, non transivit os meum*; which they expound to this purpose, as if he protested, that he was no hypocrite or dissembler; but that what his heart conceived, his mouth uttered, and no other. Though we cannot find fault with this translation of the words, neither with the sense, or sentence (if considered in itself) that they gather out of it; yet how to gather that sense from those words, I do not well see. The same translation leaving out the *quod*, which they add to the Text, to make that sense they would have, will afford this sense also, which we have here approved of by some of the best: and I should think Psal. 39.1. *I said I will take heed to my ways, that I sin not with my tongue: I will keep*, &c. if nothing else should put it quite out of doubt. There is more difficulty in the next words, how to connect them either with that before, or that which follows. But most follow this sense, as if he intended to profess his innocency in matter both of words, and works. Concerning words, thus; *I am purposed*, &c. as it is in the former verse. Concerning matter of works, and life, (that is, in his expression, *Concerning the works of men*) *By the word of thy lips*, &c. that is, say they, being guided and directed by your word, I have kept myself, &c. Psal. 52. if well considered, may much confirm this exposition, where proud words, and violent works (here styled, *the paths of the destroyer*) are interchangeably set out, and condemned. Yea, Psal. 140.11. *a man of tongue*, and *the violent* (איש לשון and איש חמס) are put together in one verse. Such a one the Greeks in one word express, μεγαλήγορον.

If this will satisfy, it is well. But because divers are not satisfied with it; neither indeed does the coherence so clearly appear, which breeds new conjectures, and expositions: I shall further add; First, that these former words, *Quod cogito, non transgredietur os meum*: may very well, from Psal. 39.1,2. be rendered: *what I think*; (or, *What thoughts soever arise in me*;) yet *I will not offend with my tongue*: I will be silent. Then secondly, that those words immediately following, which here begin a new verse, to wit, *Concerning the works of men*, may as well, if not in verse, yet in sentence be joined to the former: as if he said, *What I think*; or, *What is in my heart, concerning the works of men, shall not pass my mouth*; that is, Though I see the prosperity of the wicked, and what many godly suffer in this world, and thereupon my heart within me is moved often with indignation; yet I will take heed that I offend not with my tongue; no man shall hear me murmur against God, or question his providence therefore. Then, *By the word of thy lips*, &c. that is; Neither shall I be tempted (being better instructed by the Word of God) by the success of the wicked and violent, to follow their example. Best interpreters agree that the prosperity of the wicked is here spoken of by David: which is also confirmed by Psal. 73. which treats wholly of that subject, where we find some of these expressions, as ver. 2. *My feet were almost gone; my feet were well nigh slipt*; as here, verse following, *Hold up my goings in thy paths, that my footsteps slip not.* And as here, *I am purposed, that my mouth shall not offend*: So there, verse 15. *If I say, I will speak thus; behold I should offend*, &c. Neither is it a bold thing to question the truth or fitness of those divisions into chapters and verses, which is done by divers upon occasion, and may be done by any without offence, since that it is not authentic, but of later (as all men agree) institution or invention.

**V. 5. ***{Hold up my goings}* I observed your word as my circle, that I might walk uprightly by keeping within it.

*{Hold up my goings in thy paths, that my footsteps slip not}* Heb. *To uphold my goings in thy paths, that my footsteps might not slip*.

*{slip not}* Heb. *be not moved*.

**V. 6. ***{for thou wilt hear me}* Because you use to hear me, I believe you will hear me now, Psal. 116.2.

**V. 7. ***{Shew thy marvellous lovingkindness}* Psal. 31.21.

*{kindness}* Heb. *kindnesses*.

*{thou savest by thy right hand, &c.}* Or, *that savest them which trust in thee from those that rise up against thy right hand*; that is, against your power; to frustrate your counsel.

**V. 8. ***{Keep me as the apple of the eye}* As men are careful to keep their sight, Deut. 32.10. Zech. 2.8.

*{hide me under the shadow of thy wings}* As a hen does her chickens from the kite, or other ravenous creatures, Mat. 23.37. Psal. 91.1,2.

**V. 9. ***{that oppress me}* Heb. *that waste me*.

*{from my deadly enemies}* Cruel enemies, that cannot be satisfied, but with my death, Psal. 35.4.

*{my deadly enemies}* Heb. *my enemies against the soul*. Or, *who compass me about for my life*.

**V. 10. ***{in their own fat}* Their prosperity makes my misery the more, Job 15.27. Psal. 73.7. & 119.70.

*{proudly}* 1 Sam. 2.3. Psal. 31.18.

**V. 12. ***{Like as a lion that is greedy of his prey}* Heb. *the likeness of him* (that is, *of every one of them*) *is as a lion that desireth to ravin*.

*{lurking}* Heb. *sitting*.

**V. 13. ***{disappoint him}* Heb. *prevent his face*.

*{which is thy sword}* Or, *by thy sword*.

**V. 14. ***{From men which are thy hand}* Or, *from men by thine hand*. See upon Psal. 80.

*{whose belly thou fillest with thy hid treasure}* That is, with food and other worldly comforts that come out of the earth, in these wicked men abound sometimes, when good men want them, Psal. 73.7.

*{they are full of children}* Or, *their children are full*.

**V. 15. ***{As for me, I will behold thy face in righteousness}* I will not live wickedly, as they do, but righteously, and then I shall enjoy your favor here, and heaven hereafter, Matth. 5.8. 1 Cor. 13.12. Some think by all this, are only meant those inward joys and comforts, arising from a sense of God’s favor, and certain confidence of his love, proper to the godly in their greatest crosses and afflictions; these to be meant by *the face of God* here; and by his *likeness*. But if they restrain those joys, to present sense and feeling only, and make not things future, and hoped for, to be their chief object; David having yielded in the former verse so fully the comforts of this world, to worldly men; I do not see where the strength of the antithesis (*As for me*, &c.) does lie, if he also looked no further. It is true, these spiritual joys are incomparably greater, and sweeter than any temporal can be; but as the earnest, or first fruits of that which we expect to be perfected in heaven, in the perfect enjoyment of God, *face to face*, &c. So the Apostle testifies of Moses, that he despised the treasures of Egypt, &c. *because he had a regard to the recompense of the reward*, Heb. 11.26. And might not David as well? Besides this, the words themselves, of themselves, seem to import as much: and it was not perchance without some allusion to them, that in the New Testament, *to see God*, Heb. 12.14. and *to see the face of God*, Revel. 22.4. is taken for eternal life.
