As for the word תמונה translated, *likeness*: Saint John might perchance allude to it, 1 Epist. 3.2. *We know that when he shall appear, we shall be like him.* This expression: *the likeness of God*, we find also Numb. 12.8. where Moses is said to have seen the similitude, or *likeness* (it is the same word, as is here) *of God*; by which most understand, *the glory*: and so is the word translated there by the Septuagint; as here also, δόξα in both places: and because we find the translation of the Septuagint often retained in the New Testament, it is possible there might be some allusion to this place, where the kingdom of heaven, or eternal life, is expressed by this word, either δόξα alone, or, δόξα Θεοῦ; as Rom. 5.2. & 8.18. and elsewhere. From all which, I conclude that David in these words, had a further aim, than this world, and things present; whether spiritual, or temporal.

*{when I awake}* Out of trouble, or when I am raised from death, Isa. 26.19.

*{with thy likeness}* With being like you here: or, with the visible sign of your glory in heaven, 1 Joh. 3.2.

# PSALM XVIII

## TITLE

*{servant of the Lord}* Psal. 36.1.

*{this song in the day that the Lord delivered him from the hand of all his enemies, &c.}* 2 Sam. 22.

*{from the hand of Saul}* Especially of Saul, his greatest enemy, Mark 16.7.

This ἐπινίκιον, or Psalm of victory, we find inserted in the history of the Kings, 2 Sam. 22. but not without some, though to the substance of the Psalm not very material, yet observable variations and differences of words: which how they happened, is not so easy to resolve, as they are conspicuous. The chiefest are, some additions and omissions; if we may so call, what is more in the one, than we find in the other: an addition, where it is found: an omission, where it is not. Which nevertheless must be understood with sobriety, not as though there were anything in either, absolutely and simply, either abounding or wanting; but only comparatively. Besides the first verse here, which is not in Samuel, such differences of small additions or omissions may be observed in the second verse, compared with 1 Sam. 21.3. and in the 13. compared with the 14. of that chapter in Samuel; and again in the 14. compared with the 15. and in the 35. compared with 36. Now the cause of these differences; though not any can be given perchance of which we can be confident, yet it is probable, that they were found in copies written with David’s own hands; or that they were so fitted, (either by David himself, or with his approbation,) to different instruments of music, or different parts, in concords. As for differences in single words, those that proceed from a bare metathesis, or transposition of letters, as עברו, and בערו in the 12. verse, compared with 13. in Samuel; and תתפתל in the 26. compared with the 27. in Samuel; and the like, if any other be of that nature: as also those, where the difference is of one letter only, as וידא, and וירא in the 10. verse, compared with the 11. in Samuel: and one or two more, of the like nature: in all these, interpreters needed not for one such letter’s difference, or transposition, to have made a difference of sense, or interpretation, since that in divers other places of Scripture, such transpositions, and changes of single letters in some words are found, which nevertheless are taken for the same, and make no difference at all in the translation. The Masoreth of the Hebrews will afford store of examples in this kind, to them that are acquainted with it.

**Verse 1. ***{I will love thee}* Heb. ארחמך, that is, intimately, as a mother loves her child that comes out of her womb.

**V. 2. ***{The Lord is my Rock, &c.}* He uses this diversity of names, to show the greatness of his confidence in God, a great ground of his deliverance. See upon Psal. 16.1. *In thee do I put my trust.* As also to declare, that he ascribed his deliverance unto God only.

*{my strength}* Heb. *my rock*.

**V. 3. ***{I will call}* Or, *I called*.

*{who is worthy to be praised}* For delivering me from all my enemies, Psal. 50.15.

*{shall I be saved}* Or, *I was saved*.

**V. 4. ***{The sorrows of death compassed me}* Psal. 116.3. The better to set out the greatness of his deliverance, he does amplify by rhetorical metaphors the greatness of his danger, and distress. When the danger is once over, many men are apt to forget what they have escaped, whereby the vigor of their thankfulness is much abated. See Psal. 22.22. *I will declare*, &c.

*{ungodly men}* Heb. *Belial*.

**V. 5. ***{sorrows}* Or, *cords*.

*{hell}* Or, *the grave*.

*{the snares of death prevented me}* Deadly snares, or engines set for my death, Eccles. 9.12. Prov. 13.14. and 14.27.

**V. 7. ***{Then the earth shook and trembled}* This and what follows to the 15. verse, (*inclusive*, as we use to speak,) is not to be taken as a simple or historical relation of what happened, but as a poetical description of God’s presence and (in his powerful help and assistance,) concurrence, in David’s conquests. The Scripture, even where no poetry otherwise is suspected, is full of such poetical, or to speak more properly, sublime descriptions and expressions; far above the strain of the most sublime either poets, or orators. So the Prophets speak often of Christ, the Son of God, his coming into the world: a high subject indeed, and well deserving highest expressions; though in the manner of the execution to outward appearance, a very mystery of humiliation. So upon divers other occasions. This not understood by the Jews, has been a great occasion (and so of divers others, besides them,) of their infidelity.

**V. 8. ***{out of his}* Heb. *by his*.

**V. 9. ***{He bowed the heavens also and came down}* Psal. 144.5.

*{wings of the wind}* Psal. 104.3.

**V. 13. ***{The Lord also thundered in the Heavens, &c.}* What is here spoken of, of thunder, and lightnings, &c. in this and the next verse; and of hailstones in the former, whereby great armies have been overthrown, and the victory by heaven cast upon the weaker party, has really happened more than once. Besides what we read Josh. 10. to that purpose, ecclesiastical histories tell us of the very like that happened under several emperors; and of a certain Roman Legion from thence (because at their prayers obtained) called κεραυνοβόλος. However, we do not read of any such thing that happened in David’s wars, or time; and therefore, as was said before upon the former verse, conceive this to be rather a poetical expression, and acknowledgment of God’s help and favor to David, as though he had been visibly seen; with allusion also to what had happened in former times, as Josh. 10. and elsewhere. So is Isa. 29.6. also by the most, as allegorically spoken: but by some, taken literally.

*{the Highest gave his voice}* Thundered, lightened, and hailed, Job 37.2,4. Psal. 29.3.

**V. 14. ***{he sent out his arrows}* His lightnings.

**V. 16. ***{he drew me out of many waters}* Out of sundry and great dangers.

*{many waters}* Or, *great waters*.

**V. 17. ***{From my strong enemy}* To wit, Saul.

*{they were too strong for me}* Therefore God sent me succor.

**V. 18. ***{but the Lord was my stay}* My staff, to keep me from falling.

**V. 19. ***{large place}* Psal. 118.5.

*{because he delighted in me}* *He delighteth not*, &c. *The Lord taketh pleasure in them that fear him; in those that hope in his mercy*, Psal. 147.10,11.

**V. 20. ***{The Lord rewarded me according to my righteousness, &c.}* Some would have David by this, and all that follows to the 25. and 26. verses, to have no further aim or meaning, than to assert the justness of his cause against Saul, and others, that had opposed him. And I make no question but his words have a more special reference to his carriage in that business. But nevertheless, they are more general, than to be restrained to that only. Yet we must not think, that he intended by these words to justify himself before God absolutely, as in strictness of Law, blameless and innocent, which himself (Psal. 143.2. *And enter not into judgment with thy servant*, &c. Psal. 130.3. *If thou Lord shouldest mark iniquity*, &c. and elsewhere often) does openly, and clearly disclaim; but only to profess a general desire and endeavor to serve God, and to keep his Commandments: which, if true and sincere, will in any man beget a religious (so far as man is capable) and innocent life. Such a life and resolution, out of God’s bounty and goodness, having promises of God’s favor and protection, David might well lay claim to it, (a good evidence of his faith and trust in God,) and desire God to reward him *accordingly*, though not for the merits of the works themselves; which is another thing.

**V. 22. ***{I did not put away his statutes from me}* No danger could keep him from obeying God.

**V. 23. ***{before}* Heb. *with*.

*{from mine iniquity}* From following my corrupt affections, to the sin I was most inclined unto.

**V. 24. ***{in his eye sight}* Heb. *before his eyes*.

**V. 25. ***{With the merciful thou wilt shew thyself merciful}* He speaks of God, according to our capacity, who shows mercy to his, and punishes the wicked, Levit. 26.21,24,27,28.

**V. 26. ***{thou shalt shew thyself froward}* There is an ἀντανάκλασις, as they call it, in this, and the former words: that is, when the same word is repeated in a sentence, but in a different sense; as *periissem, nisi periissem*; and the like. Frowardness in man, or spoken of man, is a vice opposed to truth and sincerity. It may import also, stubbornness and perverseness. Spoken of God, it imports no more, than that God will meet with them that are such in their own way, and make them reap the fruits of their own doings. Much to the same purpose as that of Job, *He taketh the wise in their own craftiness: and the counsel of the froward is carried headlong*, Job 5.13. However, the words in the original Hebrew, are different in this place: yet is the translation by the same word, sufficiently warranted, both by what goes before, and by what we find elsewhere in the same kind; as Levit. 26.27,28. *If you will not for all this hearken unto me, but walk contrary unto me: Then will I walk contrary unto you also.* So in the English there: but most translate it, *Si vos perverse; (vel, temere) ego vicissim perverse, (vel, temere) contra vos.* So Prov. 3.34. *he scorneth the scorners*, &c. and Deut. 32.5. both words עקש & פתל (written there, *in forma reduplicata*, פתלתל) are used, and put together for one thing. These words 2 Sam. 22.27. are translated, *and with the froward, thou wilt shew thyself unsavory*. So in the Text: in the margin, *wrestle*. But there is no occasion at all for this difference. For it is the same word in the Hebrew: here, full, תתפתל: there, contracted, תתפל: so of purpose contracted; (as is well observed by some of the Rabbins) for the sound of the ear, that it might agree with the former (by which the *oratio* is made *multo concinnior*, as the masters of the rhetoric speak) תתבר and תתמם.

**V. 27. ***{but wilt bring down high looks}* When their sin is come to the full measure, Gen. 15.16.

*{high looks}* Isa. 2.11. & 5.15.

**V. 28. ***{light my candle}* You are he that do restore me from adversity to prosperity: that turn my mourning, and my heaviness into joy. Thus is the word *candle*, or *lamp*, often taken metaphorically in the Scriptures: *The light of the righteous rejoiceth, but the lamp of the wicked shall be put out*, Prov. 13.9. and elsewhere often. See Job 29.2,3,4. It may be understood of spiritual illumination also; as, *The word is a lamp unto my feet*, Psal. 119.105. but not so properly in this place.

**V. 29. ***{For by thee I have run through a troop}* He attributes it to God, that he both got the victory in the field, and also destroyed the cities of his enemies.

*{run}* Or, *broken*.

**V. 30. ***{the word of the Lord is tried}* Psal. 12.6. & 119.140. Prov. 30.5. Be the dangers never so many or great, yet God’s promise must take effect.

*{tried}* Or, *refined*.

**V. 31. ***{For who is God, save the Lord?}* Deut. 32.39. 1 Sam. 2.2. Psal. 86.8. Isa. 45.5.

**V. 32. ***{girdeth me with strength}* Psal. 93.1.

*{and maketh my way perfect}* Takes away all lets, and gives me victory in my wars.

**V. 33. ***{like hinds’ feet}* He made me speedily to take the forts of the enemies, Deut. 32.13. & 33.29.

*{upon my high places}* It is commonly expounded of the strongholds and castles of the enemies, built on high, as the fashion was; which David calls his, because (as some would have it) he had a right unto them; and was warranted by God for what he did: but rather, as others, his, because he had taken them, otherwise thought impregnable. *Mine*, therefore emphatically. Or, *my high places*, such high places or those particular holds, as David had to do with: as we say, *mine enemy*, &c. But when I consider Hab. 3.19. & 1 Sam. 9.13. I rather incline to believe, that *my high places*, or, *his high places*, were words proverbially used, importing, whatever it be that we most trust to, and wherein we think ourselves most secure. So *murus aheneus*, in Latin, much to that purpose. So Esay 33.15,16. *He that walketh righteously*, &c. *He shall dwell on high: his place of defence shall be the munition of rocks*, &c. Hence also, Psal. 20. v.2. *the Name of Jacob defend thee*: in the Text: for which the margin more literally, *Set thee on a high place*.

**V. 34. ***{He teacheth my hands to war}* Psal. 144.1.

*{so that a bow of steel is broken by mine arms}* Which is stronger than iron, Job 20.24.

**V. 35. ***{Thou hast also given me the shield of thy salvation}* To defend me from dangers.

*{thy gentleness hath made me great}* Or, *with thy meekness thou hast multiplied me*. He attributes the beginning, continuance, and increase of his good success to God’s favor.

**V. 36. ***{Thou hast enlarged my steps}* God gives me free room to walk, by removing my enemies, so that I need not tremble to make my feet slide under.

*{my feet}* Heb. *mine ankles*.

**V. 39. ***{subdued}* Heb. *caused to bow*.

**V. 42. ***{They cried—even unto the Lord}* A great aggravation of their wickedness, that God should not hear them, when they cried unto him in their extremity. The Psalmist would have us to think otherwise of God, and therefore does so often inculcate it, Psal. 107.6,13,19,28. and which is more strange, he speaks there, not of them only that called upon the true God, known unto the Israelites only; but of others also, who, in their distresses, called unto such as they knew. But of that see more there. See Prov. 15.29. Psal. 34.17,18. and 145.18.

**V. 43. ***{thou hast made me the head of the heathen}* Which dwell round about me.

*{a people whom I have not known shall serve me}* Although this and what follows, verse 44,45. in some sense, if understood of several neighboring nations, (not taken in the largest sense) subdued by David, 2 Sam. 8. and made tributary, as is intimated in the last verse of the chapter, may belong unto David too: Yet it is very probable, that his pen was so directed by the Holy Ghost, that the same might have a more proper application to, and accomplishment in Christ, who was of the seed of David, and whose type David was. Concerning Christ indeed, there be many prophecies to this purpose, of the gathering of the Gentiles unto him, and of his universal dominion. The Prophets are full of such. The last verses of this Psalm, the Apostle Saint Paul does expound of Christ, Rom. 15.9.

**V. 44. ***{As soon as they hear of me}* Not so easily applicable unto David, though we read somewhat in the history that may be drawn to it; but to Christ most properly, and emphatically, if the words be *verbatim* translated, (as in the margin) *At the hearing of the ear*; that is, by the preaching of the Gospel.

*{the strangers shall submit themselves unto}* Or, *lie*. Signifying subjection constrained, and not voluntary, as some would have it. But if those several places where the word is found in this sense, as Deut. 33.29. Psal. 66.3. and Psal. 81.15. be well considered of, it will appear, that it signifies subjection in general, whether forced, or voluntary: but more properly, such a degree of subjection, as is very low and servile: the reason of this *metonymia* (as they call it) being, not a supposed reluctancy: or as others would have it, contrariety of the mind and outward profession; but a supposed inclination in them, that are so low and servile, to lie, or flatter; which includes lying. So in Latin *vernilitas*, is taken for base flattery, from *verna*, a bondslave, or servant: *adulatio* from δοῦλος, a servant: and many like in all languages. See more upon Psal. 66.3. *submit*.

*{the strangers}* Heb. *the sons of the stranger*.

**V. 45. ***{The strangers shall fade away}* Fear shall cause them to come forth of their secret holes, to seek favor of me the conqueror.

**V. 47. ***{avengeth me}* Heb. *giveth avengements for me*.

*{subdueth}* Or, *destroyeth*.

**V. 48. ***{above}* Or, *from*, Psal. 69.1.

*{from the violent man}* That is, Saul, who of malice persecuted him.

*{violent man}* Heb. *man of violence*.

**V. 49. ***{Therefore will I give thanks unto thee}* This prophecy appertains to the kingdom of Christ, and vocation of the Gentiles, as Rom. 15.9. See before upon ver. 43. *a people*, &c.

*{give thanks}* Or, *confess*.

**V. 50. ***{and to his seed for evermore}* This did not properly appertain to Solomon, but to Jesus Christ.

# PSALM XIX

**Verse 1. ***{The Heavens, &c.}* The intention of the Psalmist in this Psalm is, to praise God for those many, whether ordinary, or extraordinary; natural, or supernatural, by which men come to the knowledge of God; or to speak more properly, to the acknowledgment of a Deity. Those means are, the natural, and common to all men, the consideration of God’s works, and among them, of the heavens; and in them, of the sun, as most eminent and conspicuous: of which kind of means in general, the Apostle also speaks, Rom. 1.20. and Heb. 11.3. and the Psalmist before in the 8. Psalm. The supernatural, not common at that time, but of a special grace and favor, indulged unto the seed of Abraham, the nation of the Hebrews; the Word of God, here called, *the Law*. And it is observable, that one word is used in the Scripture to express the course of nature (as we speak commonly) as well in heaven as sublunary things; and the precepts or ordinances of the Law; which word is חק: of which, as it had particular reference unto David, see before, upon Psal. 2.7. *I will declare*, &c. Besides places there quoted, Job 38.33. *Knowest thou the ordinances of heaven*, חקות שמים. The Latins also, when they speak of the course of nature, use often, (as the Grecians, νόμος) the word *Lex*; and more particularly, of the course and revolutions of sun and moon, and other particulars of heaven, *Qui lege moveri Sidera*, &c.

**V. 2. ***{Day unto day uttereth speech, and night, &c.}* The vicissitude, or continual succession of night and day, so constant, and so beneficial, and necessary, (there being no less necessity of the night in its kind, than of the day:) is a very considerable effect of the sun’s course, or conversion: the due consideration whereof might well incite a natural man to praise the author of so great a work, and benefit. And so it did, this in particular, among other things of that nature. *Tum vicissitudines dierum atque noctium*, &c. and *Quem vero astrorum ordines; quem dierum noctiumque vicissitudines*, &c. So Tully in his *Tusculans*; and in his *De Legibus*: not to mention others.

**V. 3. ***{There is no speech nor language, where their voice is not heard}* Heb. אין אמר ואין דברים בלי נשמע קולם. To keep then to the words of the original precisely, it would be; *No speech; and no* (or, *nor*) *words: their voice is not heard.* Or, (because the particle בלי may be translated either *non*, or, *sine*,) *without* (these to be understood) *their voice is heard*; as here in our English margin. The former, *non auditur vox eorum*, is embraced by Bucer. The only objection against it is, that the Psalmist would commit a great tautology, repeating the same thing three times. Well, if that be all, it might easily be answered. But because I like of the latter well enough, it shall not need. It is that (this latter I mean) which Junius and Tremellius in their translation; and since them, learned Grotius, in his late notes, commend unto us; the most genuine, and every way most probable, as I conceive.

The meaning then will be, that although the heavens cannot speak (properly) nor use any language: yet their voice is heard. *Their voice*; as Exod. 4.8. *And it shall come to pass, if they will not believe thee, neither hearken to the voice of the first sign; that they will believe the voice of the latter sign.* Neither is the expression less emphatical, than that of our Savior’s; *if these should hold their peace, the stones would immediately cry out*, Luke 19.40. or that of the Apostle’s, *being dead, yet speaketh*, Heb. 11.4. or this, that τὰ ἀόρατα τοῦ Θεοῦ, *the invisible things of God* (that is, the invisible God) καθορᾶται, *are seen*, or made visible, by his works, Rom. 1.20. A thing that might very well be insisted upon, and pressed by iteration of words, (without any fear of tautology) to tell men, whom God has endowed with speech and language, to that very end, to glorify him: of those things that have neither speech nor language in them, and yet cease not to glorify God, and to declare his power and greatness, yea and goodness, all the world over. This conceit (whatever others may think of it) pleased David so well, it seems, that not content with ordinary expression, he thought good to vary it by apt metaphors of *babes and sucklings*, as we understand him, Psal. 8.2. whereof see more there. These two places compared do much illustrate one another, and the right understanding of this, confirms much what had been said upon the other.

**V. 4. ***{Their line is gone out}* Some by *line*, understand their curious frame and structure, made as it were, *ad amussim* by line and rule, Zach. 1.16. *and a line shall be stretched over Jerusalem*; but rather Job 38.5. *Who hath laid the measures thereof, if thou knowest? or who hath stretched the line upon it?* being spoken of the Creation. But that which immediately follows, *and their words*, which according to the Scripture style, seems to be a further explication or prosecution of what went before, makes their interpretation more probable, who by קו (as Isa. 28.10. *precept upon precept, line upon line*) understand a line, or verse (as anciently they called it) of writing: and so the Psalmist makes the heavens to write, as well to speak: to have a pen, as well as a tongue. *Liber naturae*, and *liber caeli*, has been used by divers, to this purpose. And in very deed, the heavens (as is well observed by some here) are more like unto a book, which we may read, looking upon it with our eyes; than to a preacher, who calls for our ears. The word was by the Septuagint rendered in Greek φθόγγος, as if it had been קולם, or for some other reason. However, the difference is not great, and to the main purpose, it comes all to one. The Apostle therefore, Rom. 10.18. quoting this place of the Psalmist, contents himself with the Septuagint translation of the word, as writing to them, to whom it was best known, and ordinary.

But to what purpose these words of the Psalmist are there quoted by St. Paul, and how by him applied, will more concern us to know, because some mistaking the Apostle’s aim there, have also been led to a misunderstanding of this Psalm (so far as the sun and the effects of it are spoken of) as though all were to be understood allegorically, and no other thing had been intended by the Psalmist. Most think that the Apostle applies these words to the sound, or preaching of the Gospel; which indeed was even then gone very far; but hardly so far, as well to bear this application. But to let that pass, if the scope of the Apostle in that chapter, be well weighed, the coherence of the words, his several objections, and answers, and close inferences, all well observed, (a thing otherwise not so obvious and easy to everybody, because of the conciseness of his speech in this whole chapter) it will appear, that the Apostle had no other aim by this allegation, than to prove that God of old had revealed himself to other nations, (though not in the same manner, or degree) as well as to the Jews: and therefore that they had no reason to be now so extremely scandalized, or offended, that the Gospel of Christ should be preached unto them also. For further proof, let Calvin be read, both here upon this Psalm; and in his commentaries upon that Epistle: whose reasons are unanswerable: approved also (though *tacito nomine*) by Vatablus upon this Psalm.

*{in them hath he set}* In the heavens before spoken of, ver. 1. *The heavens*, &c. what follows of day and night, is no interruption of the first subject, because he mentions them only as effects of the sun’s revolution. How the sun is said to have a *tabernacle*, who is in perpetual motion (except a man should understand it of the Zodiac, which I do not think was David’s meaning) see upon the fifth verse, *out of his chamber*.

**V. 5. ***{Which as a Bridegroom}* Marriages among the Hebrews were performed with extraordinary public rejoicings, and great solemnities; as may be gathered from the parable of the ten Virgins, Mat. 25. and what by way of comparison is written of the *children of the bridegroom*, Mat. 9.15. and Joh. 3.29. See also Isa. 61.10. and 62.5. Among other rites and solemnities then in use, one was it seems, and is yet after a sort, (as Buxtorf, in his *Synagoga Jud.* tells us) that a fair tent, or canopy, was pitched abroad in the open air, (whether field, or garden) where the bride and bridegroom did meet; and there the bride being delivered to the bridegroom; upon their coming forth (not without the long expectation of them that were without) being set out with all the pomp and bravery that could be devised, there was wondrous joy and exultation. So does the sun when he rises in a morning, after the darkness of the night appears to us; very glorious to our eyes, and very welcome to most; whence proceeded that acclamation, used afterwards proverbially among the ancient Grecians, ἔξεχε φίλ’ ἥλιε: *Exorere Clare Sol*.

*{out of his chamber}* Heb. מחפתו. Some render it, *de umbraculo suo*; in reference to that custom we have spoken of, of a tent, or canopy pitched, &c. The word, of itself, may signify any covering, that is for a shelter, in general. But of a bride’s chamber (or tent) as here of a bridegroom’s, it is properly used Joel 2.16. and therefore by most is here rendered by *Thalamus*. Now this of the sun here, is not spoken according to astronomical exactness, no more than many other things in Scripture: but according to outward appearance, and vulgar judgments. Though therefore the motion or conversion of it (if astronomically considered) be equal and constant: yet to us, when he first appears, he seems then first to rise and come abroad, which presupposes a resting place; if not a new birth, as some heretofore were of opinion.

*{as a strong man to run a race}* The ancient Grecians, we know, had their λαμπαδηδρομίας, which were public exercises, and matches in running, performed among them with great solemnity. Now the custom was, that they that did run, bare a burning torch in their hand, from whence came the Greek word λαμπαδηφορία; and the Latin proverb, *lampada tradere*. If any such custom were among the Hebrews; (for the Grecians might have it from them) or in case this custom of the Grecians were but known to the Hebrews, as well as it was to the Latins, though not in use among them; there were no question to be made, but that these words did allude unto it. But we have no certainty of either, and therefore can conclude nothing. The Romans’ way of running (publicly and with solemnity) was most upon chariots, called *bigae*, or *quadrigae*: and they were said absolutely *currere*, to run, though carried. To which of these kinds of running, with the legs, or upon horses and chariots, as the sun is ordinarily represented upon a chariot: David here does allude, I know not: the allusion will be proper enough to either kind.

**V. 7. ***{The Law of the Lord, &c.}* What here follows in commendation of the Law, might all, or the greatest part, be interpreted with some reference or allusion to the sun spoken of before, and its properties: as when he says of it, that it is pure, that it enlightens, that it causes joy, &c. But I do not find that any interpreters go about it, and I will rather imitate their sobriety, than be an example unto any of too much subtlety. Of the coherence in general, see before upon verse first.

But besides, what is there said of it, which I account chiefest, having St. Paul himself for our warrant, as is showed upon the fourth verse; there is other coherence to be found, very plausible, and well insisted upon by some of the learnedest expositors. As first: Having commended the sun, and set out his glory: which, upon this very ground of its glorious appearance to the eyes of men, had been turned by most men into an object of idolatry, and was worshipped for a God: yea by not a few, as the only God: lest he might seem to say anything that might unadvisedly be drawn that way, either as allowing, or excusing that practice: he immediately, and abruptly (as to the words, and obvious construction) falls into commendation of the Law of God, by which, as all idolatry is strictly and frequently forbidden; so this particularly, the worshipping of the sun, and moon, and stars; grounded upon contemplation of their glorious luster and beauty: as in these words of Moses, in Deuter. *And lest thou lift up thine eyes into heaven, and when thou seest the Sun, and the Moon, and the Stars, even all the host of heaven, shouldst be driven to worship them, and serve them*, &c. Deut. 4.19. where also it is observable that a little before this caution concerning the sun, &c. Moses had remembered them of God’s mercy and particular favor towards them in giving them the Law; *Statutes and judgments*: as it is there, verse fourteenth. Besides, there may be ground of coherence in this, that as the sun is the light of our eyes, and the chief comfort of our natural lives, &c. we might gather from this opposition, or parallel of praises, that what the sun is to the body, the Law and word of God is to the soul: the only true light and life of it, without which it is in darkness, and destitute of life.

*{law}* Or, *doctrine*.

*{is perfect}* Though the creatures cannot serve to lead us to God, yet his word can, Psal. 29.9. & 119.96.

*{converting}* Or, *restoring*.

**V. 8. ***{pure}* Psal. 12.6.

*{enlightening the eyes}* See upon Psalm 13.3. *lighten mine eyes*.

**V. 9. ***{the judgments of the Lord are true}* Psal. 111.7.

*{true}* Heb. *truth*. So that all man’s inventions and intentions are lies.

*{righteous altogether}* Every one, without exception, and in all things, Psal. 119.128.

**V. 10. ***{More to be desired are they than gold}* Except God’s word be esteemed above all worldly things, it is contemned.

*{yea, than much fine gold}* Psal. 119.72,127. Prov. 8.19.

*{sweeter also than honey}* Psal. 119.103.

*{and the honeycomb}* Heb. *the dropping of the honeycombs*. That which comes forth without pressing, and is counted the sweetest and purest honey.

**V. 11. ***{great reward}* For God accepts our endeavor, though it be far imperfect.

*{reward}* Mat. 5.12. Gen. 15.1. Heb. 10.35.

**V. 12. ***{Who can understand his errors, &c.}* So much in commendation of the Law, that it is just, pure, perfect, (perfect in itself, and requiring perfection from those under it;) and the like; without any reflection at all upon his own infirmities, might argue some kind of presumption, in David. He therefore now in the conclusion, makes his confession to God, and at the same time craves his gracious assistance against sins of ignorance, in the 12. and sins of willfulness, or presumption, in the 13. verse: intimating also thereby the disability of the Law, how pure and perfect soever in itself, to bring man (in this state of natural corruption) to happiness; and the necessity of another Covenant; to wit, the Covenant of grace, in Jesus Christ: implicitly here included, or aimed at.

*{Who can understand his errors?}* Then there is no reward of duty, but of grace, Rom. 4.4. for where sin is, there death is the reward, Rom. 6.23. This interrogation has the force of an affirmation. *Who can?* no man; not the most perfect and innocent. So St. Paul of himself, 1 Cor. 4.4. *I know nothing by myself, yet am I not hereby justified.* And though all sins in general are sometimes called *errors*, Hebr. 9.7. and elsewhere: yet here, that sins of ignorance are particularly intended, appears by the opposition, v.13. *from presumptuous sins*. See Numb. 15.27. *through ignorance*, &c. and v.30. *presumptuously*, &c. which place it is very likely that the Psalmist had in his thoughts.

**V. 13. ***{Keep back thy servant}* Heb. *draw*. For we are naturally prone to great sins.

*{from presumptuous sins}* Which are done purposely, without fear of punishment, with an high hand, Num. 15.30.

*{let them not}* Or, *that they may not*.

*{dominion over me}* Psal. 119.133. Rom. 6.12,14.

*{The great transgression}* Great, in comparison of lesser sins, of weakness and infirmity; of which, Psal. 130.3. *If thou Lord, shouldst mark iniquities; O Lord, who shall stand?* And again, *Great transgression* because of the punishment, (alluding to Numb. 15. as is said before) which was, absolutely *to be cut off*; whereas sins of ignorance might be expiated by certain offerings.

**V. 14. ***{and the meditation of my heart}* That I may obey you in thought, word, and deed.

*{my strength}* Heb. *my rock*.

# PSALM XX

## TITLE

*{Of David}* Composed by David himself, as a Prophet, for a public form of prayer, to be used by the people for himself, their King. Hence it is, that he speaks of himself in the third person. The particular occasion, upon which, as is by most conceived, this Psalm was first penned, was David’s war against the Ammonites, and Syrians; of which we read 2 Sam. 10. & 1 Chron. 19. This conjecture is chiefly grounded on the 7. verse, *Some trust in chariots, and some in horses*: because particular mention is made in the history (if both places be looked upon and compared) of great number of chariots and horsemen, taken and slain in that war. As for that seeming contradiction, that is observed by most in that relation, in the number of the chariots and horsemen: we will not meddle with it here, as not so necessary to this place.

**Verse 1. ***{The name of the God}* That is, the God. See upon Psal. 9.10. *that know thy Name*.

*{of the God of Jacob}* As Psal. 46.7,11. & 81.1,4. and elsewhere. In which places either *Jacob* is put for Israel; and so *the God of Jacob*, is as much as of the Israelites: or, *Jacob* particularly, as, *the God of Abraham, and the fear of Isaac*, Gen. 31.42. But a more particular, and not improbable reason is given by some of this compellation of God in this place, by the name of *the God of Jacob*, because they are Jacob’s very words that are used here in the beginning, Gen. 35.3.

*{defend thee}* Heb. *set thee on an high place*. Set you in an high place, safe from your adversaries. See upon Psal. 18.33. *upon my high places*.

**V. 2. ***{thee help}* Heb. *thy help*.

*{strengthen thee}* Heb. *support thee*.

*{strengthen thee out of Zion}* For the battle, that you may overcome your foes. See Psal. 13.7. *out of Zion*.

**V. 3. ***{Remember}* Part of the meat-offering was to be burnt on the altar for a memorial, Lev. 2.2. that God might remember to do good to the offerer.

*{all thy offerings}* David before he went to war, offered sacrifices.

*{accept}* Heb. *turn to ashes, or, make fat*.

*{accept thy burnt sacrifice}* Burn to ashes by fire from heaven, as an extraordinary token of acceptation, Lev. 9.24. Judg. 6.21. 1 King. 18.38. 1 Chron. 21.26. 2 Chron. 7.1. But it is not likely, that David would require of God such an extraordinary sign, which being done without some special warrant from God himself, is a kind of tempting of God: neither is it likely, if any such thing had happened, but that it would have been recorded in the history. Although therefore the word properly signifies, *to turn into ashes*; (or, *to make fat*) yet because that miraculous *turning into ashes*, or consuming of the sacrifices by a fire from heaven, was a certain token of God’s acceptation; by an ordinary trope of the effect for the cause, it may signify no more here, than, *to accept*, in general; as it is well rendered in the Text.

This commemoration of David’s sacrifices, is not to this end, as though the oblation of such things was of itself pleasing unto God, or meritorious with him; as the idolatrous heathens did believe of their Gods, (though reproved for it by divers of their own philosophers:) and therefore made bold in their addresses unto them by prayer and supplication, to put them in mind of their cost and charges in their oblations, whereof we have an example in the very first of Homer his *Iliads*, in Chryses, Apollo’s priest, his prayer to Apollo: God himself does often profess to the contrary: as particularly, Psal. 50.8,9,10. &c. *I will not reprove thee*, &c. the drift and scope of this commemoration here is but this, to set out David his piety and fear of God, by those outward solemn performances, appointed then by the Law of God, and in reference to his commandment and institution, not to be omitted, but performed with all reverence and devotion. And because they did pray at the same time, David’s prayers which he made are also included in the mention of these sacrifices, as if he had said, *Remember thy prayers*, &c.

**V. 5. ***{We will rejoice in thy salvation}* Safety and deliverance granted to the King, in whose weal our felicity stands.

*{we will set up our banners}* In token of victory.

**V. 6. ***{Now know I, that the Lord saveth his anointed}* Most interpreters take these words as spoken by way of thanksgiving, after the victory obtained. If so, then was not this Psalm made at one time, nor these two parts so fitly joined into one. More likely therefore, that they are words of a more than ordinary confidence only, as though they had already obtained what they prayed for: which kind of confidence, if well grounded, is oftentimes very available, to the obtaining of requests: and there be examples of it elsewhere. But that these words were used by or intended for the priest alone, when he offered, as some here are of opinion, I know not upon what ground it can be said.

*{from his holy heaven}* Heb. *from the heaven of his holiness*.

*{with the saving strength of his right hand}* Heb. *by the strength of the salvation of his right hand*.

**V. 8. ***{They are brought down}* The words, according to the Hebrew idiom often using preterits for futures, and futures for preterits, (as upon this very place Vatablus,) may also be translated, *They shall be brought down and fall: but we shall rise and stand upright*. However, preterits of purpose here might be used for futures, to show their confidence; whereof see upon the sixth verse.

**V. 9. ***{Save Lord, let the King hear us}* All acknowledge, the Hebrew words might as well be translated; *Save Lord the King, hear us when we call*: but that there is a distinction in the Hebrew Text, after *Save Lord*. It is true, there is a distinction of a colon, which they call *atnach*; but if occasion were, we could show that no interpreters so precisely bind themselves to these distinctions, but that sometimes, if occasion be, they can dispense with themselves. But we will not except against this translation, since there is no need.

*{Let the King hear us}* Most understand, God, or the Messias. Calvin would have David himself (as any other King in the like case, that represents the person of God) to be meant. To which purpose he quotes Lam. 4.23. & Mic. 2.13. to which may be added, 1 Sam. 8.20.

# PSALM XXI

## TITLE

*{A Psalm of David}* There is no question to be made of it, though some upon weak grounds, would have it otherwise; but that David himself was the author. The reason why he speaks of himself in the third person is, because it was intended by him for a form of a solemn thanksgiving, as the former of a prayer, to be used publicly by the people; in which case the third person would sit better than the first.

**Verse 1. ***{Shall he rejoice}* The future is here put for the present, not excluding present joy, but the better to set out the greatness of the benefit; the memory whereof was to continue long, and with the memory, the joy: with the joy, the praise. Though present joys be stronger, perchance; yet in our remembrance, is our chiefest thankfulness; and to remember, in the Scripture, is as much sometimes, as to be thankful.

**V. 2. ***{his heart’s desire}* *The wicked boasteth of his heart’s desire*, Psal. 10.3. It is a judgment to him, that he has his will, that so encouraged by success, he may proceed in his iniquity, until he be ripe for utter destruction. But God, whom he favors, takes care of them, and prepares their hearts, that they should not desire anything hurtful to themselves, or displeasing to him. Or if he suffer them, (as in his infinite wisdom he knows best, how to order all things for the best, according to every man’s particular case, temper, and disposition,) yet he grants it not unto them. A man that shall carefully observe it, shall in the course of his life find occasion enough to thank God, as well for those things that God has not, as for those that he has granted. See also upon Psal. 57.2. *performeth all things*.

**V. 3. ***{a crown of pure gold on his head}* Some think this to have a particular reference to the story, 2 Sam. 12.30. *And he took their King’s crown off his head, (the weight whereof was a talent of gold, with the precious stones) and it was set on David’s head*, &c. But it is not likely that David, a King, would value so much the worth of that particular crown, so literally taken, though precious otherwise, as to make such solemn mention of it in this his public thanksgiving. As therefore a crown (in ordinary use) is taken metaphorically for a kingdom; so must a *crown of pure gold*, be here taken metaphorically, for a choice kingdom: such as was the kingdom of Israel, not only in respect of the land and people, and divers other prerogatives; but also as it comprehended divers little kingdoms, yet of good credit in those times; as we read more at large in the history of the Israelites’ first conquests, and of after-times.

**V. 4. ***{He asked life of thee}* I conceive (which is the opinion of some others also, though not so fully set out and explained by any I know) that there is an antithesis, not in the words of this verse only, but between this and the former verse also: by which antithesis the goodness of God, and the humility of David is more fully and movingly set out unto us, and expressed. Life, though the chiefest of temporal blessings in some respect, (as in point of price and valuation; for what would not a man give, or forgo, for life?) yet as it is the foundation of all others, always to be presupposed before any other can be asked or granted, (for what can any man enjoy in this world, if he have not life?) so it is the meanest and lowest of all others. This antithesis is used by God himself, in that answer he made to Baruch, by the mouth of Jeremy, Jer. 45.4,5. *Behold that which I have built, I will break down*, &c. *And seekest thou great things for thyself? seek them not: for behold I will bring evil upon all flesh, saith the Lord: but thy life will I give unto thee for a prey in all places whither thou goest.* Whereas therefore David in the midst of his many dangers and adversities, though he found God very gracious unto him, and might presume upon his favor as much as any; yet in great humility desired of God no more than bare life, as we say; that God would but let him live, &c. God in his great mercy, and favor towards him, did not only grant unto him that which he desired, the meanest of requests, life; but with it, the highest of temporal blessings, a crown, a kingdom; and that too, not an ordinary Crown, or kingdom; but a *crown of pure gold*; of which words more has been said upon the former verse.

*{length of days for ever and ever}* Though the Hebrew words עולם ועד here translated *for ever and ever*, do not so precisely import absolute eternity, but that they may, and are used to express the long continuance of several things in their kind, and so might here also be understood of a life stretched out beyond ordinary computation; yet because we neither find that the period of David’s life was so over-long above what is ordinary: nor that he had any direct promise from God by any revelation, or otherwise, that he should live so long as he did; it is more probable, that this is to be understood of his living, or surviving in his posterity, by a continued succession of Kings and princes that came out of his loins, to divers ages after himself. Plato, that so much admired philosopher among the heathens, says, that marriage is a remedy against mortality; as not accounting them altogether dead, that survived in their posterity. Since him, others have said it: yea and before him too. For Ocellus Lucanus, a Pythagorean philosopher of very great antiquity, has in effect the same. And this is it, according to best interpreters, that Christ himself aimed at, Luk. 21.35,36. where he gives the reason, why the blessed, or as it is there, *those that are accounted worthy of that world, and of the resurrection of the dead, neither marry, nor are given in marriage*: because they *cannot die any more*; as if he had said, that being now immortal, they stand in no further need of that help, or antidote against mortality.

However, because even that life that David lived in his royal posterity, to the last that sat upon his throne, was not of so long continuance, as will so well stand with so solemn a promise and prophecy, and because it is certain, that other like promises made unto David, of his posterity’s enduring as long as sun and moon, Psal. 89.29, &c. and the like, must of necessity be understood of Christ (the Son of David) his spiritual kingdom, truly and properly, both universal and eternal: I like well, that this also should more especially be referred unto Christ, either his eternal kingdom, or eternal life, by his resurrection from the dead: according to that of the Apostles, *Christ being raised from death, dieth no more*, &c. Rom. 6.9. and, *ever liveth*, Heb. 7.25. We may also very literally (which is the most generally received interpretation) understand it of the assurance David had from God of his salvation and eternal life; as in all probability he had. See also Psal. 61.6,7. *Thou wilt prolong*, &c.

**V. 6. ***{Thou hast made him most blessed for ever}* Heb. *blessings*. That is, most blessed in himself, and an instrument of blessing others, Gen. 12.2. Ezek. 34.26. Psal. 72.17.

*{made him most blessed}* Heb. *set him to be blessings*.

*{made him exceeding glad}* Heb. *gladded him with joy*.

**V. 8. ***{Thine hand shall find out all thine enemies}* Your hand, O King: for they had named him, ver. 7. and his enemies they mention, verse 11. and God’s assisting him to destroy them, ver. 9.

**V. 9. ***{them}* Or, *for them*.

*{the Lord shall swallow them up in his wrath}* This teaches us patiently to endure the cross, till God destroy the adversaries: whom he consumes as fuel in an oven, Mal. 4.1. Esay 26.11.

**V. 11. ***{For they intended evil against thee}* Your adversaries intended to destroy you, O King. See notes on ver. 8.

*{which they are not able to perform}* Heb. *He is not*. That is, their leader, who brought them to destroy David.

**V. 12. ***{shalt thou make them turn their back}* Or, *thou shalt set them as a butt*.

*{back}* Heb. *shoulder*.

*{make ready thine arrows upon thy strings}* Psal. 7.12,13.

**V. 13. ***{in thine own strength}* Show your power in upholding King and state, that we may praise you.

# PSALM XXII

## TITLE

*{Aijeleth Shahar}* There is not any title that admits of more variety of interpretations and conjectures; nor of more uncertainty, in all that variety. Bucer and Mollerus will afford most of them; whom it were an easy thing to English, if to as much purpose, as the labor would be easy. But when all is done, I shall only set down variety of fantasies; nothing that I can pitch upon with any probability. If anything, this seems to be most probable, that this was the beginning of some known song or canticle in those days, to the tune of which this also was appointed to be sung.

Though most Psalms, (in some parts and passages, at least,) have reference, more or less, and are applicable to Christ; yet this difference is observable, that whereas the most, literally belong unto David, or some other; and allegorically, or metaphorically to Christ: some there be, (in part at least, as we said before,) that are literally applicable to Christ; to him more, than to any other. Of which kind, this is; as may appear by the 8,16, & 17. verses; besides all in the end, from the 26. verse; which all men confess, more properly and literally to belong unto Christ and his kingdom, than to David, or any of his posterity, besides Christ. It will be therefore our task in such, to take notice especially of the main, and most proper scope: that is, to consider them, as principally intended of Christ.

**Verse 1. ***{My God, my God, why hast thou forsaken me?}* These words were uttered by Christ upon the Cross, and though not the very last, yet among the last that he spoke there, before he gave up the Ghost. They are very considerable words, in divers respects, as shall appear afterwards: though I allow not of that reason, that is given by some, who think the Evangelists, S. Matthew, and St. Mark, to have recorded them, not translated only, in that tongue they wrote in, to wit, the Greek; as is usual with them upon other like occasions; but in the very language and syllables they were uttered in by Christ; that is, the Syriac, a dialect of the Hebrew, then in common use among the Jews; of purpose, as they think, that we might take the more notice of them. Whereas the true reason is, because if these very words that Christ used, had not been recorded; none, but very skillful in the Hebrew, or Syriac, could have understood the ground of that bitter mock and derision of some then spectators, who because Christ had said *Eli, Eli*, (or, according to the Syriac pronunciation, *Eloi, Eloi*;) that is in English, *My God, my God*: hence took occasion to mock, (or rather indeed, to slander him; for it would have been a very odious thing in those days, to hear a dying man call upon any other than God:) as though he had called upon, or for Elias, Matth. 27.47.

But now to come to the sense of the words; they are words at which divers have been not a little scandalized, as either arguing some kind of despair, or at least much impatience: whence it is, that divers glosses and constructions have been devised, to turn the scope and sense of them another way, than in ordinary construction they seem to run. Some have been bold to say, that what Christ spoke, was but feignedly, not out of any real sense or meaning; not to express his own thoughts, but the better to conform himself to the common opinion. But this is too absurd, as to need any confutation. If there were any appearance of evil in these words, as it seems they conceive, that are so much offended at them; they might have considered, that we are to forbear, not only that which is evil in itself, but even that (where no indispensable duty does bind, nor consideration of a greater good does preponderate; neither of which can be said of these words, by them that are offended at them;) which has the appearance of evil.

But this conceit is not so absurd, but theirs (though not more true, perchance;) may be accounted as witty, who would have Christ to have repeated this whole Psalm, hanging upon the Cross, or a good part of it, and so to have uttered these words (which only are mentioned by the Evangelists, they say, because they are the first of the Psalm, as is usual in quotations;) no otherwise than by way of repetition or meditation. I would not say, what some do, that it is not likely Christ would repeat so long a Psalm at the Cross: I have read of divers Martyrs that have repeated the whole *Te Deum* at the stake, or going to it, which is not much less in length. But the words of the Evangelists, if looked upon, are a sufficient confutation of this opinion; who say not, (neither of them that record them;) that Christ spoke, or repeated; but, *cried with a loud voice*.

Many of the ancients have said, that Christ spoke these words, not in his own person, properly, but in the person of whole mankind. Which indeed is true enough, in some sense; but in that sense they mean, to free Christ in uttering of them, from all trouble of mind, or sense of grief, or fear, I cannot allow of it. They might as well say, because he suffered not for himself, but for mankind, therefore he felt no pain. Yet this is more tolerable by far, than to say, that the Deity of Christ, did withdraw itself at that time, so that there was a real separation; and from that separation, this confusion of the humanity left to itself. By these, and divers other expositions that we will pass by, does appear what men have thought of the literal sense of these words, which they have been so solicitous to avoid.

But now let us see what is said, or can be said to take away the offence, and yet to preserve the literal sense. That Christ, as man, (though God and man still, but the Divine nature by a special providence and dispensation withdrawing or suspending its influence for a while:) did suffer partly in his body, and partly in his soul, (but more in his soul, than body) more than can either be expressed by man, or imagined, I do not see how any reasonable man can question, that reads the story of his passion, from his bloody sweat, unto the end; and considers Christ his own expressions, recorded unto us to that very end, that we might know how much he has suffered for us. I will not say, that there was a necessity he should suffer so much, both in body and soul, to make his sufferings available to our redemption, both of our bodies, and of our souls. This I dare not say, because I have no warrant for it in Scripture; and bare human ratiocination in these things, is mere folly and madness: It is enough for me to know, that God had appointed it so, and in humility to adore beyond what I can understand. But that Christ did actually suffer very much, even beyond what can be conceived: I believe, as I believe the Scriptures.

If then his sufferings were extraordinary; why should not his expressions be so, by which we might understand (so far as can be conceived by man) the greatness of his pangs and agonies? In this case, such words as these, *My God, my God*, &c. were words of sense, not infidelity. Neither are such expressions inconsistent with a perfect security of God’s love and favor, at the same time. There may be a contradiction in the language of sense, and that of faith, if in themselves considered; but no such inconsistence in the nature of the things, from which that variety does flow, but that they may meet in one. Does not Ecclesiastes in the same clause, and with the same breath say, *Though a sinner do evil, and his days be prolonged: yet I know he shall not prolong his days*, Eccl. 8.12. and the Prophet Habakkuk, of his vision, *though it tarry, it will not tarry?* (Hab. 2.3.) And even Plato, a heathen philosopher, among them that were led by sense and reason only, of greatest account, in one of his dialogues called *Phaedon*, towards the end of it, tells us that at the same time a man may believe, and not believe? And why not a man in such an extremity expostulate with God that he has forsaken him; though his heart and faith tell him at the same time, that God has not, will not, forsake him?

And indeed, if we mark it, (it is observed by most interpreters) the very words imply as much, and interpret themselves. For at the same time that he says, God has forsaken him, which his sense, and the violence of his pangs expressed from him, he calls him his God, (*my God, my God*:) an argument of his faith: to which agrees well that of David in another place, *But I trusted in thee, O Lord: I said, Thou art my God*, Psal. 31.14. And if this be not satisfactory, consider we the words, first as spoken by David; and there we find in the same Psalm, (verse 24.) *For he hath not despised, nor abhorred the affliction of the afflicted, neither hath he hid his face from him, but when he cried unto him he heard.* Again, consider them (which is our chiefest business here) as spoken by Christ, (though it may be supposed, that he spoke them in no other sense than David did:) and you shall hear him soon after, (as in Saint Luke we find) saying, *Father, into thine hands I commend my spirit*, Luk. 23.46. Why then should any offence be taken at the words?

But because Christ was no ordinary man, and that to bear any pain or grief, without any sign of commotion, has been thought by many to belong to perfect magnanimity, and height of spirit; besides what has already been said, that what Christ did suffer, was quite of another kind, so far above the measure of ordinary sufferings, as himself was above ordinary men; we further briefly say, that Christ was pleased to yield to sense (or feeling:) so far, 1. That he might show himself a perfect true man; (a thing not easily believed, as appears by the multitude of heresies about his nature, that sprang up soon after the first plantation of the Christian faith,) there being no greater evidence, to ordinary judgment, at least, of his perfect humanity, than his being subject to the common infirmities of man, sin only excepted. 2. For our sakes, to keep us from utter fainting, and despair in our greatest trials, combats, and afflictions, whether spiritual, or corporal, when God seems to forget us, withdrawing the comforts of his grace, and the benefit of his help, as sometimes he does, even from the best, and his best beloved for a while; that then remembering what Christ himself, so near and dear unto him, was put to, we might have comfort.

And although this of Christ’s sufferings were an extraordinary case, not liable to the rules of ordinary human ratiocination; yet for them that think a Stoical *apathy*, or unpassionateness, to be the greatest evidence, or character of true magnanimity, I shall commend unto them the disputes of two famous philosophers, recorded by Aulus Gellius, a known ancient writer, in the 19. book of his *Noctes Atticae*, (so called) cap. 1. & 12. There be that translate these words, למה עזבתני not, *quare dereliquisti*: *Why hast thou forsaken me?* but, *quousque deseres me*: *how long wilt thou forsake me?* which the Hebrew will bear well enough. And perchance this, though neither can justly, would less offend, because, *Why hast thou?* sounds more like an absolute desertion: *how long?* has, or may seem to have in it an intimation of a confidence, that it will be but for a time.

*{from helping me}* Heb. *from my salvation*.

*{from the words of my roarings}* Being tormented with extreme anguish, Psal. 32.3.

**V. 2. ***{am not silent}* Heb. *there is no silence to me*.

**V. 3. ***{But thou art holy}* Some think that by these words God’s eternity and immutability, is here opposed to man’s short continuance, and perpetual mutability, which makes his condition, if well weighed, so inconsiderable, (take it at the best) in this world. Which opinion is not without some ground; for so we find it, Psal. 90. and elsewhere. However, their opinion likes me better, who by *holy*, here understand gracious, and propitious: in which sense we know the Latin word *pius*, is often ascribed to God, by ancient Christian writers. The ground of this signification might be, from the place where the Ark, and propitiatory, or Mercy-seat, (the covering of the Ark) stood, which was called the *holy of holies*. This being the place from whence God gave answers, and towards which men turned themselves, that made their prayers to God, he was commonly said to inhabit there, and to hear the prayers of men, and grant their request from thence.

*{O thou that inhabitest the praises of Israel}* Some render it, *But thou continuest holy, the praises*, (or, *O thou that art the praises*) *of Israel*. But, following the most approved translation, *O thou that inhabitest the praises of Israel*: that is, thou that dwellest in the place, (the Temple, in general; or more particularly, the Ark:) where all the people of Israel are perpetually putting up their petitions unto thee, according to their several wants and necessities; and praising thee for thy mercy towards them, in granting them their requests; according to that, Psal. 50.14,15. *Offer unto God thanksgiving, and pay thy vows unto the most High: And call upon me in the day of trouble and thou shalt praise me*, as in the former English; or, *glorify me*, as in the last translation. By this the Psalmist does very pathetically exaggerate his own pitiful condition, that whereas God was so easy to be found by others; and so ready to grant them their requests, for which praises were daily, by all manner of people, returned unto him: he only and his prayers, were excluded from the benefit of his presence; he only, not regarded: as more plainly follows in the next verses.

*{Our fathers trusted in thee}* He does modestly insinuate, that God was in some manner bound to hear, because there was the same reason for him, now; as there was heretofore for his forefathers. They *trusted*, they *cried*, so does he. And he insists upon this as a pressing argument: or motive: making also profession of his faith and constancy, that although he did not in his own particular (as yet at least) find it so: yet he did not for all that, (which some weaker professors in that case would be apt enough to do) doubt of the truth of those deliverances in former times. Gideon might seem to aim at some such thing, *and where be all his miracles which our fathers told us of, saying, Did not the Lord*, &c. Judg. 6.13. I believe he meant no otherwise than as David here, though his words are not so full. Now as David by mentioning, with reiteration, what God had done in former times, professes his faith and constancy; so, rather than he will seem to charge God with any partiality, or inconstancy, he seems (obscurely though; for his words may have another aim too, by setting the despicableness of his present condition among men, the more powerfully to provoke God’s compassion to his release, as Jerem. 30.17. *For I will restore health, because they called thee an outcast*, &c.) to answer for God, in the 4. verse, *But I am a worm*, &c. intimating thereby, that he had not the same reason, to expect that God should do for him, as he had done for them, because of the inconsiderableness of his person, in comparison of them, &c.

**V. 5. ***{and were not confounded}* Heb. *ashamed*; to wit, as men frustrated of their hope, Rom. 5.5. & 9.33. but triumphed over all calamities.

**V. 6. ***{But I am a worm}* A despicable creature trampled under foot, Job 25.6. Esay 41.14. Thus Christ also was abased for us, which shows God’s infinite love to us. See more upon verse 4. *Our fathers*, &c.

**V. 7. ***{All they that see me, laugh me to scorn}* Matth. 27.39.

*{shoot}* Heb. *open*.

*{they shake the head}* In sign of scorn, Job 16.4. 2 King. 19.21.

**V. 8. ***{He trusted on the Lord, &c.}* Mat. 27.43.

*{He trusted on the Lord}* Heb. *He rolled himself on the Lord*; as Psal. 37.5. See upon Psal. 114.7. *Tremble thou earth*.

*{let him deliver him, seeing he delighteth in him}* See before Psal. 18.19. *he delivered me, because he delighted in me.* The Hebrew word חפץ signifies *to will*; and also, *to delight*, or, *take pleasure* in a thing. The Septuagint desirous (as in many other places) to keep to the Hebrew as close as they could, rendered it by the Greek θέλει, intending thereby doubtless the same sense, to delight, or take pleasure; though the Greek word of itself, according to the propriety of the Greek tongue, signifies, *to will*, only. It is well known that there are infinite Greek words, used by the Greek translators, in another sense, or notion, than that which is proper unto them: which as they are used by them, are commonly called *hellenistical* words. Now the Evangelists we know, used for the most part, the translation of the Septuagint, as that which was then commonly received among the Jews; and which they were so used unto, that their Greek often has more of the Hebraism, than it has of the Greek, that was accounted true Greek in those days. These things considered Mat. 27.43. where this passage of the Psalmist is used, εἰ θέλει αὐτόν, should not have been translated, *if he will have him*, which does much obscure both the sense, in itself, and the reference the words have unto this place: but, as it is here, *seeing he delighted in him*; or, as it is in the margin, *if he delight in him*.

**V. 9. ***{But thou art he}* This alternation of expressions, some tending as it were to despair; others to hope and confidence; often used in this Psalm; does set out very lively the Psalmist his conflict (and in him, every godly man’s in the like case:) between sense and faith: and makes the whole Psalm, by this mixture of several passions and affections, the more pathetical, and poetical too.

*{that took me out of the womb, &c.}* It is generally believed, that David’s aim here is, to comfort and encourage himself by remembering the wonderful power and providence of God toward infants, even while they are shut up in the womb; and afterwards, in bringing them forth into the world; in their nursing, and preservation, while so tender and helpless of themselves: in all which it cannot be denied, but the power and providence of God does singularly appear in divers particulars, as any man in some measure, if he be not very stupid, may understand; and is acknowledged by most accurate anatomists and physicians, as well heathens, as others. The very opening and shutting again of the body, when the child is to be born, and to come forth into the world, is a thing so incomprehensible to naturalists, that they acknowledge (some of them at least) the immediate hand and power of God in it; because they see not by what power of nature (though that also be the power of God, the author of nature:) it is brought to pass. And there be many questions proposed about it, which no man can resolve, or scarce any does take upon himself to resolve, as to give any satisfaction.

Nevertheless, this being so general a providence, not extending to men, or mankind only, (though more particularly belonging to them, because they only are capable by nature to understand it, and to praise God for it:) but even to brutes, (more or less,) of all sorts; it seems more probable, that David had a more particular aim in these words: as either to remember some particular providence of God, towards him, even in the womb yet; or since that, in his infancy; in some extraordinary deliverance, or the like; as it has happened, and does daily unto many: or that he intended to say, (which he might have good reason to believe, and must needs, if he did believe it, breed great confidence,) that God *even from the womb had separated him*; that is, among many others, by a particular, or special providence, had culled him out, and designed him to some particular work, or ministry, and therefore had, and would have, in reference to that end and first election, a more particular care of him, and everything that happened unto him. It is acknowledged by the learned, that these words *from the womb*, were commonly used among the Hebrews proverbially, and hyperbolically; that is, by way of stronger expression; as Psal. 58.3,4. *estranged from the womb*, and Isai. 48.8. *called a transgressor from the womb*: and so of a man called by God to some extraordinary work, or otherwise eminent above the common condition of men, it was said, *That God had separated him from the womb*; as the Apostle speaks of himself, Gal. 1.14.

Thus David might speak of himself: but if the words be referred to Christ, to whom we have already said, that this Psalm does more properly belong, we need not go to metaphors, and figures for application; the bare letter and history will go far beyond the hyperbole (if any there be in it) of this expression. I need not insist upon particulars, because none, I hope, reads this, to whom the history of Christ is not known. It shall serve in general to say, that from his first conception, to the last hour and moment of his conversation among men, nothing did happen unto him, but either was altogether miraculous, or apparently carried by a special Divine providence and dispensation. There be prophecies to that purpose too, many, besides the relations of the Evangelists: but it needs no further proof.

*{Thou didst make me hope}* *Hope* is sometimes taken for life, in the Scripture, as Job 14.19. *thou destroyest the hope of man.* Or, the words may be taken (and so by most expounded) figuratively, as Job 38. last verse, (nor Psal. 147.9. to which most refer, for there is nothing there in the Original text, of the Ravens, but what is literally true: to wit, that they *cry*) *Who provideth for the ravens food, when his young ones cry unto God*: and Psal. 104.21. of young lions, that *they seek their meat from God*; and verse 27. that *they wait upon God.* The words may also be translated, (as is here in our English margin) *thou didst keep me in safety*, or, *security.* So Prov. 10.9. הולך בתום ילך בטח (there is a sweet allusion in the Original words, which is lost in the translation, *He that walketh uprightly, walketh surely*; or, *securely*; that is, shall be safe.

*{didst make me hope}* Or, *kept me in safety*.

**V. 11. ***{Be not far from me}* See upon Psal. 10.1. *Why standest thou so far off, O Lord?*

*{for there is none to help}* Unless you help me.

*{none to help}* Heb. *not a helper*.

**V. 12. ***{strong Bulls of Bashan}* The breed of Bashan, Deut. 32.14. of fat beasts, or cattle; because of the fertility of the place, very fit for pasture, Num. 32.1, &c. He means that his enemies were so fat, proud and cruel, that they were rather beasts than men. Psal. 17.10.

**V. 13. ***{They gaped upon me with their mouths}* Not in scorn, as elsewhere, but to devour me.

*{gaped upon me with their mouths}* Heb. *opened their mouths against me*.

**V. 14. ***{I am poured out like water}* All my bodily strength is gone.

*{out of joint}* Or, *sundered*.

*{my heart is like wax}* My heart is faint, Job 23.16.

**V. 15. ***{my tongue cleaveth to my jaws}* For thirst, Lam. 4.4. Joh. 19.28.

*{thou hast brought me into the dust of death}* I am without all hope of life.

**V. 16. ***{they pierced my hands and my feet}* Much ado there is about these words, as between Jews and Christians; in general; so between Christians among themselves Papists and Protestants. That the ground of the quarrel (of no great consequence indeed) on both sides may be understood, thus it is, כרו *caru* in Hebrew, signifies, *foderunt, they have pierced*: כארי *caari, sicut leo*; *as a lion*; the כ in the first, being a radical letter: in the second, a servile prefix; as Hebrew grammarians are wont to speak. Now our Hebrew Bibles, all that ever were printed, (one or two editions, as I take it, excepted) bear, not כרו *foderunt*, which is our translation, and is of all Christians, generally; but כארי *sicut Leo.* This therefore the Jews lay to our charge, as a corruption of the Bible. But we answer, that though all Hebrew copies, of late ages, have it so indeed, as it is commonly printed: yet some ancient copies, as by their own testimony is proved, had it in כארו (with a *Vau*) not כארי (with a *Jod*,) as it is now. כרו and כארו is all one in sense; all the difference is but of one vacant *Aleph*, which in many places of Scripture besides this, is added to words, without any alteration of sense at all; as the Jews themselves, must, and do acknowledge. So that if there be any wilful corruption, it must lie upon their score, and not ours, who translate according to the reading of the most ancient copies; followed also, and expressed by the Greek translation, called the Septuagint; a translation of great credit and antiquity: as used and received among the Jews before Christ’s time. They render it, ὤρυξαν; that is, *foderunt, they have pierced.*

Of this the Papists (some of them, not all: Genebrard upon this place, says plainly, *Nec tamen propterea locum corruptum dixerim, sed variae esse lectionis*; that is, Yet I would not say, that the place is corrupted, but of different reading only) take hold against us Protestants, to prove that the Hebrew Text has been corrupted. To them we answer; First, that there is no direct corruption of the Text, as long as the true reading may, by good authority of ancient copies, and the testimony of the Jews themselves and their Masoreths, whose great work and task has been to preserve the integrity of it, be asserted and vindicated. Secondly, neither does it appear, that any such corruption was ever intended by the Jews, in general; or that the alteration that has happened, did at first proceed from any such intention or purpose, either of Jew, or any other. Some learned men have thought that the ascititious (or א *Aleph*) might be the occasion; at which, as they conceive, some ignorant scribe (because not ordinary) stumbling, and not knowing what to make of the word, to make some sense, changed ignorantly (thinking it should have been so) the final ו (or *Vau*) into a final י (or *Jod*:) and so the error from one copy to another, as usually in such cases, was propagated.

This, as it is here proposed, is probable enough: but all things well considered that is said concerning this matter, by either Jews or others, (all which to set down here at large, I doubt would be too tedious and impertinent) well weighed and considered, I think their opinion not less, if not more probable, who think that כארי, as the Jews, and our ordinary Bibles read it, to be the true and genuine reading; but not in the sense that the Jews of these days would have it: but כארי for כרו or כארו; so that the sense shall be the same still, (*foderunt, they have pierced*) though the writing be somewhat different. Now that there should be a *Jod* at the end, instead of a *Vau*, it is an anomaly indeed, but not without example. So נשאי for נשאו, Ezra 10. last verse, and elsewhere in many places, which some have gathered together.5 But however we read the word, of the sense there can be no question at all, as out of Jewish writings of best account in this kind, as I have already said, easily to be proved and asserted. So that here we have a clear testimony for the cross of Christ, which the more offensive it has always been, both to Jews and Gentiles, (as indeed what can be more repugnant to sense, than a crucified God, the most vile and ignominious of all punishments) the more we ought to make of such testimonies: the best (though not only) and surest evidence (as Saint Peter tells us, 2 Pet. 1.19.) of our Christian faith.

Piercing of hands and feet, was one of the chiefest circumstances of crucifixion. Now to David, the words can have no application, but metaphorical: and so too, we shall be put to it, to show how, I know not any expositor that does. But thus it may be perchance: *Fodere*, may be put, (in this metaphorical sense) for *pungere*; in Latin it is often, I am sure. Now the hands and feet being, (according to the custom of those times and places, where most went barefooted) the naked places of the bodies, most exposed to the injuries of thorns and briars; *to pierce one’s hands and feet*, might be a proverbial kind of speech, taken from the similitude of thorns, and other like annoyances of those bare parts, to express any other smart and sticking vexation, whether of body or mind.

**V. 17. ***{I may tell all my bones}* Because of leanness.

*{they look and stare upon me}* They behold my pain with pleasure, Psal. 35.21.

**V. 18. ***{They part my garments}* Luke 23.34. Joh. 19.24. Of David it must be understood metaphorically, as if he said, They divide my goods, whether lands or others; movable, or immovable, among themselves. They make a prey of all I have. The Latins also say, *spoliare*; whereas *spolium*, properly and originally, signifies a garment.

**V. 20. ***{my darling}* Heb. *my only one*. That is, dearest to me, for I have but one soul, Psal. 35.17.

*{from the power}* Heb. *from his hand*, Dan. 6.27.

**V. 21. ***{Save me from the lion’s mouth}* 2 Tim. 4.17. Christ is delivered with a more mighty deliverance, by overcoming death, than if he had not tasted death at all.

*{for thou hast heard me from the horns of the unicorns}* In the Hebrew, ומקרני רמים עניתני by all, that I have seen, but this one English, translated, *and from the horns of the unicorn hear me*, or *answer me*: by some, *hearing me*; and *thou hast answered me*. But the first is most ordinary. Which to make good first, the Hebrew preterit עניתני by a double *enallage*, as they call it, must be put for the future; and then the future for an imperative; which is not ordinary. I think therefore, that this translation here ought to be preferred, as more literal, according to the words; (for as for the *ve*, or *u*. in the beginning, commonly rendered *and*, to be taken for the illative, or causal, *for*, is ordinary enough) so to the sense, not inferior. So David himself elsewhere, *The Lord that delivered me out of the paw of the Lion, and out of the paw of the Bear, he will deliver me out of the hands of this Philistine*, 1 Sam. 17.37. So also, Psal. 56.13. *For thou hast delivered my soul*, &c. and what is there noted. And Psal. 63.6,7. *When I remember upon my bed*, &c. And Psal. 85.1,2,3, &c. It is the same argument, and it fits here very well in this Psalm, where David, as has been said, interchangeably complains, and comforts himself, as present sense on the one side, and a lively faith, on the other, did prompt him.

As for the Unicorn here mentioned in the translation, certain it is that the Hebrew word ראם is the name of a wild beast, approaching to the nature of a Rhinoceros; but different from it, as most conceive; described, Job 39.9, &c. But what creature it should be, is not certainly known. Mention indeed is made by Pliny, and some other ancients of *Monoceros*, (that is, unicorn) which is commonly taken for the ראם, not here only, but often mentioned in Scripture. And yet whether there be any such creature indeed, as that which we commonly call the Unicorn, with that shape, and those properties, as are commonly ascribed unto it, is much doubted, and by not a few, both travelers and others, decried. The Unicorn’s horn also, so much in esteem, is by many thought a mere mistake, or imposture.
