It may suffice us to know, leaving the further search of this to naturalists, that ראם, undoubtedly is the name, (by what name soever it be, or may be called in English, or Latin) of a wild beast, very fierce and furious, and of great strength; an apt emblem of unjust, violent, tyrannical men or princes.

**V. 22. ***{I will declare thy Name unto my brethren}* Heb. 2.12. Here at this verse begins the second part of the Psalm, all that follows afterwards to the end of the Psalm, containing matter of thanks and praises for deliverances. Now, if as is generally believed, David made this Psalm in the time of his distress, then it must be conceived that he wrote this part by a prophetical Spirit foreseeing the issue, which we know he does in some other Psalms. However, the opinion of others is not less probable, that this Psalm was composed by David after his deliverance. It is true, he does set out his condition, which makes the greatest part of the Psalm, so amply and so pathetically, that a man would not think he could write so, that was now out of all sense of danger, or pain: and in present possession and enjoyment of a blessed estate. But this might be done of him purposely, as for some other causes, so particularly, to stir himself up to a more vigorous and full apprehension of God’s mercy, in delivering of him from such and such woes, that gave him occasion thus and thus to bemoan himself. However, supposing that the writing of this Psalm, were after David was delivered, and at ease; yet it is to be believed, that what he relates here of his thoughts, or words, is truly and punctually related as his very thoughts and words, in the time of his sufferance. And it were well, if those that have had any experience of woes and sorrows, did often in time of their ease and prosperity, call to mind, what their thoughts, and purposes were, when in affliction. It was the judgment of a learned Heathen, that men needed no other teaching, or philosophy (that is his word) than affliction itself, and the like: but so, *Si tales esse sani perseveramus, quales nos futuros profitemur infirmi*, that is, if we practice when we are in health, and prosperity, what we do purpose and resolve, when we are in misery. See upon Psal. 18.4. *The sorrows*, &c.

**V. 23. ***{Ye that fear the Lord, praise him}* He exhorts the Church by his example to praise God.

**V. 24. ***{nor abhorred the affliction of the afflicted}* The poor afflicted are comforted by the example of David, and Christ.

*{he cried unto him}* Heb. 5.7.

**V. 25. ***{My praise shall be of thee in the great congregation}* Rather, *from thee*: Though now I be afflicted, yet you will give me cause to praise you openly, by a famous deliverance. But see upon verse 22. *I will declare.*

*{of thee}* Or, *from thee*.

*{the great congregation}* Psal. 35.18.

*{I will pay my vows before them that fear him}* Psal. 116.14,18. Which were sacrifices of thanksgiving, which they offered by God’s commandment, when they were delivered out of any great danger, Psal. 66.13,14. & 116.18. Isa. 19.21.

**V. 26. ***{The meek shall eat and be satisfied}* Most translate it, *the poor shall eat*, &c. The Hebrew word will bear either. It was the fashion in those days, (not among the Jews only, but Gentiles also) that when they did sacrifice, they did feast also: (from whence arose that controversy in the Apostles’ time, so largely handled and agitated by St. Paul in his Epistle to the Corinthians, about things offered to idols: hence also it is, that this word *feast*, which properly signifies a holy day, a day of oblations, and other religious ceremonies, came to signify a solemnity of eating together:) and the greater was the sacrifice, and the number of beasts offered, as upon occasion of any vow, victory, or the like; the greater was also the solemnity of feasting and eating. Now because David in the former verse speaks of vows to be paid; it is generally conceived by all interpreters, (as many as insist upon the literal sense: for some pass it by, and take no notice of it at all;) I have seen, nor any excepted, that David does here promise great relief to the poor, to be bid by him, and plentifully fed at his solemn offerings. And this being the literal sense, it is by the same interpreters mystically and spiritually expounded of the calling of the poor and contemptible (for such were they, for the most part, that were first called) by the preaching of the Gospel; and particularly, of the sacramental receiving of the blessed body and blood of our Lord and Savior, by which our souls (if received with Faith, and other due conditions and circumstances) are feasted and nourished unto eternal life.

This interpretation, (the literal I mean, of the poor to be fed and feasted) though I do not, were it but for the number and authority of them that follow it, altogether reject; yet it does not seem to me very probable, that David in such a Psalm would make so solemn a commemoration under such general terms, of a thing not to last above one day: or if two perchance, yet the utmost that can be made of it, is but one good meal, or two. And then granting that this, even this relieving of the poor for one good meal or two, were a thing so considerable, (as in very deed, the feeding and relieving of the poor, and the like deeds of charity, in general, we acknowledge to be a main point of Religion, Luk. 11.41. Jam. 1. last verse, *Pure Religion*, &c.) as to be so solemnly mentioned: yet how comes the rich also to be spoken of, as follows in the 29. verse, *All they that be fat upon earth*, &c. is it likely that David would make such a matter of it, and in such general terms? But if not this; what then shall the literal (for of the spiritual, there will be no controversy) sense be?

Here I would have remembered what was said at the beginning of this Psalm, that there be divers passages, which literally cannot be expounded of David, but figuratively only; but of Christ, literally. The most plausible and literal application to David that I can fancy, is, that piety and godliness (as in a good measure it was:) shall be established in his days; and with piety, plenty, and security: all his kingdom over. By *eating*, and being *satisfied*, an easy life, may be understood; as by eating and drinking, Jer. 22.15. whereof see before, upon Psal. 14.4. *who eat my people*, &c. *To eat and rejoice*, (where, by *eating*, that drinking also is comprehended, appears by the context:) was a command of the Law, and an expression of great temporal happiness, Deut. 14.26. and elsewhere. Which makes the Psalmist to insist upon it the longer; That the *poor*, (or, *meek*,) *shall eat*; here in this verse: and the rich, (or, *those shall be fat*;) *shall eat*; but with a limitation in both places, (else it might seem to relish of Epicurism;) that so eating, *they shall praise the Lord*; in this: *they shall worship*, in the 29. verse; that is in general, that both rich and poor, great and small, shall live at ease, and want nothing; as it is, Psal. 34.10. *The young Lions* (to which *the meek*, are here opposed) *do lack, and suffer hunger; but they that seek the Lord, shall not want any good thing.*

This I conceive to be the sense of the words, in reference to David. To Christ and the Gospel by him preached the application is easy. Of the generality of men, of all sorts, professions, and conditions, (here included by rich and poor, and they that go down to the dust, &c. of which more there,) to be called by him, the Prophets are full, to which agree the relations, and testimonies of fact, in the Gospel. Of the poor particularly, because twice here mentioned, in this, and the 29. verse; the words of the Prophet Esay are very considerable, and fit to be compared with these, Esay 55.1,2. *Ho, every one that thirsteth, come ye to the waters, and he that hath no money; come ye, buy and eat; yea come buy wine and milk without money and without price. 2. Wherefore do ye spend money for that which is not bread? and your labour for that which satisfieth not? hearken diligently unto me, and eat ye that which is good, and let your soul delight itself in fatness.* Of the rich, see a notable prophecy, Esay 49.23. *And Kings shall be thy nursing fathers, and their Queens*, &c. Of the first Disciples of Christ, in general, (not without some reference to this very place, perchance:) it is recorded, Act. 2.46,47. that *they did eat their meat with gladness and singleness of heart; praising God, and having favour with all the people.*

Now if we will go higher, and expound the words, (which some of our most approved interpreters pitch upon as the only sense;) of that sublime Sacrament of Christ his blessed body and blood, by that worthy participation whereof man (whether rich, or poor: the rich having no prerogative above the poor in this inestimable benefit;) is elevated to the highest degree of honor and glory, that ever was enjoyed by mortal man in this world: the words will bear it very well, and no other sense can fit the place better. Nevertheless, the words may also be taken more generally of all spiritual gifts and graces; in the Gospel also, Joh. 6. figured unto us, by *eating the flesh, and drinking the blood of the Son of man*, verse 53, &c. Of which, the words of the Prophet Esay 25.6. are by most understood, *And in this Mountain shall the Lord of hosts make unto all people a feast of fat things*, &c.

**V. 27. ***{All the ends of the world shall remember and turn unto the Lord}* Psal. 2.8. & 72.11. & 86.9.

**V. 29. ***{All they that be fat upon earth, shall eat and worship}* That is, rich men, Deut. 31.20. See before upon verse 26. *The meek*, &c.

*{all they that go down to the dust shall bow before him, and none can keep alive his own soul}* The most literal translation of the last words, which seem to be most obscure, is, *& qui animam suam non vivificat*; (or, *vivificavit*,) that is, *and he that quickeneth not*, (or, *hath not quickened*) *his own soul*: and that most followed interpretation both of these words, and those before, *that go down to the dust*; is, to understand by this description, poor wretched people, such as (whether for want of necessary nourishment, or through any other grief and misery;) are not able to hold out, or to keep life any longer, but are at the very point of death: said also proverbially, *to go down to the dust*; or, *to sit in the dust*, Psal. 113.7, &c. And though there will be no great inconvenience in it, if we make the Psalmist to say the same thing twice, because we had the same already in 26. verse according to most translations, as was there said: yet it may be so much the better perchance, if we keep the English there as we find it, (so Junius has it too:) *the meek*; that so what was there delivered in general, (for by *the meek*, all that have the knowledge and fear of God, are included:) may here more particularly be expressed, both rich and poor; be they never so rich, or so poor. This is the common interpretation, which I cannot except against. Yet because it is an obscure passage, (as to those last words I mean:) I will not pass by (some others I do, because too violent:) two other expositions, neither of them much improbable, or inconsistent with either words, or scope.

By *them that go down to the dust, that have not kept their souls alive*, some understand plainly and literally, those that be dead: and conceive that the same is here meant, as is (with some allusion to this very place, some think:) expressed by the Apostle; in these words; *That at the Name of Jesus every knee should bow, of things in Heaven, and things in Earth, and things under the Earth*, and by the author of the Apocalypse, in these; *And every creature which is in Heaven, and on the Earth, and under the Earth, &c.* Rev. 5.13. The second is, *qui animam suam in vita non conservaverit*: (so are the words translated by Junius, and in him it is that I find this other exposition:) that is, *Qui ultro flagranti studio gloriae Dei, abjecerit vitam suam, &c.* He that out of an honest zeal to God’s glory shall cast away (or neglect) his own life, and renounce unto himself for his service: the best argument or evidence of a free voluntary people.

*{bow before him}* Phil. 2.10.

*{and none can keep}* Or, *and he that cannot keep*: or, *though he cannot keep*.

**V. 30. ***{A seed shall serve him}* That we might not think, that all that has been said, should be the happiness of one age only; by these words and those that follow, a perpetuity as in those things which from age to age by generation are propagated, is promised unto the Church. Some translate it, *Semen eorum*: *their seed*; which comes all to one.

*{it shall be accounted unto the Lord for a generation}* The Hebrew דור signifies either age, or generation; which diversity of signification has produced a different translation. For some render it, *accensebitur Domino in quaque aetate*, that is, *It shall be accounted the Lord’s in every age.* But it makes no variety of sense, as to the main scope, the perpetuity of the Church.

**V. 31. ***{and shall declare his righteousness}* That is, his goodness and mercy; as the word is often taken both in the Old and New Testament: whence it is that as the צדקה *righteousness*, or *justice* properly, is often translated by the Septuagint (in the) ἐλεημοσύνη: (that alms, charity, mercy:) so is the word חסד *mercy*, properly, as often rendered, by them δικαιοσύνη. So Matth. 1.19. of Joseph, δίκαιος ὢν might better be rendered, *being a merciful man*: or *because he was a merciful good man*. For as a *just* man, believing her as he did, he might and ought by the law, have dealt with her otherwise. See also upon Psal. 71.2. *righteousness.* However, if we take *righteousness* here (as some do) for the righteousness of God, whereby we are justified before him; or for that measure of righteousness and sanctification, wherewith, by the operation of God’s Spirit, we are endowed in this life, wholly to be ascribed unto him, as his gift and grace: so we shall not need that former interpretation, but keep the word in its more proper and usual notion.

*{unto a people that shall be born}* That shall be to posterity, that is; and after-ages. And here the means of the Church’s perpetuity, or of perpetuating the Church of God in this world, are set down, and commended unto us; (as is by some well observed) that is, diligent preaching, and all other good means, whereby the manifold goodness and mercy of God may publicly be declared, and carefully transmitted to posterity. Or, *that shall be born*, that is, that shall by the means of preaching be converted and regenerated. See Psal. 87.4,5,6.

*{that he hath done this}* The glory of it must be given to God only: and he has done it, with great might and power: no mean, or ordinary thing; but full of miraculous events and providences.

# PSALM XXIII

**Verse 1. ***{My shepherd}* Esay 40.11. Jer. 23.4. Ezek. 34.23. Joh. 10.11. 1 Pet. 2.25.

*{I shall not want}* He has care over me, and ministers unto me all necessary things, Phil. 4.19.

**V. 2. ***{He maketh me to lie down in green pastures}* Like a good shepherd he gives me rest, food, guidance, and drink.

*{green pastures}* Heb. *pastures of tender grass*.

*{still waters}* Heb. *waters of quietness.* Still waters, best and safest for cattle.

**V. 3. ***{He restoreth my soul}* He heals me, as a shepherd does the sick sheep, that else would die.

*{he leadeth me in the paths of righteousness for his Name’s sake}* מעגלי צדק *Semitae justitiae*, properly *paths of righteousness*, is by most interpreters of our side taken here, for *straight ways*: that is, smooth, and even: not rugged, or crooked, or mountainous, as they further explain it. I am not against it: yet I wish that they that are so eager for it, (as some are) had produced some other parallel place of Scripture, to confirm this interpretation. For my part, I do not remember, or find, upon seeking, any such place elsewhere, where those two words so are joined: nor any, where the word צדק is so used, for *plain*, or *smooth*. Yet I do not deny but it may be so taken: and the rather, because I find Joel 11.23. מורה לצדקה that is, (taking the word מורה here for *rain*: which also, and more frequently, signifies a teacher: as Es. 30.11,20. *And though the Lord give you*, &c. twice, in that one verse, and Job 36.22. *Behold, God exalteth*, &c. and except, it be in this place of Joel, where it is twice; but controverted in one of the places: and Psal. 84.6. *Who passing through*, &c. where it is also questioned by some: is not found in that other sense for *rain*, anywhere:) *rain of righteousness*, or, *rain according to righteousness*, that is, *moderate rain*: according to most accurate expositors, though some to uphold the authority of the Vulgar Latin, are against it, and prefer that sense, *a teacher of righteousness.*

As therefore in Joel, *a rain of righteousness*, &c. So I confess that here *paths of righteousness*, may be taken, (as to the words) for good, plain paths. See also upon Psal. 51.19. *Sacrifices of righteousness.* But why there should be any *absurdity* conceived (as some are bold to censure) in that other sense and translation, embraced by some others, I see not sufficient reason. The question is not, whether this whole Psalm may be interpreted mystically, and applied to Christ, the great Shepherd of souls, &c. whereof I think there is no question at all to be made; since it is generally granted, that all or most Psalms, besides the literal or historical sense, may after a more sublime way, either wholly, or in part, be applied to Christ, *the end of the Law*, Rom. 10.4. not of the Prophets and Psalms only, but almost of all that is written, and contained in the Old Testament. And since we find Christ by himself and his Apostles, so often set out unto us as a *shepherd*; it were very strange, that any man should question the mystical or allegorical application of these words, and expressions, unto him.

The question is, whether in these particular words the word צדק may be translated in its proper signification, justice, or, *righteousness*: and this to be taken for the proper and literal sense of the words. *He restoreth*, (or, *converteth*, or *bringeth back*, as others:) *my soul, he leadeth me in paths of right*, this to be understood spiritually, of the guidance of God’s Spirit. As, or much to that purpose, Psalm 119. last verse: *I have gone astray like a lost sheep: seek thy servant*, &c. For why should it be unlikely, that having spoken of temporal and momentary blessings, such as belong to this life; he should proceed to the chiefest, those of the soul, the fruit whereof is eternal? This, we know, is done elsewhere frequently. But it is objected, neither is there any other objection against it, that I know of; that it cannot be here, because he does even after this, go on in the same metaphors of an ordinary shepherd, as at the beginning: *thy rod and thy staff*, &c. I do not conceive this so great an objection, as it is apprehended by that party that objects it: and besides what examples common authors might afford, I find this rule set down by very learned commentators, and well skilled in the idioms of the Scripture: that *Solet saepe Scriptura non semper eandem servare metaphoram, sed paululum ab ea divertere ac mox redire*: (Ribera in Joel c. 1. p. 47.) whereof this example among others is given; Ezek. 16.13. *and thou didst prosper into a Kingdom*: literally and properly to be understood of the Jewish commonwealth; which, both before and afterwards in the same chapter, in the verses immediately following, is spoken of figuratively under the type of a woman.

According then to this sense, and translation: *he leadeth me in the paths of righteousness*: that is, he takes care of me, to frame me to all goodness and virtue, whereby my person may be acceptable unto him, and I secured of a better life, an eternal inheritance hereafter in heaven, after I have run out my course upon earth. According to that of the Apostle: *Nevertheless the foundation of God standeth sure, having this seal; the Lord knoweth them that are his: And let every one that nameth the name of Christ, depart from iniquity.*

*{of righteousness}* Justice, or, *righteousness*, is often taken in the Scriptures in a large sense, and comprehends all manner of goodness: and not in the Scriptures only, but in the writings of ancient philosophers also. I shall set down some of their words, that Christians may blush, if their righteousness (for according to the opinion a man holds, so is his practice commonly:) come short of the righteousness of heathens, that were led by natural reason only. It is defined by one of them: *Animi affectio suum cuique tribuens*: &c. that is, *A certain temper*, or *disposition of the mind, affording to every man what is his own, and studious of the preservation of human society by bounty* (to his power) *and equitableness*: *From which cannot be separated*, (his own words:) *pietas, bonitas, liberalitas, benignitas, comitas.* And the same author, in another book, maintains, and partly proves, that no man can truly and constantly be just, or good; *qui mortem, qui dolorem*, &c. that is, who fears either death, or pain, or banishment, or poverty; or who does prize life, wealth, honor, and the like, more than right, or a good conscience. A further confirmation of this interpretation of the word *righteousness* in this place, we may fetch from Psal. 5.8. *Lead me O Lord, in thy righteousness*, &c. and Prov. 8.20. *I lead in the way of righteousness*, &c. Let the phrases be compared, and there will appear great affinity, if not identity.

**V. 4. ***{through the valley of the shadow of death}* Though he were in danger of death, as the sheep that wanders in the dark valley without his shepherd. Yea, and though he should be in the very middest of greatest evils, and calamities that can befall a man. For so the *shadow of death* is taken in Scripture; as Psal. 44.19. *Though thou hast sore broken us in the place of Dragons, and covered us with the shadow of death.* And Psal. 107. verse 10. and 14. *Such as sit in darkness*, &c. Neither is it the intent of the Psalmist by these words to set out his confidence, as though he believed that no evil should, or could happen unto him: but no such evil, as should take him from his confidence in his God: What that? that God’s *judgments are right*; that *in very faithfulness*: Psal. 119.75. for *his good*: verse 71.61. and Rom. 8.28. He does afflict him: in a word, from that confidence, wherein St. Paul, that suffered so much for Christ, who daily did encounter with so many terrors, set out at large by himself in divers places of his Epistles, as no man ever did with more; does so rejoice and glory in; and which he proposes unto all true Christians, as matter of highest comfort; *Who shall separate us from the love of Christ*, &c.? Rom. 8. verse 35,36, &c. unto the end. See more upon Psalm 14.5. *For God is in the generation*, &c.

*{I will fear no evil}* Psal. 3.6. and 118.6. What *fear* a godly man is liable unto, how different from an ungodly fear, &c. See upon Psal. 46.2. *We will not fear though the earth be removed.* And upon Psal. 91.5. *Thou shalt not be afraid neither for the terror*, &c.

*{thy rod and thy staff they comfort me}* God protects me, and sees me, and brings me safe home like a good shepherd.

**V. 5. ***{Thou preparest a table before me}* Albeit his enemies sought to destroy him, yet God deals most liberally with him, in sight, and spite of his enemies, to his joy, and their grief, Psal. 112.10. This also may spiritually and mystically be interpreted, according to that of Christ to his Disciples: *And I appoint unto you a Kingdom, as my Father hath appointed unto me: That ye may eat and drink at my table in my Kingdom*, &c. Luk. 23.29,30.

*{thou anointest my head with oil}* It was the manner at great feasts, to anoint the guests with oil, Amos 6.6. Luke 7.46.

*{anointest}* Heb. *makest fat*.

**V. 6. ***{I will dwell in the house of the Lord for ever}* He sets not his felicity in the pleasures of this world, but in the fear, and service of God. It may also be understood of heaven, especially if by *length of days*, as it is in the Original, we understand, not a long time, properly, as most interpreters; but eternity, as some do: and as the word does import sometimes. So Psal. 21.4. *He asked*, &c. *even length of days for ever and ever.* And Psal. 93.5. *Thy testimonies are very sure: holiness becometh thine house, O Lord, to length of days*: so in the Original; well expressed, in the English there, *for ever*. See upon Psal. 15.1. *Who shall abide in thy Tabernacle?*

# PSALM XXIV

**Verse 1. ***{Earth is the Lord’s}* Deut. 10.14. Job 41.11. Psal. 50.12. 1 Cor. 10.26,28.

*{and the fulness thereof}* Before he speaks of the Church and people of God particularly, which he does afterwards, he first begins with the generality of God’s sovereignty over all the world, and over all men, as Creator of all: to let us know; First, that although the Church of God has a particular relation unto him, to be called his people, &c. yet God is the God of all men, and has a care of all. Secondly, that although God, by right of Creation, Administration, Sustentation, &c. be the God of all men in general: yet there is a select people, (called the Church) who have a more particular right to, and interest in his love, favor, and protection; whom he describes by certain marks and properties, external and visible: And because therein the happiness of man chiefly consists to be of this number, that thus belong unto God more particularly; he therefore invites in the end all people and nations of the world joyfully to receive and entertain him, (by seeking to him, as verse 6. and conforming themselves to his doctrine, in their life and conversation; which presupposes a right knowledge of him) that so they may be partakers of the same happiness.

Some make the coherence of the words to be; that being to speak of the Church of God, then included in Israel, the better to set out the mercy of God, in choosing to himself a peculiar people and nation, to own it for his after a special manner: He first begins with the generality of all men’s relation unto God, as a Creator, and in that respect, the God of all, equally; and then upon mention of Zion, (called here, *the hill of the Lord*) lest all Jews should think themselves as Jews, merely, true members of the Church, (as indeed many did;) he sets down some marks and tokens whereby a true Israelite might be known, and discerned from one that is so by name and outward profession only. And then for the latter verses, how those also are by some made to cohere, differently from what was showed before, we shall see there.

Moreover, these first words contain a tacit prophecy concerning the future calling of the Gentiles, and generality of the Church, to consist of all people and nations of the world; upon this ground; that God is the God and Creator of all men, as well as of the Jews and Israelites. The Apostle, 1 Cor. 10.25,28. cites these words to confirm his doctrine there, that we ought not to be scrupulous about meats, because, if all God’s, (as made by him) then all theirs also, (for their use, &c.) that belong unto God; or, *whose God he is*; as some there: or rather, because none bad or evil in themselves, as created all by God, and all owned by him, for his. But I think it more likely that the Apostle in citing those words had respect unto Psal. 50.12. where we have the same words again. The context there has more affinity with Saint Paul his scope in that place, than the context here.

**V. 2. ***{For he hath founded it upon the seas}* Job 38.5,6, &c. Psal. 104.5,6, &c. 136.6.

*{upon}* Or, *besides*, Psal. 1.3. Isa. 32.20. Besides the seas, yet so as it is not overthrown by them.

*{upon the seas}* The water, in the order of the Elements, and by its nature, as at first created, is above the Earth, and should cover the earth, but that by the power and providence of Almighty God, to the end that the earth might become habitable, it was driven down lower than the earth: than that part of the earth, at least, which was useful and necessary to man: which may in a general sense be termed *the earth* absolutely. It is very likely that the Psalmist does allude to, and rhetorically set out this peculiar ordinance and providence of God for man: wherein as much as in anything, his great and sovereign power over all things does appear. *Founded* therefore, not with regard to the nature of the Element of water, or first order of God in the creation: but *founded*, as it does now appear to our eyes: elegantly and rhetorically to set out the power of God. It is the greatest care of human architects, to lay their foundations upon things very firm and steady; as rocks, and the like; especially the foundations of great and bulky structures: but behold, here is an architect that has founded such a Structure as the whole Earth, upon the waters, and yet it stands. See upon Psal. 103.3. *Who layeth the beams*, &c. And yet who knows what may be in and about the Center of the Earth? We read in the Scripture, Gen. 1.6,7. Psal. 148.4. of waters in or above the Heavens, where though by *Heavens*, ordinary expositors understand the Air, the lower Region of it; yet there be very learned men that are not satisfied with that interpretation; and choose rather to acknowledge their ignorance, than absolutely to determine where they see just cause of further consideration.

*{upon}* Or, *besides*.

**V. 3. ***{Who shall ascend into the hill of the Lord?}* Psal. 15.1. See there.

**V. 4. ***{He that hath clean hands, and a pure heart}* Isa. 33.15,16. and Rom. 12.14. *Follow peace with all men, and holiness, without which no man shall see the Lord.* Clean hands, not by external ablution; which being done, not for outward cleanliness, or bare legal ceremony; but with an opinion of holiness and sanctity, was reproved by our Savior; Mark 7.2. *And when they saw some of his Disciples*, &c. to verse 24. *And from thence*, &c. *Clean hands, and a pure heart*: that is, innocent actions, that proceed from a sincere heart, actions that are both materially and formally good and godly: without any fraud, dissimulation, and hypocrisy. Again, *clean hands*, and *a pure heart*: that is, innocent, not only from doing, but even from thinking, intending, projecting, contriving, or wishing anything that is evil. *Let the wicked man forsake his way, and the unrighteous man* (or, *man of iniquity*; that is, wicked, as before:) *his thoughts.* Esay 55.7. This the very Heathen by the light of nature, could teach: *Nam scelus intra se tacitum qui cogitat ullum, Facti crimen habet.* No wonder then if our Savior require it of Christians: *Ye have heard that it was said*, &c. *But I say unto you, that whosoever looketh on a woman*, &c.

*{He that hath clean hands}* Heb. *the clean of hands*.

*{who hath not lift up his soul unto vanity}* That has not his affections set upon wealth, honor, pleasures, Jer. 22.27. But about the right interpretation or translation of the Original word, there’s no small controversy among the Rabbins, or Jewish doctors. The reading of most Hebrew copies is not נפשו *his soul*; but נפשי *my soul*. This being the most received reading of the two, hence the Rabbins infer, that it is God himself that speaks; and would have *swearing* by God, or *by the soul of God* to be intended. The verb נשא, will sit well enough for that purpose, we grant. And Jerem. 51.14. Amos 6.6. we read of God’s *swearing by his* (own) *soul*. Yet we do not find that it was a custom among the Jews, so to swear, *by the life*, or *soul* of God; which this interpretation presupposes. But of swearing nevertheless, though not by God, yet by a man’s own soul, according to the other reading, *his soul*; the words may be taken and are indeed, by most accurate interpreters: who make the following words, *nor sworn deceitfully*, but an *Epexegesis*, or further interpretation, and confirmation of these. This, because it pleases men of such worth and judgment; and is warrantable by the Original words, I cannot but like. However, this that we had before being no less warrantable, need not be rejected. And certain it is, that there is nothing that more provokes a man often to swear, than to set his affection upon worldly things.

*{thy face, O Jacob}* There is no more in the Hebrew than bare *Jacob*, without any *O*, which either should not be added, or with it somewhat else to make the sense perfect; as in the margin, *O God of Jacob*. But by others, it is otherwise supplied; as, *the generation of Jacob*; by some: by others, *that seek thy face in Jacob*. There be that leave *Jacob* alone in the Text; and read by itself, *Jacob*; as if he had said, Who are (those that *seek thy face*, &c. as before) the only true posterity of *Jacob*; or, *true Israelites*. See the like, Psal. 68.26. *from the fountain of Israel*; or as in the margin there, *ye that are of the fountain*, &c.

**V. 7. ***{Lift up your heads, O ye gates}* It was the fashion in ancient times, when they would solemnize the entrance of any prince, or other, that had well deserved of the public, or by way of flattery perchance, and more out of fear than good will; to break down the walls, and pull off the gates of the city: partly, that the party in his triumphant Chariot, with all his train, might have the more free entrance; and partly, as professing by that ceremony, that their city needed no walls nor gates, as long as it had such a Guardian and Protector within it. It is likely that David by these words, does allude to some such custom.

*{ye everlasting doors}* Heb. פתחי עולם some translate it, *Portae saeculi*; or, *mundi*: that is, *you doors*; or *gates of the World*: as if he directed his speech to all the nations of the world; to the great ones, princes and Potentates of the world, especially: all whom (according to this translation) the Psalmist does invite, readily, and cheerfully to embrace the truth of God, and so to become members of his Church. Of this somewhat has been said before, at the beginning. But the more generally (and more warrantably) followed translation, is that we have here; according to which, the more literal sense is supposed to be this: David in great zeal intended a magnificent house to the God of Israel, and made preparations for it. He did not build it himself we know; but yet he knew that his son after him should, and thereupon according to his zeal much rejoicing and cheering himself with the very thought of it, he sung this triumphant and gratulatory Ode. He calls them (the doors of this intended Holy house:) *everlasting*: partly in reference to God’s promise, expressed Psal. 132.14. of his eternal abode there; (of which see more upon Psal. 68.16.) and partly in opposition to the former Tabernacle, now upon the building of this Temple, to cease; and to the condition of the Ark, which in former times had been removed from place to place, till it was settled in this house, from whence it never stirred afterwards, till the destruction of the Temple itself. Some conceive this to have been sung by David, when the Ark was brought in by him to the place that he had prepared for it; as Psal. 47. and 68. where see more.

In a more mystical sense, by these *gates and doors* here are understood the souls of the faithful; or of men in general, which are here exhorted to prepare themselves for the due entertainment of Christ, and his Gospel: to which not much unlike is that of the Prophet, Isa. 40.3. applied to Christ coming in the flesh, whereof the Ark was a type, Luke 3.4,5. *The voice of one crying in the wilderness, Prepare ye the way of the Lord, make his paths straight. Every valley shall be filled, and every mountain and hill shall be brought low*, &c. And again, in a mystical too, but different sense, the words by others, are applied unto Christ, as directed to him after his resurrection, and glorious conquest of hell and the grave; now ready to ascend into Heaven: by *gates*, understanding the gates of Heaven.

# PSALM XXV

**Verse 1. ***{Unto thee, O Lord}* This Psalmist shows in the beginning, that he does not put his trust in any worldly thing, but seeks to God alone for deliverance.

*{I lift up my soul}* Deut. 24.15. Psal. 86.4.

**V. 2. ***{I trust in thee}* Psal. 22.5. & 31.1. & 34.8. Esay 28.16. Rom. 10.11. For deliverance from mine enemies, See upon Psal. 16.1. *In thee do I put my trust.*

*{let me not be ashamed}* By missing that I hope for. It may be he means the kingdom promised.

*{let not mine enemies triumph over me}* When they have overcome me, 2 Chr. 20.27.

**V. 3. ***{which transgress without cause}* Who deal ill with me of malice, not any way provoked by me.

**V. 4. ***{Shew me thy ways}* Psal. 27.11. & 86.11. & 119. Of holiness, which lead to heaven, as a way leads to the journey’s end.

**V. 5. ***{Lead me}* Heb. *Make me to walk*, Prov. 4.11.

*{all the day}* Constantly against all temptations.

**V. 6. ***{thy tender mercies}* Psal. 103.17. & 106.1. & 107.1. Jer. 33.11. Heb. *thy bowels*.

*{for they have been ever of old}* You have helped yours from the beginning of the world, therefore forsake not me.

**V. 7. ***{Remember not the sins of my youth}* He confesses that God raised him up enemies in his age for the sins of his youth, Job 13.26. & 20.11. Jer. 3.25. David prays God to take away those sins, that his foes may not hurt him.

*{transgressions}* Or, *defections*.

**V. 8. ***{Therefore will he teach sinners in the way}* Those that err he shows them how to return into the way by repentance.

**V. 9. ***{guide}* Heb. *make to walk*.

*{in judgment}* In the right way they ought to walk in; as follows in this verse, and ver. 12. Psal. 5.8.

**V. 10. ***{mercy and truth}* All good we have comes from God’s mercy, and constancy in keeping his word, Psal. 40.11. & 100.5. Gen. 24.27. The words may bear this sense also, that to such as apply themselves to the practice of godliness, and keeping of God’s Commandments, the goodness, mercy, and truth of God in all his ways is made known, upon which the love of God (the very life of true religion and godliness) is chiefly grounded. I say upon a full apprehension, and approved experience of God’s goodness, mercy, and truth. *With the merciful thou wilt show thyself* (ἐμφανίσεις: a word used by Christ upon this occasion, Joh. 14.21.) *merciful, &c. and with the froward, thou wilt show thyself froward*, Psal. 18.25,26. I think the words will bear this too, as well as that other more obvious sense, and more proper to the place, of which we have spoken there: (Psal. 18.24,25.) But see more upon verse 14. *The secret of the Lord.*

**V. 11. ***{For thy Name’s sake}* That your Name may be praised.

*{for it is great}* And therefore needs much mercy for forgiveness, Psal. 51.1,2.

**V. 12. ***{What man is he that feareth the Lord?}* If there be any such man, God will guide him.

**V. 13. ***{His soul shall dwell at ease, &c.}* He shall prosper. See upon Psal. 37.1. *Fret not thyself.*

*{shall dwell at ease}* Heb. *shall lodge in goodness*.

**V. 14. ***{The secret of the Lord}* This phrase, *the secret of the Lord*, is not always used in one sense; for when Job 29.4. says, that *the secret of God was upon his Tabernacle*, he means it of his secret favor, and providence towards him in things temporal, blessing the works of his hands and whatsoever belonged to him; whereby many insensibly thrive in this world, as to the admiration of others, so beyond their own expectation; yea, and knowledge too: Of which the Wise man has this observation, *There is that scattereth, and yet increaseth; and there is that withholdeth more than is meet, but it tendeth to poverty*, Prov. 11.24. See also Prov. 3.32,33. *For the froward*, &c. This cannot be the meaning here. Some understand by it, a particular assurance of God’s favor, whereby happiness is secured to us, both present and future. But this does not seem so well to agree with the scope of the words, neither here, nor elsewhere, where this expression is used. Besides, it is a doctrine subject to many restrictions and limitations, without which, many are deceived by it, or rather deceive themselves.

Others therefore understand by it, the doctrine of God, as particularly, before Christ, the Law; a secret, not unto all nations of the world only besides the Jews; but even among the Jews, though commonly professed, yet hidden, but to the godly, who earnestly applied themselves to the search and study of it: which made David elsewhere to pray; *Open thou mine eyes, that I may behold wondrous things out of thy Law*: Psal. 119.18. and so highly to prize the knowledge of it, as he does in that Psalm, and elsewhere often. Before Christ then, the Law; since Christ, the Gospel; of which Christ said unto his Disciples, *Unto you it is given to know the mysteries of the Kingdom of heaven, but unto them it is not given*, Matth. 13.11. And Joh. 7.17. *If any man will do his will, he shall know of the doctrine*, &c. And the Apostle, that *if any man love God, the same* (ἔγνωσται: *edoctus fuit*, Beza;) *is taught by him*: 1 Cor. 8.3.

All this granted as pertinent and probable, yet I conceive by *the secret of the Lord*, (both here, and Prov. 3.32. if we take verses 31,32 together, and begin a new paragraph at 33. as it is in our English Bibles: otherwise, joining verses 32. and 33. then *secret* there, as we have said before:) more particularly to be understood of a certain knowledge and acknowledgment of God’s providence, and manifold dispensations not obvious to carnal, yea many times contrary to human judgment, which they, who in all humility fear God, and heartily desire and endeavor in all things to submit unto him, by long observation, and God’s special favor, in time attain unto. Of which the Psalmist elsewhere speaks, *Whoso is wise, and will observe these things*, &c. Psal. 107. last verse; and the Prophet Jerem. 9.23,24. *Let not the wise man glory in his wisdom*, &c. *But let him that glorieth, glory in this, that he understandeth and knoweth me*, &c. As also the Prophet Hos. last chapter, last verse, (not to mention others) in these words, *Who is wise, and he shall understand these things; prudent, and he shall know them; for the ways of the Lord are right, and the just shall walk in them; but the transgressors shall fall therein.*

*{and he will shew them his covenant}* Or, *and his covenant to make them know it*.

**V. 15. ***{Mine eyes are ever towards the Lord}* Psal. 123.1.

*{pluck}* Heb. *bring forth*.

**V. 17. ***{The troubles of my heart are enlarged}* My grief is increased, because of mine enemies’ cruelty.

**V. 19. ***{for they are many}* The greater and more cruel his foes were, the more he needed God’s help.

*{cruel hatred}* Heb. *hatred of violence*.

**V. 21. ***{Let integrity and uprightness preserve me}* Forasmuch as I have behaved myself uprightly towards mine enemies, let them know that you are the defender of my just cause.

This Psalm (which I put here at the end, of purpose, as a thing not very pertinent to the matter of the Psalm, nor for everyone’s reading:) is one of those, which we may call Alphabetical Psalms, because both for the number, and order, (but in different kinds,) they follow the Hebrew Alphabet, which does consist of 22. letters. Of this kind be, besides this, the 34. Psalm, which begins, *I will bless the Lord at all times*, &c. the 37. *Fret not thyself because of evil doers*, &c. the 111. *I will praise the Lord with my whole heart*, &c. and 112. *Blessed is the man*, &c. For, *Praise ye the Lord*, in this and the foregoing 111. Psalm, is rather the title, than the beginning of the Psalm; and should rather be put over, (as it is by many:) than at the beginning. The 119. *Blessed are the undefiled in the way*, &c. And lastly, the 145. which begins, *I will extol thee my God O King*, &c. All these, as I said, be Alphabetical, but not exactly all, nor after one kind.

In this, the first in this kind, every verse (with some exception, of which more, by and by;) does begin with a letter, according to the order of letters in the Alphabet, *Aleph, Beth, Gimel*, &c. So in the 34. and the 145. Psalms: but, as I said, with some exceptions, and diversities, in every one of these. For in this, there be three letters, or verses, (according to the order of the Alphabet) deficient; *Beth, Vau*, and *Koph*: and instead of these, three redundant, *Beth, Peh*, (whereof one is at the end,) and *Resh*, being all double. It is true indeed, that the second word of the second *Aleph*, begins with a *Beth*; and the third word of the verse *He*, (or, fifth verse,) begins with a *Vau*; which is observed by some, as though those letters there should supply the want of two of the 3. verses that are wanting. But there is little likelihood in that conceit; especially, since we find nothing, in the same kind, to supply the defect of the *Koph*. And yet I should like of this better, than of their conceit, (too bold to be admitted) who would have the verses so divided, that that *Beth* and that *Vau* which we have spoken of, the one the second; the other the third; (as now divided:) might be the first words of the verses: and would have a word (קום) added to the 18. verse, to make it begin with a *Koph*. And then for the *Peh* at the end, they make that to be part of the foregoing verse.

But supposing this would do here, yet we shall still be to seek in other Psalms, of the same kind, where the like anomalies, or deficiencies are to be found. For not to speak of the 34. where at the end we find a supernumerary *Peh*, (or verse beginning with the letter *Peh*:) as it is here: in the 145. Psalm, of the very same kind of Alphabetical disposition as this, there is a *Nun* wanting, which cannot be supplied but by supplying a whole verse, (by the vulgar Greek and Latin indeed, out of the 17. verse of the same, with alteration of one word or two, I know not how rightly supplied:) which would be of dangerous consequence. Though there be, even of our side, some, (Mollerus for one by name) who make no great matter of it, and plainly deliver their opinion, that they believe one verse, *librariorum incuria*, by the negligence of the Scribes, to be wanting. But the Rabbins there, some of them, make a great mystery of it, as though David in his meditations had seen the dreadful נפילה afterwards expressed by the Prophet Esay, Chap. 24.20. and had, of purpose, passed it over, to hasten to the contrary and more comfortable סומך, which there begins the 14. verse. But of these conceits, or what may more safely be answered, more shall be said, when we have done with the rest, already mentioned, which we called Alphabetical Psalms. The next therefore is, the 37. Psalm, differing from the former in this, that every letter, besides the Alphabetical, has one verse more belonging to it, not tied to any letter, but free for any, as in other Psalms. So indeed we find it in the greatest part of it, by far; so that *a majore parte*, we call it Alphabetical. But otherwise it has many anomalies, and exorbitances. For besides that some letters (as the *Daleth*, and the *Caph*:) have but one verse, there is no *Gnajin* to be found: no, nor *Peh* neither; not in its due place, at least: but after the *Tsade*, there is a *Peh* indeed: but that, not Alphabetical, as I conceive, but merely casual, and belonging to the *Tsade*. Except we shall allow of their conjecture, which I confess, all things well considered, is not altogether to be rejected, who would have a new verse to begin at those words of the 28. verse, (as now divided) לעולם נשמרו, and עולם read for לעולם, so that we may have a *Gnajin* to begin with. But this last of the *Lamed* to be put away, they might well have spared, and would, I believe, if they had considered, that this servile *Lamed* can be no hindrance, but that the verse may be thought to begin with a *Gnajin*; no more, than the *Vau* before the *Thau*, (ותשועת) at the end of the Psalm: at which haply some might stumble; but they need not; for in this case, it is not considerable: and if not the *Vau*, then neither the *Lamed* before the *Gnajin*.

But to go on in the general view of these Alphabetical Psalms: The two next, the 111. and the 112. are both in their Alphabetical way contrary to this last. For whereas the 37. as we have said, has two verses belonging to one letter: these two, have two letters belonging to each verse. Neither is there any considerable anomaly in either of them; so that we shall need to say no more of them. The last is the 119. Psalm, which consists of 22. octonaries, each letter of the Alphabet in order making 8. verses, all which 8. begin with one letter: and though this be a very long Psalm, yet I do not find anything amiss, or out of order, in this neither.

Now to conclude concerning these defects, or anomalies in general; although I am not of opinion, that if it were granted, that *incuria librariorum* (as Mollerus spoke before) some have happened, much can be concluded from thence against the integrity of the Hebrew Text, which does not consist in such petty things; neither would Beza have made so bold with the New Testament, if he had thought so: yet considering the number of them, and variety, I do rather join with them that believe such defects, or anomalies, (or however to be called) to have proceeded from the author himself, who did not think himself bound to such niceties, but followed the intention of his mind, wholly bent upon the matter, where the words did not offer themselves; there being no other reason to be given of this Alphabetical disposition, or invention, but to help the memory; a thing not so considerable, as to make a business of it.

# PSALM XXVI

**Verse 1. ***{Judge me}* Or, *Judge for me*, Deut. 32.36.

*{O Lord}* He flees to God to be Judge of his just cause, seeing there is no equity among men.

*{I have walked in mine integrity}* See upon Psal. 18.20. *The Lord rewarded me*, &c.

*{therefore}* Or, *that*.

**V. 2. ***{Examine me}* Psal. 7.9.

*{try my reins and my heart}* See notes on Psal. 7.9.

**V. 3. ***{For thy lovingkindness is before mine eyes}* He shows what stayed him that he did not recompense evil for evil. Or, *because your loving kindness is before mine eyes, therefore have I walked in your truth*; that is, in the way of your Commandments, therefore have I applied myself faithfully to perform, what you have commanded. Yea though I meet with many discouragements, many crosses, oppositions, and persecutions for your sake, Yet *because thy loving kindness*, &c. So of Moses the Apostle, that he *refused to be called the son of Pharaoh’s daughter: Choosing rather to suffer affliction, &c. Esteeming the reproach of Christ*, &c. Why? because *he had respect unto the recompense of the reward.* And 1 Tim. 4.9,10. *This is a faithful saying*, &c. *For therefore we both labour and suffer reproach, because we trust in the living God*, &c. and 1 Cor. 15. last verse, after a long disputation and assertion of the resurrection of the dead, *Therefore my beloved brethren, be ye steadfast, immovable*, &c. *for as much as ye know that your labour is not in vain in the Lord.* The Scripture is full of such places. That doctrine therefore that passes ordinarily for very plausible, that we ought not to serve God for hope of reward: and that so to do is but mercenary, not ingenuous and acceptable service; whether it be as true and consonant to the word of God, as it is commonly delivered and received, I make a question. I am sure that I find Christ himself proposed unto us as an example, *who for the joy that was set before him, endured the cross, despised the shame, & is set down at the right hand of the throne of God.* Hebr. 12.2. I know of the ἀντὶ, *for*, how to be expounded, there is variety of opinions. But the doctrine however to be found, may further be asserted by St. Paul, who sticks not to conclude and say, That *if there be no resurrection of the dead*, it is best for us, *to eat and drink*, (that is, as I take it, and all men understand it, to take our pleasures of the world, whilst we may) *for tomorrow we die*, 1 Cor. 15.32. See more upon Psal. 130.4. *But there is forgiveness with thee, that thou mayest be feared.*

**V. 4. ***{I have not sat with vain persons}* Psal. 1.1. See the notes there, by comparing of which passages (the first verse there, with 4, and 5. here) both *verbs*, to sit, to go, &c. and substantives, *ungodly, sinners, scornful* there: with the *verbs*, and *substantives*, here: and their order: it may probably be inferred, that the Psalmist had no direct aim to any formal *gradation*, but put the words together, as they offered themselves, having no other intention, than to make full and solemn profession (as of great consequence, to godliness and piety:) that he did avoid the company of wicked ungodly men, as much as he could possibly. I know that a man affecting ostentation of wit and subtlety, might find somewhat even here, in the order, variety, repetition of the words, whereupon to ground some notable (in his own judgment) observation. For my part, I will not attempt it; nor because I despair of finding somewhat that might please such palates; but because it is against my judgment; if not conscience. Yet there is no great hurt (since many are much taken with them:) in such observations, so that they be not much pressed; nor conclusions of consequence in controverted matters, raised from them.

But now to the matter of the words; the avoiding of evil company: Out of all question it is that wherein great part of that circumspect walking, required of all Christians, and without which, we can have little ground of assurance, that we are not going in *the broad way* of eternal destruction: great part, I say, of our circumspect walking in the world, does consist. For good men, are more apt (such is the corruption of nature) to be corrupted by evil company, (according to that of the Apostles; or rather of an ancient Comic poet, cited and confirmed by the Apostle, that *evil communications corrupt good manners*: 1 Cor. 15.33.) than evil men to be benefited by good and godly company. It is incredible, but to them that observe it with care, what power evil examples in daily conversation have, to insinuate themselves secretly and insensibly (wherein is greatest danger) so that even the best, and best rooted in godliness, have occasion enough to fear, and to be aware.

However, we must make difference of evil communications; evil in the very communication itself: and communication, or communications with evil men. For some bad men, though bad in their private and more secret communication, and conversation, yet out of some civil respect, or otherwise, are inoffensive in their ordinary conversation. Others, as they are in their lives, and ordinary practice; so in all companies, (except they be restrained by some fear) offensive and scandalous: as by frequent swearing, cursing, rank discourse, and the like. We should not upon any terms (if we can avoid it) endure the company of men, though it be with no danger to ourselves, scandalous in their communication, and ordinary talk: except we may take the liberty to reprove them, and that it may probably do them good: so far at least, as to show our dislike and just offence. Else, men become guilty (though innocent ourselves) of that dishonor that is done unto God, by such evil communication.

Besides, we must distinguish between men, suspected, but not convicted, and still having *a form of godliness*: and open profane, scandalous offenders. According to the Apostle’s rule, and direction (though it be not altogether the same case now; because we have no professed heathens, or atheists among us: not publicly allowed of, at least; I wish I could say, nor tolerated) 1 Cor. 5. *I wrote unto you in an Epistle*, &c. 9,10. to the end of the chapter: we should, as to matter of scandal, most avoid or endeavor to avoid their company, being scandalous in their lives, who are nearest unto us: as of the same house, or parish, and the like. Others, that we have no relation to, if we light upon them by chance, as our occasions do lead us, we need not be so scrupulous. But this of avoiding the nearest, &c. must be understood, where we are not obliged by any duty: for we may not cast off obedience, and natural obligations, in the performance of which the very life of godliness is interested, under color of religion. So much of evil company here upon this place, where we find it by such variation of words inculcated and insisted upon, may serve for divers other places, where it is again, whether more largely, or briefly spoken of.

*{dissemblers}* Heb. *close, or, deceitful*.

**V. 5. ***{sit with the wicked}* Jer. 15.17.

**V. 6. ***{I will wash mine hands in innocency}* Psal. 73.13. They did use to wash their hands in token of innocence, Deut. 21.6,7. Mat. 27.24. He may withal seem to allude to the priests’ washing before they went to the altar, 2 Chron. 4.6. Exod. 30.18,19.

*{so will I compass thine altar}* With sacrifices and oblations, as the Rabbins expound it: confirmed by the Vulgar translation of the 6. verse of the next Psalm, *Circuivi, & immolavi*, &c. but that the Hebrew words there afford not that sense. Others, *I will compass*; that is, cause to be compassed with the company of singers and Choir-men. It was not lawful for ordinary men to come nigh to the Altar: they stood at a distance: which some think is all that is here intended by the word *compassing*. See upon Psal. 97.7.

*of all them that are about him.* His meaning by this expression in general is, that he will be very studious and diligent in the public worship of God; and all duties belonging thereunto. But it may be David more particularly might intend by these words to profess his charity towards his enemies, whom, though much wronged and cruelly persecuted by them, yet he did not desire to requite, nor had any evil design against. Which might seem rather Evangelical, than Legal innocence, or perfection: (at least, if that collection of the Pharisees were true, *Thou shalt love thy neighbour, and hate thine enemy*, Matth. 5.43. But it was not. The Law (not to insist in other particulars, not a few:) was directly against it; *Thou shalt not avenge, nor bear any grudge against the children of thy people: but thou shalt love thy neighbour as thyself: I am the Lord*, Lev. 19.18. And when Esay (or God rather by his Prophet) said, which comes nearest to David’s words, *When you spread forth your hands, I will hide mine eyes from you: yea when ye make many prayers, I will not hear: your hands are full of blood*, Esay 1.15. it is not to be conceived, that by *hands full of blood*, he meant their hands only, that shed innocent blood; but theirs also, who thirsted after the blood of those that had done them wrong, and were set upon revenge. It was not therefore altogether an Evangelical counsel, or constitution, (as some would have it) which our Savior Matth. 5.23,24. (which haply might have some reference to these very words of David, we are now upon) enjoins; *Therefore if thou bring thy gift to the Altar, and there rememberest that thy brother hath ought against thee, Leave there thy gift before the Altar, and go thy way: first be reconciled to thy brother, and then come and offer thy gift.* Which I take to be rather a confirmation, or further declaration and vindication of the Law against the false glosses and collections of the Scribes and Pharisees; than a new constitution. Of private revenge, see more upon Psal. 35.4. *Let them be confounded*, &c.

**V. 8. ***{where thine honour dwelleth}* Heb. *of the tabernacle of thy honour*.

**V. 9. ***{Gather not}* Or, *Take not away*.

*{Gather not my soul with sinners}* Destroy me not in the overthrow of the wicked, Esay 57.1. 1 Sam. 15.6. Gen. 18.23.

*{bloody men}* Heb. *men of blood*.

**V. 10. ***{in whose hands is mischief}* Whose cruel hands do execute the malicious devices of their hearts.

*{full of bribes}* Heb. *filled with*. And by consequence, of injustice, Deut. 16.19. Esay 33.15.

**V. 12. ***{My foot}* Or, *when my foot*.

*{in an even place}* When God shall set me free from slippery places in safety, I will praise him openly: that is, when I am set free from enemies, and settled in peace, Psal. 119.8.

# PSALM XXVII

**Verse 1. ***{The Lord is my light}* Mic. 7.8. Who delivers me from darkness of afflictions, Psal. 18.28.

*{my salvation}* Because he was assured of good success in all his dangers, and that his salvation was laid up in God, he feared not the tyranny of his enemies.

*{the Lord is the strength of my life}* Psal. 118.6.

*{strength of my life}* He that keeps me alive in despite of all my enemies.

**V. 2. ***{and my foes}* Heb. *my foes to me*. To show, that they did malice him in particular.

*{came upon me}* Heb. *approached against me*.

*{to eat up my flesh}* As a Lion that comes to devour a sheep, Psal. 7.2. & 14.4.

**V. 3. ***{Though an host should encamp against me}* Psal. 3.6.

*{in this will I be confident}* That God will free me, and destroy my enemies, verse 1,2.

**V. 4. ***{that I may dwell in the house of the Lord}* He desires more to enjoy God’s Ordinances, than wife, children, or goods, Psal. 23.6. & 84.10. See upon Psal. 15.1. *Who shall abide*: and upon ver. 8. of this Psalm, *Seek ye my face*, &c. of David’s wonderful love and affection to God’s house. It might have done there well: but because I find it here, upon this verse, I will give it place here. I find a question here proposed by a very learned man, and very zealous against all Superstition, and Popish ceremonies. It is wished by many, he had used more moderation sometimes, to have seriously considered, which may truly be so deemed, (because idle and superstitious:) and which not, because both ancient, and useful. The question is: *Videndum nunc an similiter affecti*, &c. *We are now to consider*, says he, *whether Christians ought now to be so affected towards Churches and Ceremonies, as David was.* His resolution of the question is: *Etsi autem fateor longe nos a patribus differre*, &c. That is, *Although I confess the case now is far different of what it was in the time of the Fathers*, (that is, in David’s time:) *Yet because even now God doth not keep together his people without some external order, and doth yet bring men unto him by earthly rudiments: I deny not our Churches that Beauty, which should ravish*, or *draw to itself, the wishes and affections of godly people. For the Word of God, the Sacraments, public Prayers*, (all these performed and administered in Churches) *and other like helps, may not, without impious contempt of God, be despised, who there* (in those ordinances, &c.) *as it were in a glass, or a picture*, (*imagine*: it may be he meant, more generally, some external form, or shape, or figure:) *doth exhibit himself unto us.*

*{behold the beauty of the Lord}* Psal. 62.2. God is beautiful and pleasant to his Saints in his Temple, Psal. 68.24,25. & 96.9. & 110.3.

*{the beauty}* Or, *the delight*.

*{to inquire in his Temple}* The servants of God seek to know God’s will in God’s house: from his word there read and preached, or interpreted, by God’s lawful Ministers: who if they mislead, woe be unto them! It is very possible they may; I confess: especially if they be ignorant, (for even such, in best times, by wicked practices, and unconscionable Prelates are put in sometimes:) or if parties in a faction, and the like. However if such, lawfully ordained and put in, mislead, they that are misled, may have somewhat to plead for themselves: *The Scribes and Pharisees sit in Moses’ seat. All therefore whatsoever they bid you observe*: &c. Matth. 23.2,3. Nevertheless, godly people must not so far trust to their Ministers, but that, they that are of abilities, must embrace all good opportunities further to satisfy themselves, when occasion is, as those *noble* Bereans did: who are commended for it, Acts 17.11.

**V. 5. ***{For in the time of trouble he shall hide me in his pavilion}* If I might live in God’s Church, he would defend me, and I should praise him.

*{shall}* Or, *would*.

*{hide me in his pavilion}* Psal. 31.20. & 91.3,4.

*{shall}* Or, *would*.

*{shall}* Or, *would*.

**V. 6. ***{now shall}* Or, *then should*.

*{therefore will}* Or, *then would*.

*{sacrifices of joy}* Heb. *of shouting*, Numb. 10.10.

*{will sing}* Or, *would sing, yea, I would*.

**V. 8. ***{Seek ye my face, my heart said unto thee}* In the Original, word for word, thus; *To thee my heart said, Seek ye my face; Thy face, Lord will I seek.* Which words, as all agree, are somewhat defective; that is, somewhat must be supplied to make the sense perfect: and besides, they are, not misplaced, we may not say; (for such trajections to best authors are ordinary enough; and we have examples of them in divers other places of Scripture:) but, not placed in due order of ordinary construction: Through this defect, and displacing together, great liberty is left to interpreters; and translated they are by divers, diversely: yet so, that this variety of translations, (the vulgar Greek and Latin, who read them otherwise than we do, excepted) comes all to one purpose in point of sense, or sentence: neither is there any (to my best judgment) that either supplies, or places the words better, than this our English here. So that more needs not to be said of the words.

And as for the sense, it seems plain enough; so plain indeed, (in my judgment) that the very plainness makes me suspect, that there is somewhat more in it, than has been yet observed by any that I have seen; *Thou saidst, Seek ye my face: my heart said unto thee, Thy face Lord will I seek.* That is, As you have commanded, that we should seek your face, so I do. That is the sum of what is said. But where commanded? Or, why this command of God, so expressly, by a kind of *Prosopopoeia*, more than any other, in that kind elsewhere, here set down? I would say at least, which would put some more life, or emphasis in the words, that this command of seeking God, being more particularly, by God, laid upon the heart of man, (as may appear, Deut. 4.19. to which some refer us; *But if from thence thou shalt seek the Lord thy God, thou shalt find him, if thou seek him with all thy heart, and with all thy soul*:) David in great confidence of his hearty performance, does here appeal to God, himself the searcher of the hearts, remembering him (to speak the more movingly) of his own words, and immediately answering, as his own heart suggested unto him. But this would have been more plain, I must confess, if the heart had been mentioned here in the command, as well as it is in the answer. And besides, if *my face*, had been in the command, Deut. 4.19. (the only place we find, that David could allude unto) so directly expressed, as it is here in this supposed repetition, or citation.

That therefore which I do more incline unto, is this. It is a common observation, that in best writers, sometimes one and the selfsame word, not in the same Book only; but even within the compass of one or two verses, or so, is used in a different sense. So the word מורה Job 2.23. *Be glad then ye children of Zion*, &c. twice repeated, by the Chaldee paraphrase, and most Rabbins, as likewise by our English margin there, (to which many other translations agree) is taken in the first place for a *Teacher*; in the second, for *Rain*. In the New Testament it is very frequent, according to best interpreters, who commonly call it (where one word, or phrase does occur in two different senses) an ἀντανάκλασις, or πλοκή. Now to apply this to our present occasion: There never was man (I think) either prince or other, that ever showed more zeal to God’s outward worship and service (as established in those days) than David did. Of his inward affection, there is no question to be made; else his outward carriage and profession would but little have availed with God: but that is not to our purpose here. Although therefore a man, (even in those days) that had the knowledge of the true God, might be religious, and serve God out of Judea, as we know by Job’s example, and divers others since; yet was it David’s delight, and his earnest desire, yea, his chiefest happiness in this world, and the greatest comfort of his life, to be near where the Ark of God was, the place of his residence among men, in those days; where the Tabernacle, or Altars were, where daily sacrifices were offered, and other religious services, by Priests and Levites performed, and divers feasts and solemnities kept and observed. Besides what we have here in this Psalm to that purpose, read, Psal. 65.4. but especially, Psal. 84. *How amiable are thy Tabernacles, O Lord of Hosts? My soul longeth*, &c. And Psal. 42. *As the Hart panteth after the water brooks*, &c. And besides his own testimony in the Psalms, we may learn as much from divers passages of the history of his life. When the Ark was brought unto Zion, to be placed in that Tabernacle which he had prepared for it; how was he then transported with joy, even to the scorn and contempt of his own wife, 2 Sam. 6.20,21, &c. And in one of his flights from Saul, how bitterly does he expostulate his case with Saul? *If the Lord have stirred thee up against me let him accept an offering: but if they be the children of men, cursed be they before the Lord: for they have driven me out this day from abiding in the inheritance of the Lord; saying, Go serve other gods*, 1 Sam. 26.19.

Now to *seek the face of God*, may be understood two ways. More generally taken, it comprehends whatsoever is of the substance of true Religion: So we had it before, *This is the generation of them that seek him, that seek thy face, O God of Jacob*, Psal. 24.6. And Hos. 5.15. *I will go and return to my place, till they acknowledge their offence, and seek my face: in their affliction they will seek me early.* In this general sense, *to seek the face of God*, is no more, than what is more usually said, *to seek God*; as Isa. 45.19. *I said not unto the seed of Jacob, Seek ye me in vain: I the Lord speak righteousness, I declare things that are right.* A very common Scripture phrase it is, as cannot be unknown to them that are versed in it.

In a more strict sense, by *the face of God*, is meant the Ark of God, the place of his residence, and where (by some outward signs and evidences) he was wont, upon occasion, to make himself visible. So Psal. 42.2. *My soul thirsteth for God, for the living God: when shall I come and see the face of God?* or, *appear before the face of God*. So Moller, Bucer, Calvin, translate the words; (not as we there, who leave out the word *face*, in many places, indeed, sufficiently enough included in the word *before*; but in this place, a word of great pregnancy and importance, and therefore not to have been omitted) and so expound them directly, as intended by David, of the Ark and Sanctuary. See also Vatablus upon that verse, and upon the sixth verse of the same Psalm; where he tacitly retracts his own translation, (*apparebo coram Domino*; unadvisedly followed by ours) and makes it, *Quando veniam & apparebo ante faciem Domini?* It is well observed by one upon that place, (a very exact observer of Scripture words and phrases) that what 1 Chron. 13.10. is, *before God*; in 2 Sam. 6.7. is, *with the Ark of God*.

My opinion therefore is, that David at this time, when he wrote this, being deprived of this inestimable benefit (as he valued it) of God’s presence in his Sanctuary; (as it was with him when he wrote Psalm 42. and divers others) in the ardency of his zeal, pressing God with all motives and arguments that he could frame or think of, that God would restore him to that happiness, he so wanted and longed for; among others, he presses God with his own words, enjoining all men (in that sense we have showed) *to seek his face*; that is, to inform themselves diligently concerning his will; to apply themselves to his service; to trust in him, to depend on him, &c. a precept (in this general sense) in all places, and at all times, equally binding: but David, as though he could not serve God as he should, whilst excluded from this nearer approach; (as his words seem to imply, 1 Sam. 26.19. which have already been spoken of) and by consequence, not perform what he required: He therefore retorts upon him (as it were) his own words, and presses him for performance on his side. So the words (and that without any violence, or far-fetched construction) become very pregnant, and emphatical; which otherwise seem but flat and dilute. According to this interpretation, the words would rather be thus supplied; *Thou hast said*, (it is thine own word and speech) *Seek ye my face: my heart saith unto thee*, (it is the desire of my soul; thy command is become my request) *Thy face, Lord, will I seek*.

**V. 9. ***{put not thy servant away in anger}* As angry masters use to do, for every slight offence.

**V. 10. ***{When my father and my mother forsake me}* He magnifies God’s love towards his, which far passes the most tender love of parents towards their children, Isai. 49.15. The ground of such emphatical expressions, of trust and confidence in God, see upon Psal. 16.1. *for in thee do I put my trust*. The words might have been translated, *Though my father*, &c. as well as *When my father*, &c. but that is not very material; neither can any man infer from this, that David was forsaken, or that he was afraid when he so wrote, that he should be forsaken by his parents. For this is but a supposition; and such a supposition, as is not grounded upon any probability of the thing; but only serving to set out more emphatically David’s confidence in God’s love; and the greatness withal of that love in God, so far transcending all human love. As when the Apostle says; *Though an Angel from heaven*: Gal. 1.8. nay we ought rather to infer (if we must one of the two) that David did not think it possible; at least, that he had no suspicion at all that *his father and mother* would forsake him: and therefore does use this similitude, the better to set out the greatness of his confidence. Some Hebrew interpreters expound the words of David *his father and mother*, their natural deaths; as if he intended to say, that God would be instead of a father and a mother unto him. Indeed we find God styled elsewhere, *a Father of the fatherless*: as Psal. 68.5. and *a helper of the fatherless*, Psal. 10.14. but this is not so proper to this place, and takes off a great deal of the emphasis.

*{will take me}* Heb. *will gather me*. As Isai. 40.11.

**V. 11. ***{Teach me thy way}* Psal. 25.4. & 86.11. & 119. Such a course of life, as may please you, Psal. 25.9.

*{and lead me in a plain path}* That I may not stumble, or do anything that my watchful foes may lay hold on to hurt me, Psal. 26.12. Isai. 63.13.

*{a plain path}* Heb. *a way of plainness*.

*{mine enemies}* Heb. *those which observe me*.

**V. 13. ***{the goodness of the Lord}* To enjoy the good things God has promised me, before I die, Isa. 38.11. Psal. 116.9.

**V. 14. ***{Wait on the Lord}* Psal. 31.24. Isai. 25.9. Hab. 2.3. He exhorts himself to depend on the Lord, seeing he never fails to help his, according to his promise, Psal. 62.1,5.

# PSALM XXVIII

**Verse 1. ***{To me}* Heb. *from me*.

*{lest if thou be silent to me}* Psal. 83.1. It is noted here by some expositors, that in *silence*, forbearance of acting, or helping, is sometimes included. See Judg. 18.9. *and are ye silent?* (*still*, there in the English) and 1 King. 22.3. *Know ye that Ramoth*, &c. and so elsewhere. But this granted, I think it might have been observed withal, as not improbable, that David might here, by this expression, allude to the name of the Oracle, by him particularly mentioned in the very next verse; *When I lift up my hands towards thy holy Oracle*: or, *the Oracle of thy Sanctuary*. It was called דביר, (which is the word here:) as if you should say, *The place of speaking*: from the verb דבר which signifies, *to speak*. As for God’s being *silent*, when we call again and again: it is either because our prayers are not acceptable unto him, which may proceed from divers occasions: as 1 Sam. 8.18. *and ye shall cry out*, &c. and James 4.3. *Ye ask, and receive not*, &c. Or because he has a further aim for our good, and his glory: as, to make us examples of patience unto others: and the like. And whatsoever flesh and blood; that is, natural sense and judgment, suggest unto us, that God is μακροθυμῶν, *long*, very *long* sometimes, before he hears us, and execute justice: yet the Scripture says positively, he will do it, ἐν τάχει, *speedily*, Luke 18.7,8. because many years are but as a moment of time, to eternity: which therefore should always, in our sharpest extremities, or longest trials; to prevent the assaults of impatience upon our faith and confidence; be in our thoughts, and before our eyes.

*{I become like them that go down into the pit}* Psal. 143.7. He counts himself as a dead man, till God show his favor toward him, and grant him his petition.

**V. 2. ***{toward thy holy Oracle}* Or, *towards the Oracle of thy Sanctuary*. For in that place was the Ark, and there God promised to show the tokens of his favor, and thence he gave answers to his people, Num. 7.89. & 27.21. 1 King. 6.5. & 8.38. See before upon Psal. 5.7. *towards thy holy Temple*.

**V. 3. ***{Draw me not away with the wicked}* Psal. 26.9. Draw me not away by a violent death from worldly comforts, as wicked men die, but give me a quiet and willing death, as you used to do to good men, 1 Sam. 25.29. Psal. 52.5. and 125.5.

*{which speak peace to their neighbours}* Psal. 12.2. Jer. 9.8.

**V. 4. ***{Give them according to their deeds}* 2 Tim. 4.14. Revel. 18.6. He thus prays in respect of God’s glory, and not for his own cause, being assured that God would punish the persecutors of his Church. See more upon Psal. 35.4.

**V. 5. ***{Because they regard not the works of the Lord, &c.}* As the words have particular reference unto David his case, so, *the works of the Lord, and the operations of his hands*, must be understood of God’s counsel and purpose, to bring David to the Crown; manifested and brought about by many notable events, and particular providences and dispensations; as may appear by the story: which they that took no notice of at all, showed themselves (the proper temper, for the most part, of profane men and Epicures) very supine and negligent in things of high moment: they that did, and did not therein acknowledge the hand of God, showed themselves very obstinate, and inflexible; which kind of men are properly said, θεομαχεῖν, to fight against God; as opposing to the manifestation of his Will, and clear demonstrations of his Divine dispensation, the counsels of human wit and policy.
