To this purpose we read of Moses, Act. 7.25. *For he supposed his brethren would have understood, how that God by his hand would deliver them, but they understood not.* In a more general sense, they are said, (as Esay 5.12.) *not to regard the work of the Lord, nor to consider the operation of his hands*: who being taken up with other cares, as the ambitious and covetous; or excluding all care from themselves, and therein placing greatest happiness; neither of what happens to themselves, in the course of their lives; nor what happens unto others, either publicly, or privately thereby to understand the ways of God, and of his providence, make any observation at all; Who neither in adversity, nor prosperity, go beyond nature and common chance: so they live, and so they die. They also more particularly may be charged with those words, who are altogether for the present, and judge of men generally as good or bad, in favor, or out of favor with God, from their present condition, and what happens unto them in this world. Whereas it is a mystery of providence, to husband our salvation by contraries; and to let them have their wills in this world most, that have least of God’s favor. The Psalmist speaks of it, as of a great mystery, *O Lord, how great are thy works! and thy thoughts are very deep. A brutish man knoweth not, neither doth a fool understand this: When the wicked spring as the grass; and when all the workers of iniquity flourish; it is, that they shall be destroyed for ever*, Psal. 92.4,6,7. And again, *They that sow in tears, shall reap in joy. He that goeth forth and weepeth, bearing precious seed, shall doubtless come again with rejoicing, bringing his sheaves with him*, Psal. 126.5,6. But of God’s judgments also, which by a sudden alteration oft fall upon the wicked, even in this world, to the great astonishment and confusion of the beholders on the one side, and as great comfort and edification of others on the other side, the words may likewise be understood. For these also, these visible palpable temporal judgments are called *the work of God*. So Psal. 64.9. *And all men shall fear, and declare the work of God; for they shall wisely consider of his doing.* And so Prov. 21.12. *The righteous man wisely considereth the house of the wicked: but* (or, *for*) *God overthroweth the wicked for their wickedness.* See also Psal. 66.5. *Come and see.*

*{he shall destroy them}* They shall be utterly destroyed, Mal. 1.4. A metaphor from a house ruined for ever.

**V. 6. ***{Blessed be the Lord}* Because he felt the assurance of God’s help in his heart, his mouth was opened to sing his praises, Psal. 13.6. See before upon Psal. 22.22. *I will declare.*

**V. 8. ***{their strength}* Or, *his strength*. The strength of his people, verse 9.

*{saving strength of his anointed}* Heb. *strength of salvations*.

**V. 9. ***{bless thine inheritance}* God’s people are as dear to him, as our inheritance is to us.

*{feed}* Or, *rule*.

# PSALM XXIX

**Verse 1. ***{Give unto the Lord}* Psal. 96.7,8.

*{O ye mighty}* Heb. *ye sons of the mighty*. He exhorts the proud tyrants to humble themselves under God’s hand, and not to have such an opinion of their own power and greatness, as most men in high places have. This exposition I like very well, and somewhat the better, for that we read of so many great princes, both good and bad, but bad and cruel especially, that have been very fearful of thunder, the subject of this Psalm: as Augustus, Caligula, and divers others. But it will be objected; How then does David invite such *to worship God in the beauty of holiness*: (that is, in his Temple, his Sanctuary, at Jerusalem:) as in the next verse? Yea very well, if we take the words as an invitation not so much to solemn service in his Temple; as to the knowledge and acknowledgment of the God of that Temple, and of his divine Service, there performed: to which Temple and service such Kings and princes as had knowledge of the God of Israel, were wont to send great presents every year, and performed some duties by their deputies, in acknowledgment of his sovereignty.

However, the greater part of interpreters, by *sons of the mighty*, understand, some, the Israelites in general, so styled because of Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob: men of famous memory, to posterity; of great power in their lives, and of great credit with God, both living, and dead: others, the Angels, as Psal. 89.7. *For who in the heavens, &c. who among the sons of the mighty*, &c. and some, the stars of Heaven, as Job 38.7, *When the morning Stars sang together, and all the sons of God* (בני אלהים there, בני אלים here, in the Psalm: which may be understood of the same, because אל is often used for God:) *shouted for joy.* All things well weighed, it is not easy to determine, which interpretation is most genuine. If Angels, (according to the Chaldee paraphrase, and divers others) be spoken unto; by the *beauty of holiness*, (which is not so usual) we must understand the heavens. But to the general sense of the Psalm, to set out the power and majesty of God, and to assert his providence, (whereof see upon the third verse, *The voice of the Lord*:) any of these expositions may serve well enough.

**V. 2. ***{Give unto the Lord the glory due unto his name}* 1 Cha. 16.29.

*{the glory due unto his name}* Heb. *the honour of his name*.

*{in the beauty of holiness}* Or, *in his glorious Sanctuary*, Psal. 96.9. See upon Psal. 110.3. *in the beauty of holiness.*

**V. 3. ***{The voice of the Lord}* It was one of Epicurus his arguments against a God, and a providence ordering and disposing all things both in Heaven and Earth; that many things, which commonly are ascribed unto God, (as particularly thunder and lightning,) proceeded from natural causes. Hereupon his conclusion was, (as it was indeed the main end of all his philosophy, which in these Atheistical times is by some much endeavored to be revived, though never was anything, in the judgment of ancient wise Heathens, more foolish and senseless:) that all fear of God, and all Religion, was a groundless thing; *Caetera quae fieri in terris caeloque tuentur Mortales, pavidis cum pendent mentibus saepe Efficiunt animos humiles formidine Divum*, &c. So does Lucretius the poet, (a great abettor of Epicurus his doctrine) preface to his long discourse of the cause of thunder and lightning: and repeats it often, and therefore men should not fear, because it is an effect of nature. Which is, in very deed, a very senseless argument. For why may not the same things be ascribed unto God, as supreme; and yet acknowledged, to have more immediate and secondary (but subordinate unto the supreme) causes? And if, God as we teach, and the more sober heathens believed, be the author of nature; why less powerful, or less to be feared, in natural causes, or events, than in those things, that he brings to pass by a more immediate power?

Although therefore if we should take time to consider well of all particulars, there might somewhat be found perchance in the effects of thunder and lightning, that would pose a wiser man than Epicurus, or any that have written for him, to show a natural reason for;) though I do not hence conclude, that there is no natural cause, because not understood:) but however, granting that it is a work of nature, and that the cause is not hidden; yet nevertheless it does not follow, but that thunder after a more sublime way of contemplation, (by Anaxagoras, an ancient philosopher, of good account; and by Plato after him, much commended:) without any respect to the natural and secondary causes, may be ascribed to God absolutely, and styled, *The voice of the Lord*, or, *of God*: as it is here: *The noise of his voice, and the sound that goeth out of his mouth*, Job 37.2. *The voice of the Almighty God, when he speaketh*, Esay 30.5.

There is no question, but that Thunder and Lightning, though in that order and course of nature, (or series and connection of natural causes the one depending of the other) by Him at first established, it has its place and time; was nevertheless among other ends, to this end intended and appointed by God, to repress the insolencies of some, and to awaken the consciences of others, (of all, I do not say: God has his end in that too,) whom the consideration of his other works, and daily providence, cannot move to the fear, or acknowledgment of a superior Power and Deity. A very fit expression therefore it is of the Thunder, to call it *the voice of God*: and it were not amiss, (such is the dullness of most) if all natural things, had the Name of God (some such way as the name of God is added by the Hebrews to all great things, as, *a tree of God, a City of God*, &c. that is, *a great tree, a great City*, &c. and so in the New Testament, ἀστεῖος τῷ Θεῷ, *exceeding fair*, Act. 7.20.) insoluble, it being the chief end of all natural things, to put us in mind of God.

The Grecians also for some such reason, called all extraordinary great tempests, θεομηνίας. And the ancient Romans were wont to say, *Deus tonat, Deus fulgurat*, &c. for which afterwards (as now it is used) by an *ellipsis*, as they call it, of the Nominative, for more brevity was said, *tonat*, (for, *Deus tonat*) *fulgurat*, &c. Yet the Hebrews have a more proper word too, for Thunder: whence *Boanerges*, in the New Testament (according to the Greek pronunciation, for *Benerehem*,) is interpreted, *The sons of Thunder* Mark 3.17.

As for that other argument of Epicurus, (which whether he borrowed of the stage, or the stage from him, I know not: but an argument, it seems, among the profane atheistical rabble, of old, in great request;) *Quod si Jupiter, atque alii fulgentia divi Terrifico quatiunt sonitu caelestia templa, Et jaciunt ignes, quo cuique voluntas: Cur quibus incautum scelus aversabile cumque est, Non faciunt*, &c. that is, in effect, that if the gods had anything to do with the Thunder, &c. they should make use of it against sacrilegious perjured persons, and the like; and their habitations: and not suffer either any good and godly men to be struck, or their own Temples to be battered or inflamed by it: this is no more than what the Scripture, in another case, teaches, to wit, that *the sword devoureth one as well as another* (and in Uriah’s case it so fell out indeed:) 2 Sam. 11.25. which if we were to treat here of providence at large, we could show, no ways repugnant to providence, but very suitable to that Order by God at first, since the fall of men, established in the world; by which all things in this kind, must and ought to be tried: and not to be deemed contrary to providence, that is not contrary to that first Order. But enough has been said, to serve for this place; and more we may have occasion to say hereafter. See upon Psal. 37.

*{many waters}* Or, *great waters*.

**V. 4. ***{powerful}* Heb. *in power*.

*{full of majesty}* Heb. *in majesty*.

**V. 5. ***{The voice of the Lord}* The thunderbolt breaks the most tall and strong trees, and shall men think their power to be able to resist God?

**V. 6. ***{Sirion like a young unicorn}* Deut. 3.9. Called also Hermon. He means, the trees that grew on those hills, for thunder moves not hills, but an earthquake. So ver. 8,9. the wilderness, for the beasts there.

**V. 7. ***{divideth}* Heb. *cutteth out*.

*{divideth the flames of fire}* It causes the lightnings to shoot, and glide.

**V. 8. ***{of Kadesh}* In places most desolate, where God seems not to be present, such as Kadesh was, Num. 13.26.

**V. 9. ***{maketh the hinds to calve}* The thunder makes the hinds to calve suddenly for fear, though at other times they do it with difficulty. See notes on Job 39.1.

*{to calve}* Or, *to be in pain*.

*{discovereth the forests}* Makes the trees bare of leaves, or pierces the most secret places, or makes the beasts flee into their dens, and so the wood is bare of them, or, without them.

*{in his Temple doth every one speak of his glory}* The words of themselves, are plain enough: but the coherence is not so plain. David in the beginning of the Psalm, does exhort all men to glorify God, in general: and particularly, to glorify him *in the beauty of Holiness*, (or, *in the glorious sanctuary*; as the margin there:) that is, in his Sanctuary. Now in the conclusion he remembers the Temple again; (see upon Psal. 5.7.) whereby he may be thought to invite all men to become Jews, or Proselytes. Some conceive that this Psalm was appointed by David to be sung in the Church, (Temple, or Tabernacle) in time of great Tempests and Thunders; which is not unlikely. The Litanies used of old in Christian Churches, had such a beginning. But in reference to the words immediately before, part of this very verse, *and discovereth the forests*,) the Temple having some relation to the forest, (as built, in part, out of it, Psal. 74.5.) it may be thought that David having said that God glorifies himself by the terrible effects (among others) of the thunder among the trees of the forest; he presently adds, that God is glorified in his Temple, by the voices of men: as deprecating that former way of glorification, upon it. He might also allude to the loud instruments of music, (imitating, in some manner, the voice of the thunder) used in the Temple to praise God with, mentioned by David in many other places.

*{doth every one speak}* Or, *every whit of it uttereth, &c*.

**V. 10. ***{The Lord sitteth upon the flood}* To moderate the rage of the tempests and waters, that they destroy not all. Others think that he alludes to the flood in Noah’s time.

*{the Lord sitteth King for ever}* Psal. 10.16.

# PSALM XXX

## TITLE

*{A Psalm and song}* Where the musical instruments went before the voice, *a Psalm and Song*, (as it is here) in the inscription: where the voices of men went before the instruments, then *a song and Psalm*. This is the device of some Rabbins upon this title. But other Rabbins plainly confess they know no ground of this assertion, or which should be the cause of this difference. Indeed this observation concerning the different placing of the words, seems to be a mere quirk. But that the adding of the word *song*, (שיר) whether before or after, to the usual word *Psalm*, may imply somewhat of men’s voices more than was usual and ordinary, is not improbable. And here it might well be said that שיר is added of purpose (*A Psalm: a canticle*) to intimate, that this Psalm was made upon an extraordinary occasion of public joy, which seems to be the most proper use and signification of the word שיר according to that of the Prophet: *And I will turn the feasts into mourning, and all your songs* (השיריכם) *into lamentation*, Amos 8.10. and before in the same chapter, verse 3. *And the songs* (שירות) *of the Temple* (so I write it, *Temple*, not *Temples*, because I find it so in the original Hebrew: all English Bibles of the last translation, as many as I have seen, upon what ground I know not, exhibit it, *Temples*) *shall howl*: that is, shall be turned into howling. But because this will not hold always, though it would here very well, I dare not much rely upon it. The safest will be, with those Rabbins to acknowledge our ignorance.

*{at the dedication of the house of David}* Either when he first dwelt in it, Deut. 20.5. or, after Absalom had polluted it, which is more probable, more properly therefore a *purification*, than dedication: but that that purification of it, was a kind of new dedication.

**Verse 1. ***{Thou hast lifted me up}* Drawn me out of afflictions, as out of a pit.

*{for thou hast lifted}* This thankful return of praises unto God, for deliverances, and other benefits received, how acceptable unto Him; himself, by the Psalmist, professes: *Offer unto God*, &c. *And call upon me in the day of trouble: I will deliver thee, and thou shalt glorify me*, Psal. 50.14,15. And the Psalmist elsewhere earnestly calls upon all men for it: *O that men would praise the Lord for his goodness, and for his wonderful works unto the children of men*, Psal. 107. v. 8,15,21,31. It is his principal aim in all that Psalm, and therefore he often repeats it. So this *for* here of the Psalmist, must be understood, as a declaration of the ground, and subject of his present thanks, and *exaltation*, or *extolling* of God: not as a general ground, as if we were not bound to praise and extol him, but when he does us good: or rather, (for when is it, that he does not: since we *live, move, and have our being in Him*: Acts 17.28. and enjoy daily the benefit of his light, and the like) plies us with extraordinary occasions. All men, the very worst and atheistical of men, are moved (by mere instincts of nature) to lift up their hearts, and hands, (though they acquit themselves of it but very superficially, or superstitiously) for present mercies, and successes. But in time of distress and discouragement, to continue zealous (upon our former principles; and not in base compliance, for our advantage) in God’s service; to *bless God* for all, as well unpleasing, as pleasing; is the very touchstone of rooted and well-grounded religion, whereby God is most glorified.

*{hast not made my foes to rejoice over me}* You have not given me into their hands, for then they would have triumphed over me: and in triumphing over me, would also reproach you, as saying, *where is now thy God?* and, *He trusted in God*, &c. to the great prejudice of my righteous cause, and great scandal of the godly. Though the insulting of an insolent enemy may go to the heart of a good and godly man, such a one as David was; and so very lawful for us (even for our own sakes,) to pray, that God will not give them cause; yet that which in these cases we are bound most tenderly to prevent, is, the dishonor that is done to God, and the prevailing of impiety thereby.

**V. 3. ***{from the grave}* Meaning, that he escaped death most narrowly.

**V. 4. ***{O ye Saints of his}* חסידיו The word signifies them that show mercy liberally unto others. See more upon Psal. 86.2. *for I am holy*.

*{at the remembrance}* Or, *to the memorial*.

*{at the remembrance of his holiness}* Before his tabernacle.

**V. 5. ***{For his anger endureth but a moment}* Heb. *there is but a moment in his anger*. His corrections last but a while, but his favor lasts all our life long, Esay 54.7,8. But how does David say here, that God’s *anger endureth but a moment?* which may seem so contrary both to what he testifies of himself in divers places, (as where he says, *I am afflicted and ready to die from my youth up*, &c. Psal. 88.15,16. and elsewhere, that *his terrors have been upon him all his life time*, or to that purpose: and in the very next Psalm, that *his life is spent with grief, and his years with sighing*, Psal. 31.10.) and what both he, and other sacred authors do write of the generality of men, and of God’s people in particular, *For all our days are passed away in thy wrath; we spend our years as a tale that is told*: Moses in his song, Psalm 90.9,10, &c. as Job, and others, to the same purpose?

It may be said, that David spoke this rhetorically, the better to set out the greatness of his joy. For so our Savior compares the sorrows and afflictions of godly people in this world, to a woman’s travail in child-birth. *A woman when she is in travail, hath sorrow, because her hour* (or time of suffering) *is come: but as soon as she is delivered of the child, she remembereth no more the anguish, for joy that a man is born into the world.* Joh. 16.20.24. What is it to *forget*, but to think so little of it, whether for length of time, or present sense of grief, as if it were nothing in comparison? So Jacob’s seven years service seemed but *as a few days* unto him, says the Scripture, *for the love that he had unto her*, Gen. 29.20. David therefore might speak it not rhetorically only, but really, according to present sense and judgment, through excess of joy. And it shows withal the gentleness, humility, and ingenuity of his disposition, prone to make the most of God’s favors, and to judge of his severest chastisements with moderation: contrary to the course of professed Atheists, and Atheistical men, who carefully observe and set out with advantage whatsoever can with any color be objected against all providence, (whence is that wicked and malignant judgment of Tacitus in a place, that the gods regard not the piety, or integrity of men, to reward it; but take good notice of their wickedness, to punish it) but pass by so many obvious arguments, and evidences of it.

If we might understand David of inward spiritual comforts, such as God by his holy presence does afford oftentimes, (for always he does not) to his people in their greatest troubles and extremities, (of which see Esa. 57.15,16, &c. *For thus saith the High and lofty one*, &c.) there would be less difficulty in the words, because it is not likely that David, so godly a man as he was in his life and conversation, and so dear unto God, (though not without experience of some spiritual desertions, (as to present sense) as when he made Psal. 51. *Have mercy upon me, O Lord*, &c. where he so earnestly prays for his restitution:) yet not likely, I say, that he was in his lifetime either long, or often out of sense and fruition of God’s love: which he could not but prize above all worldly comforts and prosperities. But however, if this was not his chiefest aim, and most immediate sense, yet certainly in delivering this general sentence, he could not but have a respect, not only to those spiritual joys and comforts of a pious soul, even in this world, which we have spoken of: but also to those future, and eternal, whereof he speaks Psal. 17.15. *As for me, I will behold thy face*, &c. and Psal. 49.17. *But God will redeem my soul*; &c. To which also the Apostle does refer us for comfort in affliction, in words that have much resemblance to these of David’s, and may help well to give light unto them. *For our light affliction*, says he, *which is but for a moment*, (so David here, *but a moment:*) *worketh for us a far more exceeding and eternal weight of glory.* To which purpose, to confirm this sense of the words, the words of our Savior, of which we have already spoken, are very pertinent. For that joy, which he promised there unto his disciples, which should make them (so great and so powerful it would be) to forget all former sorrows, though it began after his resurrection, yet it was not to be fulfilled here, but in heaven: and even that joy which they enjoyed here, what was it, a great part of it, but an anticipation, through faith and hope, of that heavenly? as St. Peter teaches at large, *Blessed be the God and Father*, &c. *Wherein ye greatly rejoice, though now for a season if need be, ye are in heaviness through manifold temptations.* 1 Pet. 1.3,4,5,6.

*{weeping may endure for a night, but joy cometh in the morning}* Joy is ascribed to the morning, as adversity to the night; because the morning is, as it were, the reviving time after the death of sleep: and because it brings the light again (the emblem of joy) after darkness. So the poet; *Spes igitur superest*, &c. *Huc utinam nitidi Solis praenuncius ortus Afferat, admisso Lucifer albus equo.* It may be also, because the morning is the chief time of prayer; as Psal. 5.3. *My voice shalt thou hear in the morning, O Lord; in the morning will I direct my prayer unto thee, and will look up.* So also Psal. 46.5. & 143.8.

*{for a night}* Heb. *in the evening*.

*{joy}* Heb. *singing*.

**V. 6. ***{And in my prosperity, I said}* I put too much confidence in my quiet estate.

**V. 7. ***{thou hast made my mountain to stand strong}* I thought you had established me in Zion most surely.

*{made my mountain to stand strong}* Heb. *settled strength for my mountain*.

*{hide thy face}* Psal. 104.29.

*{and I was troubled}* After that you had withdrawn your help, I felt misery.

**V. 9. ***{What profit is there in my blood}* What honor will come to you, O God, if I die a violent death?

*{shall the dust praise thee}* Psal. 6.5. & 88.11. & 115.17. Esay 38.18,19. See upon Psal. 6.5. *For in death*.

*{shall it declare thy truth}* The truth of your promises, if they fail me.

**V. 12. ***{my glory}* That is *my tongue*, or, *my soul*. See notes on Psal. 16.9.

# PSALM XXXI

**Verse 1. ***{In thee, O Lord, do I put my trust}* Psal. 22.5. & 71.1. Esay 49.23.

*{deliver me in thy righteousness}* For then God declares himself just, when he preserves his, according as he has promised. See the Apostle too: *For God is not unrighteous to forget your work*: That is, his promise made to such as should work, &c. For otherwise, when we have done our best, we are taught to say that *we are but unprofitable servants*: Luk. 17.20. *For by grace ye are saved*, &c. *Not of works, lest any man should boast.* Ephes. 2.8,9. But this righteousness of God must not be confined to this present world: 2 Thessal. 1.5,6,7,8. *Which is a manifest token of the righteous judgment of God*, &c. We might also, take righteousness here, for mercy: as the word is often used in Scripture: both in the Old, and New Testament. In different respects, the same act may be an act of mercy, and of justice. *If we confess our sins, he is faithful and just to forgive us our sins.* To forgive, is an act of mercy: but to forgive, as it has relation to God’s promise, and Christ’s satisfaction, an act of justice.

**V. 2. ***{be thou my strong rock}* Though David made use of rocks in distress, yet he trusted not in them, but in God, Psal. 18.2,31.

*{my strong rock}* Heb. *to me for a rock of strength*.

*{defence}* Heb. *defence*.

**V. 4. ***{that they have laid privily for me}* Preserve me from the crafty counsels, and subtle practices of mine enemies.

**V. 5. ***{Into thine hand I commit my spirit}* God is the Creator both of body, and soul, or spirit: yet in a more particular manner of the soul, or spirit: *Then shall the dust* (or body) *return to the earth, as it was: and the spirit unto God, that gave it.* Eccles. 12.7. therefore styled, *the father of spirits*: Hebr. 12.9. We may therefore commit and commend our bodies unto God, who takes care of them too: but our souls, or spirits, especially: They are God’s chiefest care, and should be ours upon all occasions: *Wherefore let them that suffer, commit the keeping of their souls, as a faithful Creator.* 1 Pet. 4.9. but especially then, when at the point of death, ready to give up our ghost: according to Christ’s example: Luke 23.46.

**V. 6. ***{but I trust in the Lord}* Not in lying vanities, that is, idols, Jon. 2.3.

**V. 7. ***{thou hast known my soul in adversities}* Taken notice of it, and care for it, Psal. 1.6. as a true friend. These words may have relation to sundry deliverances afforded by God unto David, from which David might infer by the issue, that God did think of him, when he was in trouble, because he delivered him; and so by remembering what he had found before, might have better ground to hope well now; and thankful acknowledgment of it, might be some motive to God, to hasten to his present deliverance: Or, relation to these inward comforts, by which God did uphold him in his miseries: or may be an expression of David’s confidence in God (an argument of greatest faith and generosity; answerable to that of Job: *though he slay me, yet will I trust in him*, Job 13.15.) in his greatest extremities, when God (as to present sense and sight:) might seem utterly to have forgotten, and forsaken him, as when he said: *But I am poor and needy: yet the Lord thinketh upon me*: Psal. 40.17. See also upon Psal. 70.5. *make haste unto me*.

**V. 8. ***{thou hast set my feet in a large room}* Largeness signifies comfort, and freedom from difficulties, as straitness does sorrow and trouble, Psal. 4.1. & 18.19. and 119.33.

**V. 9. ***{Mine eye is consumed with grief}* Psal. 6.7. Meaning, that his sorrow and torment had continued a great while.

**V. 10. ***{my bones are consumed}* Psal. 32.3. & 102.3.

**V. 11. ***{I was a reproach among all mine enemies}* as if I had been smitten of God with some great judgment, Esay 53.4. 1 Cor. 4.9,13. Psal. 41.8.

*{especially among my}* Heb. *most of all to my*.

*{especially among my neighbours}* John 19.13. Psal. 38.11. & 88.8,18. Mine enemies had drawn all men to their parts against me, even my chief friends.

*{a fear to mine acquaintance}* They were afraid to show me any token of friendship.

*{fled from me}* Psal. 38.11. Matth. 26.56.

**V. 12. ***{as a dead man out of mind}* Psal. 88.4,5. Heb. *out of heart*: For things we love not are quickly forgotten.

*{like a broken vessel}* Like an earthen vessel broken, that cannot be mended, and is not regarded.

*{a broken vessel}* Heb. *a vessel that perisheth, or, perishing*; that is, very apt to be broken, both by its constitution, being very brittle; and for the little account, that men make ordinarily of such ware. A proverbial speech: *Vas testaceum*: For a thing of little worth, and easily destroyed. *The precious sons of Zion comparable to fine gold, how are they esteemed as earthen pitchers, the work of the hands of the Potter.* Lament. 4.2. I doubt therefore whether *Vas perditum*, as divers: or, *a broken vessel*, as we have it here, be so right to the sense of the original words; which rather import, *a vessel easy to be broken*. St. Paul (to whom some refer, for illustration:) his σκεύη κατηρτισμένα εἰς ἀπώλειαν, or, *vessels fitted to destruction*, is upon another consideration: to wit, of the different use: some, *to honor*; others, *to dishonor*: but, *of the same lump of clay*, both: and the same Potter’s: whereas, *potters vessels*, or, *earthen vessels*, here in general, from the matter, (brittle earth) is proverbial, for base, and contemptible.

**V. 13. ***{fear was on every side}* Jer. 6.25. & 20.4.

**V. 14. ***{But I trusted in thee}* Job 13.15,16.

*{Thou art my God}* I had this testimony of conscience, that you would defend mine innocence.

**V. 15. ***{My times are in thy hand}* Whatsoever changes come, you govern them by your providence: and no trouble can befall me before your appointed time, Joh. 6.30.

**V. 16. ***{Make thy face to shine upon thy servant}* Psal. 4.6. Num. 6.25.

**V. 17. ***{Let me not be ashamed}* Psal. 25.3.

*{let them be silent in the grave}* Or, *let them be cut off for the grave*. Let death destroy them, that they may hurt no more.

**V. 18. ***{grievous things}* Heb. *a hard thing*.

**V. 19. ***{O how great is thy goodness}* Esay 64.4. 1 Cor. 2.9.

*{which thou hast laid up for them that fear thee}* As men lay up treasures safe, so does God lay up good things for his children, though they do not always enjoy them here, 2 Tim. 4.8. Some interpret this *laying up*, as implying, that those good things are not always to be seen, and discerned by vulgar worldly men, who often by the troubles and crosses they see good and righteous men to undergo in this world, deem them to be out of God’s favor; wretched and miserable altogether, but *laid up* though, to be dispensed and revealed in due time: 1 Cor. 2.9. *Eye hath not seen, nor ear heard*, &c. This was the case of the elder son, in the Parable: Lu. 15.25,26. &c. who never offended his Father, and had reason to expect a greater share in his favor: yet himself thought otherwise, and was much troubled, when he saw how much his younger Brother (in outward show:) was made of. The Father’s answer then, tends to this, that his reward is *laid up*: he may be sure of it, may he but have patience: with which answer, he was satisfied. See more upon the next words.

*{before the sons of men}* That their innocence may appear by your miraculous deliverance of them. Some think that David’s words, here, of what God *hath laid up*, &c. by adding of these, *before the children of men*, are so far restrained to this life, that without violence they cannot be extended to eternal rewards. But I think that addition needed not be so far stretched: it is enough if it be granted, as the literal meaning of the words, (according to the condition of those times, and measure of light:) is of things temporal and visible. But his principal aim, (as I take it) was to set out the fullness and certainty of the reward, which godly men might expect at the hands of God: to which end, this clause, or addition, *before the sons of men*, is very proper and emphatical. No slight, imaginary, momentary business: but such as all men shall take good notice of, not therefore, against the principal aim; to expound the words of eternal blessedness, which alone can satisfy, and these very words, (though I press it not:) *before the sons of men*, may be expounded of that other world too: according to our Savior’s expression: Luke 13.28. *When ye shall see Abraham*, &c. See also upon the former words.

**V. 20. ***{shalt hide them}* Psal. 27.5.

*{in the secret of thy presence}* A place where they shall have comfort, and be safe from their foes. An allusion to Kings’ closets, which are sacred and inviolable in men’s account, Psal. 91.1. & 27.5.

**V. 21. ***{shewed me his marvellous kindness}* Psal. 17.7.

*{in a strong City}* Meaning, there was no city so strong to preserve him, as the defense of God’s favor.

*{strong city}* Or, *fenced city*.

**V. 22. ***{For I said in my haste, I am cut off from before thine eyes}* 1 Sam. 23.26. we read, *And David made haste to get away for fear of Saul.* The word in the Original, for *made haste*, is נחפז: the very word (there, a verb; here, a noun substantive,) that we have here: בחפזי *in trepidatione, (vel festinatione) mea; in my haste*. So that it is very probable, as by divers is observed, that David his words here have reference unto that time and occasion. But the word at large, *in praecipitatione mea*: for, in the haste and suddenness of my thoughts, occasioned by the greatness of my present danger, (as it was with him at that time, and at other times, more than once) I do not conceive that David’s purpose in these words, was so much to confess his sin, for which (as some would have it:) he deserved, by reason of his diffidence, or want of faith, utterly to be rejected by God, and forsaken: as to exaggerate or amplify the goodness of God towards him, who when he was in such extremity of danger, as in the eyes of man he could expect nothing but utter ruin; and in the greatest confusion of his soul; looked upon his distress, heard his cries, and beyond all probability, delivered him. What, though God had passed his promise unto him, which he knew, as to God, or on God’s part, could not fail: yet since most promises of God, in this kind, are conditional, (see 1 Sam. 2.30. *Wherefore the Lord God of Israel saith, I said indeed*, &c. and Ezek. 33.13. *When I shall say to the righteous, that he shall surely live*, &c.) why might not he in his humility mistrust sometimes, that he had made himself unworthy and uncapable? So 2 Sam. 15.25,26. the same David, when persecuted by his son Absalom, and forsaken by most of his people; *Carry back the Ark of God into the City*, said he; *If I shall find favour in the Lord, he will bring me again, and shew me both it and his habitation. But if he thus say; I have no delight in thee: Behold, here am I, let him do to me, as seemeth good unto him.* I do not read that David offended God with this humility; neither do I believe that he did. And perchance it was more seasonable, and more pleasing to God, than secure confidence would have been. And yet it does not follow, but that he had good hopes, even whiles he thus expressed his diffidence. Neither would the same Apostle, I think, have exhorted us to *work out our salvation with fear and trembling*, Phil. 2.12. and yet *to hold fast our confidence unto the end*, Heb. 3.6. & 14. if fear and confidence (in some degree) had been altogether inconsistent. But without any such deliberate consideration, take the words (if he uttered any such: for, *I said*, here, may as well, according to the Hebrew idiom, be expounded of his bare thoughts) as suddenly, and not without some passion, uttered by David in his extremity; yet even so, and in such a case, they may be excusable words. See before, upon Psal. 22.1. *My God, my God, why hast thou forsaken me?* I do not know that St. Paul intended the acknowledgment of any great sin, when he said of himself, 2 Cor. 1.8,9. that *he despaired even of life, and had the sentence of death in himself*: though St. Paul otherwise at that very time might have ground enough to believe, yea and to be sure too, that God had a further use of him, and therefore though his danger in appearance were never so great, yet that he should escape it. See also Psal. 77.7, &c.

*{I am cut off from before thine eyes}* Esay 38.11,12. Lam. 3.54.

**V. 23. ***{love the Lord, all ye his saints}* Psal. 34.9.

**V. 24. ***{Be of good courage}* Psal. 27.14. Be constant in your vocation, and God will confirm you with heavenly strength.

# PSALM XXXII

## TITLE

*{A Psalm of David, Maschil}* Or; a Psalm of David giving instruction.

*{Maschil}* Heb. *instructor*, Vers. 8. To give instruction out of his own experience, concerning remission of sins, and a godly life, verse 1,8,9. This is the common interpretation. But because this is a common title to many other Psalms, where this interpretation (the doctrine of this Psalm being of extraordinary consequence, and very needful to all that aspire to true happiness,) will not fit so well as it does here; it is therefore the opinion of others, which may be as probable, though not so commonly received, that this word *Maschil*, is the name of some peculiar harmony, to which this Psalm was fitted; or of some musical instrument.

**Verse 1. ***{Blessed is he whose transgression is forgiven, &c.}* These two first verses are cited by the Apostle, Rom. 4.7,8. and it is upon a special occasion, to confirm a doctrine of great consequence, by him delivered, to wit, that a man is not justified by works, but by faith. *Even as David also*, says he, *describeth the blessedness of the man, unto whom God imputeth righteousness without works; saying, Blessed are they whose iniquities are forgiven, and whose sins are covered. Blessed is that man to whom the Lord will not impute sin.* But might it not have been replied, that David as he does here describe the blessedness of man, unto whom God imputes righteousness without works; so elsewhere the same David describes the blessedness of the man, *that walketh not in the counsel of the ungodly*, Psal. 1.1. &c. and, *That is undefiled in the way; who walks in the Law of the Lord*, Psal. 119.1. where also this *Blessed*, as here in the words alleged by St. Paul, is twice repeated to the same purpose, *Blessed are they that keep his testimonies*, &c. and what is all this, but to describe the blessedness, according to St. Paul’s inference, of him that works? It will be said, perchance, that David’s purpose was to describe, as in those words alleged by St. Paul, Evangelical, so in these, Legal blessedness: herein differing, because the one grounded upon faith; and the other, upon works. But this though we allow of it, in due place; yet that David by those words intended the description of different blessednesses, we cannot allow. Our reason is, because this difference of words may be observed in St. Paul also, whose aim certainly was not to establish divers, sometimes Legal, and sometimes Evangelical, but one, and the same blessedness. What can be more plain, than what is at large, and with much vigor of expression, delivered by him upon this subject, Rom. 2.6,7, &c. That, *God will render to every man according to his deeds: To them who by patient continuance in well doing, seek for glory, and honor, and immortality, eternal life: But unto them that are contentious, and do not obey the truth, but obey unrighteousness, indignation and wrath, tribulation and anguish*, &c. as follows there, vers. 9. and 10. Store of other places there is, to the same purpose, which I forbear, because obvious enough. Hence we conclude, that it was not St. Paul’s purpose absolutely to deny, that blessedness came by good works also, which both David and himself elsewhere so clearly teach: but his aim here being to show the formal cause of blessedness; from this blessedness; or, to speak more properly, from this consideration of blessedness, he excludes works; faith and works being in this case opposite, which otherwise, in point of blessedness absolutely considered, are inseparable. And because as David describes blessedness by righteousness, and holiness of life; so also, by mere imputation of righteousness; upon which might plausibly be grounded this distinction of the formal, or efficient; and the instrumental cause of blessedness: he makes use of that passage which was to his present purpose, leaving it to his reader, by comparing of differing places, to distinguish, as we have done, and so to reconcile both (both David, and himself) unto themselves. We might also distinguish of works, which may be considered either as our own, that is, issuing from the power of our free wills, such as is left us since Adam’s fall; to which certainly no blessedness does belong: or, as the works of grace, and the effects of our spiritual regeneration: which properly be God’s works, because wrought in us, so far as good, by his Spirit; rather than ours: and in this sense the Apostle might well say, and that too, well according to David’s mind, that blessedness comes *by faith, without works*: as he there speaks. To this purpose he explains himself, Eph. 2.8,9,10. *For by grace are ye saved through faith*, &c. in which words he both excludes works; and yet says, that *we are created unto good works, in Christ Jesus*. Some Learned men are of opinion, that this Psalm was made of purpose, to be used as a *formula* of a general confession of sins, on the solemn day of Expiation, appointed Levit. 16. a day of great and public humiliation, as we read there. That it might fit well, upon such a day, and perchance was used afterwards, I will easily grant: but can hardly believe, by the tenor of the Psalm itself, that at first it was made for a public occasion. And the Jews tell us of other forms of prayer, which were used on that day. Some of them may be seen in Fagius.

**V. 2. ***{in whose spirit there is no guile}* Who has dealt sincerely with God in the confession of his sins, Prov. 28.13.

*{no guile}* John 1.47.

**V. 3. ***{When I kept silence, my bones waxed old}* While I hid my sin, I roared through pangs of conscience, verse 5. Psalm 22.1.

**V. 4. ***{drought}* Heb. *droughts*.

**V. 5. ***{I said, I will confess my transgressions unto the Lord}* Prov. 28.13. Esay 65.24. 1 Joh. 1.9. These words seem to import a very speedy deliverance, or quick return from God, upon David’s humiliation, and sincere repentance: *I acknowledged my sin: I said, I will confess*, &c. as if he meant to say, that he no sooner had made his confession to God, and humbled himself, &c. but he found the benefit of his humiliation: ἅμ’ ἔπος, ἅμ’ ἔργον, (*simul dictum, simul & factum:*) as the Grecians were wont to say. And so indeed it does often happen unto others too: God sometimes is pleased to hear, and to grant, as soon as we have faith and zeal to call. So sometimes: but it is not so always: neither must we expect it. See upon Psal. 38.18. *For I will declare my iniquity*.

**V. 6. ***{For this shall every one that is godly, pray unto thee}* Because you have pardoned my sins, upon my acknowledgment.

*{pray unto thee in a time when thou mayest be found}* Some render it, *tempore supplicationis*; and expound it, *de solemnibus & precandi horis*, of solemn standing prayers, both in the Morning and Evening. Whereof, see more in Bucer, and others. So of Cornelius in the Acts, it is noted particularly, that at the ninth hour of the day, that is, at the hour of public Evening service, (see Act. 3.1.) an Angel of God appeared unto him: And so of divers others we shall find it, that at those solemn times especially, God has been pleased to dispense his favors unto many. But however we expound it, or apply it, yet certain it is that in the original, the word is more general לעת מצא, *tempore inveniendi*; that is, *in a time of finding*, Or, *when thou mayst be found*, as we have it here.

*{surely in the floods of great waters}* By waters, great tribulations and calamities are often set out in the Scripture. So Psal. 69.2. *I am come into deep waters, where the floods overflow me.* And again, vers. 14,15. *Let not the water-flood overflow me*, &c. and Psal. 42.7. *Deep calleth after d.* The meaning, according to most expositors should be, that in time of greatest danger; and most threatening destruction, whether public or private, no evil shall reach them, that fly unto God by devout prayer for succor. See Psal. 91.2,3,4. &c. But the Hebrew word רק here translated, *surely*, is properly *adversativa*, as they call it, *particula*, importing some exclusion, or exception, extenuation, or limitation of, or from what has been said before. And so here it is rendered πλὴν, by the Septuagint; and accordingly, the words are expounded by some (which in very deed is a probable exposition) to this purpose; That there is a time, (expressed here by *floods of great waters*) when God will not be found; in opposition to that which went before, (in the same verse) *in a time when thou mayst be found*. The same words afford this sense, as naturally, as that which is more commonly followed: all the difference, is, in the different application of the relatives, *they*; that is, *the floods*, according to the ordinary exposition, but *every one that prays*, according to this: *shall not come nigh unto him*: to *the godly that prays*, that is, as commonly expounded: or, *to him*; that is, to God; as others, whom we now take into our consideration. Neither is the exposition, as we have already said, less plausible or warrantable, than the translation is. For that there is a time when God will not hear, though men cry, we are often told in the Scripture. See 1 Sam. 8.18. Isa. 59.2. and 55.6. Jer. 11.14. not to mention other places. And if the word *godly* be pressed; though that need not: for the sense will be perfect enough, if no more be made of the words than thus: That as David in his distress, whether temporal or spiritual, betook himself to prayer, and was heard; so may every godly man in the like case, encouraged by David’s example and experience, hope to speed; but provided, they come in due time: for *in the floods of great waters*, when haply extremity of distress, (or whatever else it be) may drive the most slack and obdurate, even against their wills, to that duty; then will praying be to no purpose; God (at such a time, and upon such an occasion merely) will not be found. They that embrace this interpretation, by *floods of great waters*, (a proverbial speech, as all agree) think the day of judgment, whether general or particular; and more particularly, the hour of death, or the last and worst days of our lives, (of which Ecclesiastes, *Remember now thy Creator in the days of thy youth, while the evil days come not, nor the years draw nigh, when thou shalt say, I have no pleasure in them*, Eccl. 12.1.) to be meant: or any such article of our lives, when a concurrence of several crosses and calamities (incidental to any) shall make us most uncapable of serious and hearty repentance. But however, granting that the word *godly*, must here also be supplied and understood; there may be a time found for them also; I mean a time, when God (in this sense, at least, in point of deliverance from temporal affliction) will not, even by the godly, be found. Of which see upon Psal. 11.3. *If the foundations be destroyed, what shall the righteous do?*

*{they shall not come nigh unto him}* The waters of great dangers shall not hurt him, Isai. 43.2.

**V. 7. ***{Thou art my hiding place}* Psal. 9.9. & 119.114.

**V. 8. ***{I will instruct thee}* David promises to make others partakers of his knowledge, and that he will diligently look, and take care to direct them in the way of salvation. See v. 1. Psal. 51.13.

*{I will guide thee with mine eye}* Heb. *I will counsel thee, mine eye shall be upon thee*.

**V. 9. ***{Be ye not as the horse or as the mule}* The one noted for his lasciviousness, and stubbornness, the other, for his stupor and dullness: or in general, be not as Brutes. The horse and mule, known beasts among men, and of common use; and therefore, say interpreters, rather named than any other. But here is an objection; the Jews were forbidden by the Law, to couple together creatures of different kind, Levit. 19.19. *Thou shalt not let thy cattle gender with a divers kind.* How then came mules (of themselves, and among themselves, as naturalists and experience teach, sterile;) to be known, or used among them? For that they were used, appears, 1 Kings 2.33. where we read of David’s own mule. It is answered, out of Aristotle, and Pliny, that there was a kind of mules in Syria, near Palestine, and sometimes including Palestine also,) which contrary to the nature of ordinary mules were fertile. But this is doubted by some, who think it was but in show; or that if any such thing happened, it happened rather as a prodigy, than by course of nature. But if the Jewish Gloss upon the Law, be true, and warrantable, and will agree with nature, it might be answered otherwise too: For they teach, that *beasts that are bred of divers kinds, if their dams be of one kind, it is lawful to let them gender together*. *As a mule*, say they, *whose dam is an ass, it is lawful to let him gender with a she mule, if her dam be an ass*. So that though the first invention, or contrivance of this kind of creatures (if I may so speak; for it is conceived they never were created:) noted in the Scripture, Gen. 36.24. were unlawful; yet the propagation of them (if any such be) in this kind, would be lawful.

*{lest they come near unto thee}* To kick, or to bite, that is. But the words, as is acknowledged, might as well be translated, *Whose mouth except it be held with bit and bridle, they will not come nigh unto thee*, That is, you shall not have the ruling of them, they will not be subject unto you. Some think בל קרב אליך is put here for בל קרב עליך (as indeed על and אל are often put one for another:) and because קרב, a noun substantive, is taken for *pugna, praelium*; they would have the verb also to bear that signification, and translate it, *lest they fight, or resist*. But the sense will be all one still, and therefore we shall leave it free to the reader, to follow what translation he likes best.

**V. 10. ***{Many sorrows shall be to the wicked}* Yea, and to the righteous too, Psal. 34.19. but of divers effects, and to different ends: See more there.

**V. 11. ***{Be glad in the Lord}* Psal. 64.10. Because God does compass his with mercy, vers. 10.

# PSALM XXXIII

**Verse 1. ***{Praise is comely}* The word notes a fair and comely grace, for which a thing is to be liked and desired, Psal. 93.5. & 147.1.

*{for the upright}* Praise sounds not well in the mouth of an hypocrite, Psal. 50.16. Or, there is nothing that better becomes a godly man, than to set forth the praises of God, the Creator of all things, preserver, life, and vigor: to seek all occasions of it, at home, and abroad; for particular, and for public mercies: temporal, and spiritual.

**V. 4. ***{the word of the Lord is right}* Psal. 19.7,8.

**V. 5. ***{The earth is full of the goodness of the Lord}* Psal. 119.64. He is bountiful to good men and bad, Matth. 5.45. 1 Tim. 4.10. yea, to the beasts, Psal. 36.6. and therefore all things in the world are called upon, to praise the Lord for his goodness, or mercy: as the word is there translated; (Psal. 136.1,2,3. &c. throughout the whole Psalm) being the very same (חסד) as is here: here also translated, *misericordia*, or, *mercy*, by some.

*{goodness}* or, *mercy*.

**V. 6. ***{By the word of the Lord were the heavens made}* Gen. 1.6,7. The Psalmist in divers hymns of this argument, wherein he does endeavor to set forth the praise of God, he falls upon the consideration of nature, and of the creature, as the most generally convincing argument against Atheists, who by reason of such confusions as appear often in worldly matters, and affairs, between man and man, denying God’s providence in the administration of the world: yea some, that he had created the world: some, the very being of a God. But though Providence may be obscure sometimes, by such events in the world, as may seem contrary to human reason, yea and to principles of faith, if men be not well grounded and experienced in the ways of God; yet still we have the light of heaven and earth; and in them the variety of so many objects both to delight and amaze our eyes, that we need no other evidence, if not extreme dull and stupid, to secure us, what disorders soever we see among men upon earth, that there is a God in heaven, by whose infinite power, wisdom, and goodness, all these things were so made and constituted, both for the instruction, and necessary use of man. Upon this foundation, we may afterwards more easily (the weaker sort of men I speak of) and by degrees admit other points of our belief, which are liable, (to weak judgments) to more objections. See Rom. 1.20. *For the invisible things*, &c. and Acts 14.17. *Nevertheless, he left not himself without witness*, &c. See also Psal. 74.16. *The day is thine*, &c.

**V. 7. ***{He gathereth the waters of the sea together}* He lays up the waters in deep places, that they may not drown the earth, Gen. 1.9. Job 26.10. & 38.8,16. Prov. 8.28,29. See before upon Psal. 24.2. *upon the Seas*.

*{he layeth up the depth in storehouses}* Or, *he layeth them up in the store-houses of the deep*.

**V. 8. ***{Let all the earth fear the Lord}* Great reason, which even common sense will prompt, that all people and nations should not only praise, and glorify; but also fear, and worship him, whose eternal power, wisdom, Majesty, and goodness does so clearly appear by his works, both in themselves considered, and with reference to mankind. *Hear now this O foolish people*, &c. *Fear ye not me, saith the Lord*, &c. Jerem. 5.21,22.

**V. 9. ***{he spake, and it was done}* Psal. 148.5,6. See upon Psal. 68.28. *Thy God hath commanded*, &c.

**V. 10. ***{The Lord bringeth the counsel of the heathen to nought}* Esay 19.3. No counsel can prevail against God, but he defeats it, and it shall have an evil success.

*{bringeth, &c.}* Heb. *maketh frustrate*.

**V. 11. ***{The counsel of the Lord standeth for ever}* Prov. 19.21. Esay 46.10. Shall take effect, whatsoever men plot to the contrary, Esay 14.24,27. See Acts 5.38,39. *for if this counsel be of men*, &c. *But if it be of God*, &c. where also they are termed θεομάχοι, *God’s rebels*, or *open enemies*, who endeavor by human policy, or power, to frustrate his known counsel. But to know what is God’s counsel, God’s revealed word must be our direction; in all such cases, at least, and causes, that can be determined by it. In some extraordinary cases, that have no known rule, nor reducible to such, we may ascribe somewhat to human wisdom, and to external proofs, and evidences; so still care be taken, that nothing be admitted against God’s revealed will. In this case, they that pretend to new lights, and new directions (contrary to what is revealed in the word:) by private inspirations, are no better than professed Atheists, that openly set themselves against God, and his sacred word.

*{to all generations}* Heb. *to generation and generation*.

**V. 12. ***{Blessed is the Nation whose God is the Lord}* Psal. 65.4. & 144.15. He shows that all our felicity stands in this, That God is our God by covenant, Gen. 17.5. Exod. 19.5.

**V. 13. ***{The Lord looketh from heaven}* And 2 Chron. 16.9. *For the eyes of the Lord run to and fro throughout the whole earth*, &c. From this and other like places may their error be reproved, who as though God himself took no notice of the actions of men, of particular men at least, would have Angels, or some such inferior powers to discharge that office, and to be as it were God’s informers in worldly businesses. Ancient heathens, both philosophers, and others (Plautus at large, in one of his Prologues:) have written much to that purpose: And this also, or much to this purpose, to have been the opinion of the ancients long before Christ, both Jews and others, Mercerus upon Job 33.23. *If there be a messenger with him*, &c. would have us to believe: nay that it is the doctrine of the Scripture itself. I would do him no wrong: neither to him, nor any other, by mistaking or mis-relating: but not to him especially, to whose great labors upon the Scriptures, to make them more plain and intelligible unto us, we are so much beholding. His words are: *Solet Deus Angelorum ministerio ad hum. rem uti, qui hominum preces & negotia ad se referant & perferant.* That God does use frequently the ministry of Angels, to carry his messages unto men, and for executioners of his will, in general, is sure enough by Scripture evidence: but that he uses generally, (as his words seem to import) the ministry of Angels to convey unto himself their prayers, and to make report of their actions, I think is not agreeable to the Scriptures. Somewhat perchance may be drawn from some one place or two, to show that it is done sometimes: but that it is God’s ordinary practice (if we may so speak) and settled course to do so, is contrary, as I conceive, to the tenor of the Scriptures: and particularly, very contrary to David’s opinion, as may appear by so many passages of these his Psalms. But I will not insist longer upon it here. It may much abate of our zeal to God by immediate addresses, (wherein a godly man’s comfort does mainly consist:) and dispose us to superstition, in seeking to Saints and Angels, if we be not fully persuaded, that God does immediately both hear and see. See also upon Psal. 38.9. *Lord all my desire*, &c.

**V. 15. ***{He fashioneth their hearts alike}* He that made all their hearts, knows all their wicked enterprises, Psal. 94.9,10,11. See more there.

**V. 16. ***{There is no King saved by the multitude of an host}* The intention of the Psalmist is not to deny, but that the issues of wars prove often according to secondary means and causes: which common experience does approve, and upon which, human providence grounding, seems rather to be allowed, than reproved by our Savior, Luke 14.31. *Or what King going to make war*, &c. We know that David himself, when occasion was, made preparations for war, as other Kings are wont in the like case; and made use of secondary means, according to the rules of human prudence: which if he had not; (without a special warrant and commission from God, to that purpose; whereof we have divers examples in Scripture:) his confidence would have proved rather a tempting of God, than a godly confidence. All he intends is but this, that no secondary means; no multitude of men, or horses, and the like, can do anything, where God has otherwise determined it: and therefore that though means be used, and had, as good as may be; yet is God, that must be trusted and thought upon for the issue: and in case means cannot be had, such as our present case may seem to require; and there be a necessity of fighting; then we should, (but without presumption) trust to God, and the goodness of our cause; to God, I say, who can save with few, as well as with many: as well without, (if he please) as with means. It is the manner of the Scriptures, to utter many things absolutely, for the greater emphasis; which must be understood comparatively, or conditionally; as for example, *For I desired mercy, and not sacrifice*, that is more than sacrifice; as in the next words is expressed, *and the knowledge of God, more than burnt offerings*, Hos. 6.6. *If any man come unto me, and hateth not his Father and Mother*, &c. Luk. 14.26. that is: *He that loveth Father or Mother more than me*, &c. Matth. 10.37. See also upon Psal. 38.4. *mine iniquities*, &c.

**V. 17. ***{An horse is a vain thing for safety}* Prov. 21.31.

**V. 18. ***{the eye of the Lord is upon them that fear him}* Job 36.7. Psal. 34.15. 1 Pet. 3.12. God shows that toward his of his mercy, which man by no means is able to compass.

*{them that fear him}* Psal. 147.11.

**V. 19. ***{keep them alive in famine}* Job 5.20. Psal. 37.19. See there upon verse 25. *I have been young*, &c.

**V. 20. ***{Our soul waiteth for the Lord}* Psal. 62.1,5. & 130.6. Thus he speaks in the name of the whole Church, which depends only upon God’s providence.

# PSALM XXXIV

## TITLE

*{Abimelech}* Or, *Achish*, 1 Sam. 21.11. His name was Achish; but it seems Abimelech was a common name to those kings, as Pharaoh was to the kings of Egypt.

This is one of the Alphabetical Psalms, of which see upon Psalm 25, the first of that kind.

**Verse 1. ***{At all times}* See, 2 Thess. 1.3. and 2.13, πάντοτε, *always*; or, *at all times*, and so of prayer: *Pray without ceasing*: 1 Thess. 5.17. not that we should do nothing, but pray: but *always*; or, *at all times*, when it should be done: when the performance of it is seasonable; and the omission, or intermission of it, an effect of our want of zeal, or thankfulness.

**V. 2. ***{the humble shall hear thereof}* They that are beaten down with the experience of their own evils. Or, *the meek*: as the same word (ענוים) may as strictly be translated: intending thereby, the faithful. Meekness how inseparable from true piety, see, Luke 18.2. &c. *Jesus called a little child*, &c. and, Jam. 13,14. &c. to the end, *Where is a wise man, and endued with knowledge among you*, &c. They are called, *poor in spirit*: Matth. 5.3. *Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of God*, and those there, called *Meek*, (verse 5. *Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth:*) are meant, the gentle, and peaceable (in their ordinary conversation: not violent, or contentious: such as are described v. 12,13,14. of this Psalm: *What man is he*, &c. Such, (though they be not very religious otherwise; but much more, if religious:) have a particular promise of earthly blessings.

*{be glad}* That God has preserved me for the kingdom.

**V. 4. ***{and delivered me from all my fears}* Which I conceived, for the dangers wherein I was among the Philistines, Heb. 5.7.

**V. 5. ***{They looked}* Or, *They shall look, and shall be enlightened, and their faces shall not be ashamed*: So most, and it is very usual, in the Hebrew, as all know, to put Preterits for Futures.

*{They looked unto him}* God: They shall dare to lift up their eyes unto God, being now revived and encouraged by his gracious dealing with me, Psal. 40.12. *innumerable evils have compassed me—I am not able to look up*. Or, *they shall look unto him*, that is, David himself, speaking of himself in the third person: as elsewhere, sometimes. *Aspicient eum. They shall see him*; how God has dealt with him. So Psal. 107.42. *The righteous shall see it, and rejoice.* See on the next verse, Isa. 60.5.

*{were lightened}* The proper signification of the word (נהרו) is; *They shall flow together*. So Isa. 2.2. and 60.5. *They shall see, and flow together*, &c. And because they that flow together, use to make haste, some render, *festinabunt, accelerabunt*: but what need? The sense here followed, *were lightened*, is taken from the Chaldaic use of the word. The sense is good, so too: but we need it not here, since the more proper will do us well, and is embraced by most.

**V. 6. ***{This poor man cried}* These are the words of the humble raising themselves up by the consideration of God’s favor showed to David.

**V. 7. ***{The angel of the Lord encampeth round about them that fear him}* It is well observed here by some, that it is an idiom of the Hebrew tongue (though the like may be observed in other tongues too, sometimes:) to put singulars for plurals: as tree, for trees: Psal. 105.33. *brake the trees* (Heb. *tree:*) *of their coast*. So, frogs; caterpillar, locust, Psal. 78.45,46. for frogs, &c. and Psal. 8.8. *the fowl of the air, and the fish of the Sea*. Here therefore, because encamping in ordinary construction, does presuppose a multitude; it is very likely that by Angel, an host of Angels is intended. We have it in the plural, Psal. 99.11. *For he shall give his Angels charge over thee*, &c. Though God’s power be sufficient to govern us, yet, for man’s infirmity, he appoints his Angels to watch over us, as soldiers encamped about us, Psal. 91.11. Heb. 1.14.

**V. 8. ***{O taste}* Make trial of his bounty, by seeking to him for relief,

*{O taste and see that the Lord is good}* 1 Pet. 2.3.

*{blessed is the man that trusteth in him}* Psal. 2.12.

**V. 9. ***{O fear the Lord}* Worship him according to his will, with reverence and awful dread, Heb. 12.28.

*{O fear the Lord ye his Saints}* Psal. 31.23.

*{there is no want to them that fear him}* So Christ: *Seek ye first the Kingdom of God, and his righteousness, and all these things shall be added unto you.* Math. 6.33. *For Godliness is profitable unto all things*, &c. 1 Tim. 4.8. All which how to be understood. See upon Ps. 37.1. *Fret not thyself*.

**V. 10. ***{The young Lions do lack}* The Godly that trust to God, even the poor and needy, may promise themselves more content (which is accounted great riches:) in their estate, whatever it be, than great men, that trust in their wealth. *For not by bread only*, &c. Deut. 8.3. & Math. 4.4. And, *The sleep of a labouring man*, &c. Eccles. 5.12.

**V. 11. ***{Come ye children}* Which acknowledge me for a spiritual father and teacher, Prov. 2.8. & 10.15.

*{I will teach you the fear of the Lord}* Their whole duty to God, v. 9. Eccl. 12.13.

**V. 12. ***{What man is he that desireth life}* 1 Pet. 3.10. Seeing all men naturally desire life and felicity, but of the right means, to true solid happiness, is the question. It is that, that has exercised wise men and philosophers in all ages. But they could never agree in their opinions; some maintaining one thing, and some another. It is the word of God alone that can resolve the question. See more upon Psal. 1. upon the inscription of it, *Psalm first*.

*{see}* Or, *enjoy*, Jer. 17.8.

**V. 14. ***{Depart from evil}* Psal. 37.27. Isai. 1.16,17.

*{and pursue it}* Take as much pains to do well, as hunters do to catch the prey, Heb. 12.14.

**V. 15. ***{The eyes of the Lord are upon the righteous}* Job 36.7. Psal. 33.18. 1 Pet. 3.12.

**V. 16. ***{The face of the Lord is against them that do evil}* His open anger, Levit. 17.10. Jer. 44.11.

*{to cut off the remembrance of them from the earth}* God’s anger not only destroys the wicked, but also abolishes their name forever, Psal. 37.35,36. Prov. 10.7.

**V. 17. ***{The righteous cry, and the Lord heareth}* Spoken of, v. 15. And if the sixteenth verse had a parenthesis about it, it might be read, *they cry*: or, *the broken-hearted cry*, vers. 18. for so the antecedent is taken out of the verse following, Psal. 87.1,2.

*{and the Lord heareth}* See upon Psal. 38.18. *For I will declare*. and Psal. 41.1. *The Lord will deliver him*.

**V. 18. ***{unto them that are of a broken heart}* Psal. 51.17. Isai. 57.15. Heb. *to the broken of heart*.

*{that are of a broken heart}* When they seem to be swallowed up with afflictions, then God is at hand to deliver them.

*{such as be of a contrite spirit}* Heb. *contrite of spirit*.

**V. 19. ***{Many are the afflictions of the righteous}* And of the wicked too, Psal. 32.10. The difference is; God sends them to the righteous, and them he takes more special care of, as either cures, or preservatives from spiritual evils, which would occasion far greater mischief. For such is the nature of man; so frail, so fickle and mutable, even in the best, that without this continual discipline, or exercising, by outward crosses, and sometimes inward temptations and trials, it is apt to degenerate, and to do worse. See Heb. 12.6,7,8, &c. And it makes also to the purpose, that ancient Grecians called intemperate, incontinent men, ἀκόλαστοι; as if a man should say, undisciplined, or unchastised men, as the word is expounded by Aristotle in his Ethics. David himself found it so, and ingenuously does acknowledge it, Psal. 119.67. *Before I was afflicted*, &c. and vers. 71. *It is good for me that I have been afflicted*, &c. Therefore Saint James would have us to *rejoice when we fall into many temptations*, or trials, Jam. 1.2. which nevertheless we ought, (as taught by Christ) to pray against, because as we must not confide in our own strength, so neither presume (which were to tempt God) upon the help of God. Pride and security are the worst of evils. So that the joy required and enjoined, must be rather when it is past and over, than in the time of suffering, Heb. 12.11. though, even then, not without some spiritual joy and comfort, Isai. 57.15. As for the wicked, or ungodly, it is otherwise; because they have no such opinion of God, nor are acquainted with the mysteries of his divine dispensations, their afflictions prove right afflictions, and either make them, through impatience, to murmur and repine: or if through a kind of natural stoutness and fortitude, they bear them patiently, they are apt to grow proud thereupon, and self-conceited: a worse disease than the afflictions themselves. But however they be borne, either more or less patiently, (for we know of some heathens, that have boasted, and made show of joy in their misery:) without reference to the end, eternal happiness in another world, and the good use that is to be made of them to that end; it is not the conceit of man, that can change the nature of the thing.

**V. 20. ***{He keepeth all his bones}* And, as Christ says, all the hairs of his head, Luk. 12.6,7.

*{bones}* Joh. 19.36.

**V. 21. ***{Evil shall slay the wicked}* Evil of punishment shall destroy wicked men, though not good men, vers. 20. or they shall perish for their sins, Psal. 94.23. & 145.20.

*{shall be desolate}* Or, *shall be guilty*.

**V. 22. ***{the Lord redeemeth the soul of his servants}* When they seem to be overcome with great dangers, then God delivers them.

*{and none of them that trust in him, shall be desolate}* Or, *shall be guilty*; as verse before, in the margin. Elsewhere it is, and more commonly so; *shall not be ashamed*: as Psal. 25.2. and Esay 49.23. Sometimes, *to be ashamed and confounded*, are put together, as Psal. 35.26. and 40.14. I note this variety, because Esay 28.16. where we have it, *He that believeth, shall not make haste*: Some would have יבוש (*shall be ashamed*) as Esay 49.23. to be the true reading, and not יחוש, as there translated. Which seems the more plausible, because of the affinity of the Original words, being both, as equal in number of letters; so, almost, of one sound. But by comparing several places, as we have done, it appears, that the same sense, or sentence, (as to the main substance) is sometimes set out in the Scripture with some variety; and that bare affinity of words, is no sufficient ground, to make us suspect a place. For though it may be said of Esay 49.23. and 28.17. that there is great affinity in the Original words; yet the same cannot be said of this, and other like places, where there is no such affinity of words, though no less variation of sense.

# PSALM XXXV

**Verse 1. ***{Plead my cause}* Psal. 43.1. & 119.154. Lam. 3.58. He desires God to undertake his cause, against them that did persecute him, and to plead it with the sword, as 1 Sam. 25.38,39.

**V. 2. ***{Take hold of shield}* Albeit God can with his breath destroy all our enemies, yet the Holy Ghost attributes to him these outward weapons, to assure us of his present power.

**V. 3. ***{say unto my soul}* Assure me that you will deliver me out of these troubles.

**V. 4. ***{Let them be confounded and put to shame that seek after my soul}* From this verse, divers Imprecations follow; and there be divers Psalms of the same argument, where David not content with ordinary terms, as, *let them be confounded*, &c. which with a little help of a charitable construction might pass: but with exquisite forms and successes, (the like whereof are to be found in profane writings also) does devote his enemies to the utmost of misery that his phrase could reach. Of all these in general, somewhat shall now be said; once here for all. Whether private revenge were forbidden by the law or not, divers have been the conceits and apprehensions of men about it. But first of all, to clear the terms that we are to use: by private revenge we do not understand a legal prosecution of one’s right, against unjust usurpation, or oppression; which indeed seems, even this also, to be forbidden to Christians: but seems only, as by Learned expositors is sufficiently cleared: by private revenge, we understand an alienation of mind, or bearing of malice, watching any opportunity to do mischief by way of requital; upon supposition, whether true or imaginary, of an injury done to us. That private revenge in this sense, and such a malicious vindicative mind, was forbidden by the law, though, of old, otherwise interpreted by the Jews, has already been said and showed, upon Psalm 26.6. *so will I compass thine altar*. Which may be further confirmed by that of Job, where he professes his innocence from sins of an high nature, as idolatry, &c. *If I rejoiced*, says he, *at the destruction of him that hated me, or lift up my soul when evil found him*, Job 31.29. See upon Psal. 41.10. *that I may requite them*. Not to speak of Prov. 22.22. and other places there to that purpose, because written, or at least collected, since David. Neither is the law of God, contrary to the law of Nature herein, if rightly considered. The way that some ancient philosophers have gone, to prove that revenge was against Nature, is by showing that it is against the common good; which they hold every man, as he is a rational sociable man, is naturally bound to prefer before his own. But this is of an higher contemplation. In a more plain way, among Christians, at least, as I conceive, the same may be done. For granting that every man, by nature, is to love himself best, and to prefer his own good, before any others: (wherein religion and reason agree well enough, if in the word *love* there be no mistake; that is, that we take not love of the body, and of this present world, for love of the soul, and of eternity,) and that absolutely, or generally; it is lawful, as it has always been held a dictate of nature, *Vim vi repellere*; and by consequence, to requite evil for evil, lest by bearing one injury, we expose ourselves to a second: yet this must be granted also, and it necessarily follows upon that first principle, That *E duobus malis minimum*: of two evils nature does prompt to make choice of the least. Upon this very principle of nature the Epicureans (as may be seen in Dio. Laertius, and others) who pretended nature in all things, taught that we should forbear many pleasures, to which nature does lead, not as pleasures, but as the cause of greater evils. Hereupon it will follow, that as many as believe, that there is a reward of good to them that keep the Commandments of God, either in this, or after this life, of far more valuable consideration, than the pleasure, or profit of revenge can be; as also a reward of evil to them that do not, much more to be feared, than any evil, or inconvenience that can come to us, by neglect of revenge, and forgiving of injuries: it will, I say, upon these grounds of nature necessarily follow, that such as love their own good, by nature; and as rational creatures (which is the proper nature of man, by which he differs from brutes; reason) know, that they are not only to provide for the present, but for the future also, and accordingly to make their choice, as they see cause: that such, I say, upon grounds of reason and nature, must and will rather neglect and pass by injuries done to them, by men, than to incur the wrath of God, who has (in the New Testament, we are sure) so strictly forbidden and inhibited all private revenge, and bearing of malice, upon no less penalty, than the loss of eternal life. But how then comes David, a man of God, nay, *according to God’s own heart*, 1 Sam. 13.14. and of his own temper and disposition, as may be gathered by divers circumstances, meek and mild; so freely to indulge to passion (a man would think) even to dreadful curses and imprecations? It is commonly said, that what he utters in this kind, was uttered by him rather as a Prophet, and God’s Minister, than as a party, and out of any private passion. Again, that David had not a respect to himself so much, if at all, as to the glory of God: the punishment of the wicked much conducing to the promoting of religion, and repressing of wickedness and vice among men. That David uttered many of these imprecations as a Prophet, immediately moved by God, without any respect to himself, but with a respect to God, and his glory, I verily believe it. Certainly, many of those imprecations, with some alteration of the tenses, (which in the Hebrew tongue is ordinary enough) for the Optative, or Imperative, being put in the Future; of imprecations would become mere prophecies. And so we find some of them expressed, as Psal. 21.8,9. *Thine hand shall find out*, &c. *Thou shalt make them a fiery oven*, &c. But whether all, without any mixture of any human passion, or any respect to himself at all, (as is commonly affirmed) I cannot so easily be persuaded. This I observe, that David, in most of those imprecations, pitches upon one kind of men, of all men in the world, both to God and men, most hateful: rather Devils, indeed, than men; for whom whether it be lawful, or no, to pray, I almost make a question: not that I think their sin to be the sin against the Holy Ghost; but of all sins, the most inhuman, and against common nature, and which by God himself (as shall appear) uses to be resented and punished in a peculiar manner. It is the sin of uncompassionateness, or irrelenting cruelty, when men taking the advantage of others’ misery, pursue vengeance to the utmost, and still the lower their enemy is, and the more uncapable to resist, the more their rage and malice increases against him. This is much: but there is a degree beyond this, when a man does this to another, not out of any revenge, or provoked by any wrong or injury, either real, or imaginary: but of pure cruelty, and pleasure that he takes in doing mischief, and to see others in misery. And yet beyond this too, there is a degree, when a man does this, not to his enemy; nor to them, who have done him no wrong; but to his quondam friend, yea and benefactor. But let us hear David himself, how he describes them. In the 99. Psalm, after dreadful imprecations, as any be in the whole Book, *Because*, says he, *that he remembered not to show mercy, but persecuted the poor and needy man; that he might even slay the broken in heart*. So Psal. 69.23. *Let their eyes be darkened*, &c. 24. *Pour out thine indignation upon them*, &c. *Let their habitation be desolate*, &c. *For they persecute him whom thou hast smitten; and they talk to the grief of those whom thou hast wounded*; verse 26. See also upon Psal. 55.19. *because they have no changes*. And is it not so here in this Psalm we are now upon? ver. 12. &c. *They rewarded me evil for good, to the spoiling of my soul.
