But as for me*, &c. *But in mine adversity they rejoiced, and gathered themselves together*, &c. A man would wonder, that the nature of man should be capable of such savageness: Though if poets may be credited, they are but the basest of wild beasts neither, that are of that nature. *Corpora magnanimo satis est prostrasse leoni*, &c. *At lupus, & turpes instant morientibus ursi: Et quaecunque minor nobilitate fera est.* And again, *Nec mala te reddunt mitem, placidumque jacentis Nostra quibus possunt illachrimare ferae?* But the experience of all ages is above all reasoning. But let us see what God himself does think of such, and what account, He whose judgment must be our rule and warrant, would have us to make of them. I will pass by divers passages of the Prophets where dreadful judgments are denounced against several nations for this one thing, (though not in that only sinful, it is likely) because they insulted without mercy, at the fall and ruin of their enemies. There be divers in that kind. I will ground my observation upon wise Solomon his words and observation to this purpose, which well deserves to be considered of. He gives counsel how a wise man should carry himself, when his enemy is brought low, if not quite lost, by the hand of God. *Rejoice not*, says he, *when thine enemy falleth, and let not thine heart be glad when he stumbleth: Lest the Lord see it, and it displease him, and he turn away his wrath from him*, Prov. 24.17,18. In which words, the opposite term, *in te*, (*from him, upon thee*) must of necessity be supplied, as it is well supplied by some translators in the very Text, (as Junius for one) and by some Rabbins, in their annotations upon the place. It must be so, except a man can think, that Solomon’s intention was, to give counsel, how to prevent, lest by our means the wrath of God against our enemies should be allayed: which to think, were as absurd, as it is uncharitable. See also upon Prov. 25.21,22. *If thine enemy be hungry*, &c. where we shall show how much some are mistaken in the sense of those words. Now if Solomon were in the right, and mistook not in his judgment of God’s nature, let any body judge what a sin this must be in the eyes of God, this insulting, this rejoicing, at the calamity of the distressed and afflicted, which alone can make an innocent of a sinner, and sinner of an innocent; by making him an object of compassion, who before was an object of wrath; and him an object of wrath, who before, but for this unmerciful insulting, was of favor? I know not whence they had it, but this I know, that ancient wise heathens had the very same apprehensions of God’s nature, in this particular, as wise Solomon here expresses; and that they speak of it in the very same terms almost, as Solomon does. Since therefore, both by the testimony of Solomon, and by the common apprehension of men, none are more subject to the curse of God, none more detestable to him, than such kind of men; there is little offense to be taken at these curses of David against such; and the more likely it is, (besides what we are bound to believe of it, upon another consideration) that the glory of God was his aim, in cursing those, that he knew to God, of all men, the most accursed: and in the punishment of whom, God he knew would be most glorified. But that he uttered these curses without any passion at all, (taking passion in the better sense, as an effect of nature, from which no natural man is altogether exempt) is more than I think myself bound to believe. Ancient Greek philosophers tell us of a certain πάθος, which they call Ἡρωικὸν πάθος: the proper passion of Heroes, or Heroic men. It is, as they describe it, an illimited or unbounded liberty of language, in point of reproof and reprehension, not tied to certain limits either of time, or of place. And this they make the privilege of Heroic men, because of their transcendent virtue above other men. Certainly, had David as a mere man, (not inspired I mean) used these imprecations, yet we were bound to believe, that a man of such extraordinary parts, and otherwise so meek and so charitable, had good reason for what he did; though we have no warrant at all, either from reason, or Scripture, to imitate him therein, or to take the same liberty. How these imprecations should be read and understood by godly people in their daily devotions, see upon Psal. 143.3. *For the enemy hath persecuted my soul*, &c.

*{that seek after my soul}* Let them that seek my life miss their mark, and so be ashamed.

**V. 5. ***{Let them be as chaff before the wind}* Job 21.18. Psal. 1.4. Isa. 29.5. Hos. 13.3. Smite them with the Spirit of giddiness, that their enterprises may be foolish, and they receive just reward.

**V. 6. ***{dark and slippery}* Heb. *darkness and slipperiness*; that is, very slippery.

**V. 7. ***{without cause}* Showing, that we may not call God to be a revenger, but only for his glory, and when our cause is just, Psal. 7.4. & 96.4.

*{their net}* A metaphor from fowlers and hunters, who use nets and pits to get the prey, Psal. 9.15.

**V. 8. ***{at unawares}* Heb. *which he knoweth not of*. When he promises to himself peace, 1 Thess. 5.3.

*{into that very destruction let him fall}* Which he prepared against the children of God, and me in particular. It is likely this he was Saul.

**V. 10. ***{All my bones shall say}* He attributes his deliverance only to God, praising him therefore both in soul and body.

**V. 11. ***{False witnesses}* Heb. *witnesses of wrong*.

*{False witnesses did rise up}* They did bear false witness against me, and sought to entrap me with questions, and that in things concerning my life, 1 King. 21.13. Mar. 26.59.

*{they laid to my charge}* Heb. *they asked me*.

**V. 12. ***{spoiling}* Heb. *depriving*.

**V. 13. ***{humbled}* Or, *afflicted*.

*{my prayer returned into mine own bosom}* I prayed heartily for them; and though they got no good by it, yet I did, Mat. 10.13. Luke 10.6.

**V. 14. ***{behaved myself}* Heb. *walked*.

*{as though he had been my friend or brother}* Heb. *as a friend, as a brother to me*.

**V. 15. ***{But in mine adversity they rejoiced}* When they saw me ready to slip, and as one that halted for infirmity, Psal. 38.16.

*{adversity}* Heb. *halting*.

*{they did tear me, and ceased not}* With their railing words, Psal. 57.4. & 120.3,4.

**V. 16. ***{With hypocritical mockers in feasts}* In the Hebrew, word for word, *Cum hypocriticis* (or, *impiis*) *subsannatoribus panis subcineritii*. מעוג indeed, is properly a kind of bread, baked under ashes: in the Vulgar Latin, *Panis subcineritius*: and by the LXX. rendered, ἐγκρυφίας; as if you said, *hidden bread*. More generally it is taken for any kind of cake; and thence, for dainties: and because dainties use to be provided in feasts, here rendered, *feasts*: which is somewhat far, as to the word; but to the sense, not amiss. Yet all, or most, agree, that idle jesters and scoffers, (whether of the better, or baser sort of people, for of them too, there be too many that are not ashamed of their base practice:) such as are most conversant in feasts, to make mirth, &c. are intended. Yet there be, that would have מעוג to signify, *idle, or merry talk*, also. But still, to one purpose; whether so, or so; so that we may be the more indifferent. There is an allusion in the words also, which makes them the more pleasantly (whence also we may probably conjecture that they were used proverbially:) to run in the Original, מעוג לעג but in the translation, not observable.

*{gnashed upon me with their teeth}* Psal. 37.12.

**V. 17. ***{how long wilt thou look on}* Hab. 1.13.

*{my darling}* Heb. *my only one*, Psal. 22.20.

**V. 18. ***{I will give thee thanks in the great congregation}* Psal. 40.9,10. & 111.1. & 22.25.

*{much}* Heb. *strong*.

**V. 19. ***{wrongfully}* Heb. *falsely*.

*{neither let them wink with the eye}* In token of contempt and mocking, Prov. 6.13. & 10.10.

**V. 20. ***{deceitful matters}* Heb. *words of deceits*; that is, *most deceitful*.

**V. 21. ***{opened their mouth}* Job 16.10. Esay 57.4.

*{Aha, aha, &c.}* Psal. 40.15. & 70.3.

*{our eye hath seen it}* They rejoiced as though they had their desire in David’s destruction, Psal. 54.7. & 59.10.

**V. 22. ***{keep not silence}* Sit not still, but prepare yourself to right me.

**V. 23. ***{awake to my judgment}* As if God’s connivance had given this liberty to his foes to wrong him.

**V. 24. ***{according to thy righteousness}* It belongs to God’s justice to give to the oppressors affliction and torment, and to the oppressed, aid and relief. See upon Psal. 31.1. *Deliver me in thy righteousness*.

**V. 25. ***{Ah}* Heb. *Ah, ah, our soul*.

*{Ah, so we would have it}* We have our desire, he is destroyed.

*{We have swallowed him up}* As a wild beast devours his prey: we have altogether swallowed him.

**V. 26. ***{Let them be ashamed and brought to confusion together}* Psal. 40.15.

*{brought to confusion together}* That is, at once, were they never so many or mighty.

*{let them be clothed with shame}* Covered all over with it, so that nothing may appear at any time, but their shame, Psal. 109.29. & 132.18. Job 8.22.

**V. 27. ***{Let them shout for joy}* Give them cause to rejoice for my deliverance.

*{my righteous cause}* Heb. *my righteousness*. That at least favor my right, if they be not able to help me.

*{which hath pleasure in the prosperity of his servant}* Of me: and will delight in their prosperity, if they serve him faithfully. And never afflict them, but in kindness, and for their good: *I know O Lord, that thy judgments are right, and that thou in faithfulness hast afflicted me. Let I pray thee, thy merciful kindness*, &c. Psal. 119.75,76. which is the children of God their great privilege, that God will chastise them, if it be requisite, Heb. 12.5,6. it being much better for them (and to be accounted truest prosperity:) to suffer here, be it more, or less; that they may be saved eternally. *For our light affliction which is but for a moment*, &c. 2 Cor. 4.17.

# PSALM XXXVI

## TITLE

*{the servant of the Lord}* In governing the kingdom under God, and according to his Will, Psal. 18. tit.

**Verse 1. ***{The transgression of the wicked saith within my heart}* The order of the words in the Original Hebrew, is somewhat perplexed, and intricate; which makes interpreters to vary much in their translations, though they agree well enough about the main sense. There be that expound it thus; Transgression, (or custom of sinning, or, *inbred corruption*:) says to the wicked; that is, prompts and persuades him, (*within my heart*; that is, in my conscience it is so: I verily believe it;) to put away all fear of God from before his eyes. Certain it is, that a loose licentious life, breeds in a man, though better persuaded before, sooner or later, atheistical opinions; nothing more: as a religious life disposes and prepares a man, as much as anything, to embrace the truth of God. But the more received exposition is, (whether, as here we read it, *The transgression of the wicked, saith*, &c. or, as some, *Transgression saith of the wicked*, &c.) That as the tree may be known by the fruit, so an atheist, by his life and conversation. *Within my heart*, says David; that is, as before, I verily believe it. Or, perchance he opposes the secret thoughts and suggestions of his heart, to open and confident asseveration. For many things we may believe, upon good grounds, and probabilities; which yet to speak openly and publicly, were both indiscreet, and uncharitable. There is no question, but according to the phrase and doctrine of the Scriptures, all men that live irreligiously, may be called and accounted infidels and atheists: but a legal formal atheist is another thing. Many things should be considered and known, before we take upon us, openly and peremptorily, to charge any man of atheism.

*{no fear of God before his eyes}* Rom. 3.18.

**V. 2. ***{For he flattereth himself}* Or, *Yet he flattereth himself*. Or, *For it flattereth him in*.

*{he flattereth himself in his own eyes}* But he himself knows that he does but dissemble with God, as flatterers do with men, and soothes up himself therein, Heb. 3.13.

*{until his iniquity be found to be hateful}* Heb. *to find his iniquity to hate*.

**V. 3. ***{he hath left off to be wise}* Ceasing from good, he thinks of nothing but doing evil.

*{to be wise, and to do good}* Or, *to be wise to do good*, Jer. 4.22.

**V. 4. ***{He deviseth mischief}* By describing at large the nature of wicked men, he admonishes the godly to beware of those vices.

*{mischief}* Or, *vanity*.

*{upon his bed}* He employs his time of rest and freedom from business, to devise wicked acts to execute next day.

*{bed}* Mic. 2.1,2.

*{he abhorreth not evil}* Omits no evil, but embraces all occasions of sinning.

*{not good}* Or, *very evil*, 1 Sam. 2.24. Prov. 24.23.

**V. 5. ***{Thy mercy, O Lord, is in the heavens}* Having spoken of the wickedness, both in matter of life and opinion, of men; he falls into a consideration of the goodness and mercy of God; who notwithstanding such daily provocations of the profane, perjured, sacrilegious, and the like, suffers them nevertheless to live, and enjoy the blessings of this world; *Making his Sun to rise upon the unjust, as well as upon the just*, &c. as we read in the Gospel. But from God’s goodness and mercy, in general, in the 5,6, & 7 verses, he proceeds to his more particular goodness and mercy to themwards, whom he has enlightened with his word, and called to be members of his Church. See before, upon Psal. 24. *The earth is the Lord’s*, &c.

**V. 6. ***{Thy righteousness is like the great mountains}* For eminency and stableness.

*{the great mountains}* Heb. *the mountains of God*, Job 1.16.

*{thy judgments are a great deep}* Into which the wicked, that die without repentance, sink irrecoverably; and in the consideration of the causes whereof the wisdom of the wisest of men is oftentimes posed and confounded. See more upon Psal. 77.19. *thy footsteps are not known*.

*{thou preservest man and beast}* Gen. 8.1.

**V. 7. ***{excellent}* *precious*.

*{under the shadow of thy wings}* Flee to you for succor, as chickens under the hen’s wings in danger, Psal. 17.8.

**V. 8. ***{They shall be abundantly satisfied with the fatness of thy house}* Only God’s children have enough of all things, both concerning this life, and the life to come, Psal. 17.15. 1 Tim. 4.8. Phil. 11,12,18.

*{abundantly satisfied}* Heb. *watered*.

*{satisfied with the fatness of thy house}* Psal. 65.4.

*{of the river of thy pleasures}* Prepared for your family.

**V. 10. ***{continue}* Heb. *draw out at length*.

*{unto them that know thee}* He shows who are God’s children; to wit, they that know him, and lead their lives uprightly.

**V. 11. ***{Let not the foot of pride come against me}* Let not the proud advance himself against me, neither the power of the wicked drive me away.

**V. 12. ***{There are the workers of iniquity fallen}* Wicked men have fallen while they sought to destroy good men, and so, I hope, shall these.

*{There are the workers of iniquity fallen: they are cast down, and shall not be able to rise}* Or, *There have the workers of iniquity fallen: they have been cast down, and have not been able to rise*.
